From first principles to the partner's chair

Workbench

Five calculators every London consultant should run in their head

Enter each insurer's written line. Lines marked "to stand" keep their full share; the rest are signed down proportionally to fill the order. See Module 04.

Build a combined ratio from its parts and see the underlying ratio once catastrophe luck and reserve releases are removed. See Module 01.

Lloyd's member capital is ECA = uSCR × 135%. Test whether buying reinsurance pays for itself through released capital. See Module 07.

Catastrophe excess of loss: what is recovered, what reinstatement premium is due, and what the syndicate keeps. See Module 06.

Separate the headline premium change from the like-for-like price change, and compare the price with the technical price. See Module 04.

Module 00

London Market & Lloyd's – Learning Guide (Beginner → Intermediate → Advanced)

Purpose: Build enough depth in the London insurance market and Lloyd's to (1) hold your ground in client conversations, (2) diagnose business problems, and (3) design and deliver functional and technical consulting work.

Status of information: Researched and written in November 2026. Market facts that change (results, programme status, regulation) are dated and tagged. Check the Sources page before quoting a number to a client.

1. How to read the confidence tags

Tag Meaning
certain Backed by a primary source (Lloyd's, PRA, FCA, legislation) or long-standing, stable market practice.
likely Strong inference, secondary source (trade press, law firm notes), or a number that may have moved.
guessing My professional judgement where no hard evidence exists. Treat as a hypothesis to test.

Definitions and process descriptions that are standard market practice are certain unless tagged otherwise. Tags are applied wherever a claim is numeric, time-sensitive, or a judgement.

2. The strategy behind this guide

2.1 What a "champion" in this domain actually needs

Most people who struggle in London market consulting do not lack definitions. They fail on three things:

  1. They do not understand money flow and risk flow together. In London, the risk is shared across many insurers (subscription), the premium passes through a broker, and the capital sits in a layered structure. If you cannot trace one pound of premium and one pound of claim from end to end, you will be found out in the first workshop.
  2. They describe the market as it was in a vendor slide, not as it works today. Example: in March 2026 Lloyd's retired the name and original vision of Blueprint Two certain. A consultant still pitching "Blueprint Two readiness" in late 2026 loses credibility immediately.
  3. They cannot link an operational problem to a financial outcome. Clients buy outcomes: lower expense ratio, faster premium collection, better loss ratio, less capital. Your recommendations must land on one of those.

So the guide is built around three threads that run through every level:

Thread Question it answers
Risk & contract Who carries the risk, on what terms, and how is that agreed?
Money & capital How does premium, claim, commission, tax and capital move, and who holds it?
Data & control What data moves between whom, what can go wrong, and who is accountable?

2.2 The learning path

flowchart LR
  B[Beginner<br/>Weeks 1-3<br/>Market structure, actors,<br/>classes, core terms]
  I[Intermediate<br/>Weeks 4-8<br/>Placement, delegated authority,<br/>accounting, settlement, claims,<br/>reinsurance]
  A[Advanced<br/>Weeks 9-12<br/>Capital, regulation, finance,<br/>modernisation, data, AI]
  C[Champion<br/>Months 4-24<br/>Spike, proof artefacts,<br/>real exposure, POVs,<br/>client outcomes]
  B --> I --> A --> C

The knowledge levels (Beginner → Advanced) take about 12 weeks. Becoming a world-class consultant takes about 24 months of deliberate practice and real exposure – see Module 18.

How the pieces fit: Module 18 is the spine – a 24-month path built on practice, proof and exposure, not exams. Modules 01–13 are the knowledge it draws on. Module 19 holds the weekly drills and templates. Modules 14–17 follow the CII syllabus structure and are kept as a precise reference for law, rules and calculations – useful, but not the goal.

All core chapters (01–11) are written in full detail using one standard format: learning objectives → explanation → worked examples → how it works in practice → consulting angle → Partner's lens (how a senior top-tier strategy consultant would use the material) → takeaways → check questions → practice tasks → sources.

2.5 How to study (do not skip this)

Reading alone will not get you there. For each module:

  1. Read the module once.
  2. Draw the flow from memory on one page (the diagrams are your answer key).
  3. Explain it out loud in 3 minutes as if to a CFO who has no time.
  4. Answer the module questions in module 13 without looking.
  5. Apply it: pick one scenario in module 10 and write a one-page recommendation.

3. The London market in late 2026 – one-page snapshot

Topic Position (Nov 2026) Tag
Size of London market About $187bn gross written premium in 2024 (London Matters 2025, London Market Group); about 8.7% of global re/insurance premium likely – press coverage of the report
Lloyd's 2025 results GWP £57.9bn, combined ratio 87.6%, profit before tax £10.6bn, return on capital 22.0%, central solvency ratio 496% certain – Lloyd's FY2025 release, 19 Mar 2026
Lloyd's H1 2026 results GWP £34.7bn, combined ratio 90.8%, profit before tax £3.5bn, risk-adjusted rates down 6.7% certain – Lloyd's HY2026 release
Market cycle Softening. PRA's 2026 priorities letter warns that softening in parts of the London market may lead internal models to overstate profit and understate capital likely – law firm summaries of PRA letter
Lloyd's leadership CEO Patrick Tiernan; Chair Sir Charles Roxburgh likely
Lloyd's strategy 2026–2030 strategy launched 19 Mar 2026. Four drivers: leading underwriting performance; efficient and flexible marketplace; maximised capital advantage; a Lloyd's to be proud of. 2031 ambition: most capital-efficient marketplace for complex risk; return on capital above 12% across the cycle certain for drivers; likely for detailed targets
Blueprint Two Name and original vision retired in March 2026. Shift to incremental modernisation, common data standards (the Core Data Record survives), and refocusing Velonetic. Re-platforming not expected before 2028 likely – trade press and law firm notes
Conduct regulation FCA PS25/21 (9 Dec 2025) removed larger commercial customers from ICOBS, PROD and Consumer Duty, and allows co-manufacturers to nominate a lead insurer for PROD 4 duties certain
AI LMA AI Adoption Toolkit launched April 2026. LMA survey: 93% of responding firms have an AI governance framework in place or in development likely
Delegated authority About 45% of Lloyd's premium comes through delegated authority likely

4. Ten sentences you must be able to say with confidence

  1. "Lloyd's is not an insurance company. It is a regulated marketplace where members provide capital to syndicates, and managing agents run those syndicates."
  2. "The London market is Lloyd's plus the company market plus the brokers who place business into both."
  3. "Most London risks are placed by subscription: a lead underwriter sets terms and price, and followers take shares until the risk is 100% covered."
  4. "The Market Reform Contract (MRC) is the standard contract format. Contract certainty means the terms are agreed and documented before inception."
  5. "Around half of Lloyd's premium comes through delegated authority, so data quality and oversight of coverholders is a core risk, not an admin problem."
  6. "At Lloyd's, each year of account is kept open for three years and then closed by reinsurance to close (RITC)."
  7. "Lloyd's capital sits in a chain of security: syndicate assets, members' Funds at Lloyd's, then central assets including the Central Fund."
  8. "Capital at Lloyd's is set on the syndicate's ultimate SCR plus a 35% uplift, called the Economic Capital Assessment."
  9. "Blueprint Two was retired in March 2026; the market now focuses on common data standards like the Core Data Record and incremental change."
  10. "AI value in London is highest today in submission intake, wording review, bordereaux processing and claims triage, and the constraint is data quality and governance, not models."

If you cannot explain each sentence for two minutes, go back to the relevant module.

Module 18

The Path to World-Class London Market Consultant

Purpose: A 24-month development path to become a consultant whom London market and Lloyd's executives seek out, trust with their hardest problems, and pay a premium for. Exams are not the goal. Proof of judgement and results is.

Read this first: most of this path is doing, not reading. Modules 01–13 give you the knowledge. This module tells you how to turn it into capability, evidence and reputation. Modules 14–17 (CII syllabus notes) are now an optional reference library for law and market rules – use them when you need precise rules, not as a study track.

Your starting point and the standard you are aiming at

You are already an insurance consultant, so you do not start from zero. Adjust the path as follows:

Area Adjustment
Phase 1 (Foundation) Compress from 3 months to about 6 weeks; use it to fill London- and Lloyd's-specific gaps rather than general insurance basics
Pillar E (Consulting craft) Your benchmark is the standard of top-tier strategy firms (McKinsey, Bain, BCG): answer-first communication, hypothesis-driven problem solving, value sizing in every recommendation. Each detailed chapter now ends with a "Partner's lens" section showing how a senior partner would use that chapter's knowledge with a client
Exposure Use your current client work: volunteer for London market and Lloyd's engagements, and turn every engagement into one artefact and one case study

Note: the Partner's lens sections describe widely documented top-tier consulting methods. They are not insider material from any firm, and they do not replace feedback from senior people who have worked at those firms – seek that feedback directly when you can.

1. What "world class" actually means here

A world-class London market consultant passes these six tests. Use them as your end goal and test yourself against them every quarter.

# Test How you will know
1 Domain fluency You can hold a 60-minute conversation with a Chief Underwriting Officer, CFO or Head of Delegated Authority without notes, using their language correctly (signed lines, year of account, RITC, bordereaux, SBF, ECA).
2 Financial translation Within 30 minutes of hearing a client problem, you can say which line of the P&L or capital it hits and roughly how much.
3 End-to-end view You can trace risk, money and data from client to broker to lead to follower to bureau to claim to reinsurance recovery – and point to where cost and leakage build up.
4 Bridge between business and technology Business leaders trust your judgement on underwriting and operations; technology leaders trust your judgement on data, architecture and AI. Few people have both.
5 Point of view You hold clear, published positions on the market's big questions (after Blueprint Two, soft-market discipline, AI in delegated authority) that clients quote back to you.
6 Track record You can name outcomes you delivered, with numbers: cost removed, days of premium debt reduced, bordereaux processing time cut, use cases in production.

The honest gap likely: knowledge gets you to tests 1–3 in about 6–9 months of focused effort. Tests 4–6 need real client exposure and take 18–36 months. No amount of reading replaces sitting with an operations team for a week and watching a bordereau being processed.

2. The capability model – six pillars, four levels

Pillar Level 1 – Foundation Level 2 – Practitioner Level 3 – Expert Level 4 – Authority
A. Market and domain Explains structure, actors, lifecycle correctly Explains process detail and exceptions (signing down, claims lead arrangements, DA controls) Diagnoses why a process fails at a specific client and what market rules constrain the fix Shapes market thinking; consulted by market bodies
B. Financial and capital acumen Reads combined ratio and its components Builds a class-level P&L view from syndicate accounts; understands SBF and ECA Links operational change to loss ratio, expense ratio and capital with credible numbers CFO-level adviser on cycle management and capital efficiency
C. Functional and operating model Maps an as-is process Designs a target process and operating model for one function Designs an end-to-end operating model across underwriting, DA, claims, finance Known for operating models that delivered results
D. Data, technology and AI Explains CDR, MRC data, systems landscape, basic AI concepts Builds a data model; runs an AI prototype with measured accuracy Designs data platform and AI programme with governance; leads vendor selection Sets direction for a carrier's data and AI strategy
E. Consulting craft Structures problems; writes clear slides Runs workshops; produces client-ready deliverables; manages a workstream Leads engagements; handles difficult stakeholders; storylines for executives Sells and leads large programmes; trusted adviser to CEOs
F. Market presence Follows market news Has 20+ market contacts; posts informed comments Publishes POVs; speaks at market events Recognised name in the market

Target by month:

Month A B C D E F
3 2 1 1 1 your current level 1
9 2 2 2 2 2 2
18 3 2–3 3 3 3 2–3
24+ 3 3 3 3 3 3

Level 4 is a career outcome, not a 24-month target. Anyone promising you Level 4 in two years is selling something.

3. Choose your spike – do not try to be expert in everything

Clients do not hire generalists for hard problems. They hire the person known for one thing, who also understands everything around it. Pick one primary spike and one secondary spike by month 6.

Spike Demand outlook 2026–2028 Why Fit with you
1. AI-enabled London market operations (submission intake, bordereaux, claims, reconciliation) High likely Lloyd's 2026–2030 strategy targets efficiency and cost; soft market squeezes margins; LMA AI toolkit and PRA attention mean firms need governed AI, not pilots Strong – combines your AI learning goal with functional and technical consulting
2. Delegated authority and MGA oversight and data High likely About 45% of Lloyd's premium; data quality and oversight are chronic problems; DA data standards being consulted on Strong – process-heavy, data-heavy, measurable outcomes
3. Post-Blueprint Two data standards and operating model Medium–High likely Firms need to re-plan after March 2026; CDR alignment, integration, cost-out Good – technical and functional
4. Claims transformation and leakage Medium–High likely Cat volatility, social inflation, DCA oversight Good – needs claims experience to be credible
5. Capital efficiency and cycle management Medium likely Core to Lloyd's strategy, but owned by actuaries and CFOs Weaker unless you have actuarial or finance training
6. Syndicate/MGA launches and M&A integration Cyclical guessing Rises when capital is plentiful Needs strong network first

My recommendation likely: Primary spike = AI-enabled London market operations. Secondary spike = Delegated authority and data. Reasons:

  1. It uses both your functional and technical interests instead of forcing a choice.
  2. Delegated authority is where AI has the clearest measurable value (bordereaux ingestion, validation, oversight analytics).
  3. It aligns with what Lloyd's, the LMA and the PRA are all focused on in 2026, so clients have budget and urgency.
  4. Few consultants combine real London market process knowledge with hands-on AI evaluation skill. That combination is your differentiation.

Risk in this choice: AI consulting is crowded with people who know AI but not insurance. If you neglect domain depth (pillar A and B), you become one of them. Keep 60% of your learning time on domain and finance, 40% on AI and data, until month 12.

4. The 24-month roadmap

flowchart LR
  P1[Phase 1<br/>Months 0-3<br/>FOUNDATION<br/>Language and mental models] --> P2[Phase 2<br/>Months 3-9<br/>PRACTITIONER<br/>Process, data and first proof]
  P2 --> P3[Phase 3<br/>Months 9-18<br/>EXPERT<br/>Spike depth and published POV]
  P3 --> P4[Phase 4<br/>Months 18-24+<br/>AUTHORITY<br/>Client outcomes and market presence]

Phase 1 – Foundation (months 0–3)

Goal: speak the language correctly and hold the core mental models (risk flow, money flow, data flow).

Activity Detail Hours/week
Study Modules 01–07 of this guide, one module per week, with the "draw it from memory" method 4
Market reading Lloyd's Market Bulletins, Lloyd's and LMA news, one trade publication daily (15 min) 2
Financial practice One syndicate teardown every two weeks (method in Module 19) 2
AI practice Module 09 exercises weeks 1–3 (extraction and evaluation on fictional documents) 2
Network Identify 30 people in target roles; start following and engaging thoughtfully 1

Artefacts by end of month 3:

  1. One-page market map drawn from memory.
  2. End-to-end process map: open market placement to claim payment, with risk, money and data flows.
  3. Teardowns of 4 syndicates from public annual accounts.
  4. Glossary self-test: 90% of Module 12 explained correctly out loud.

Exit test: explain Lloyd's to a CFO in 3 minutes; trace the £1m premium and £2m claim in Module 06 Part D without notes; explain why the underlying combined ratio is rising while headline profits are strong.

Phase 2 – Practitioner (months 3–9)

Goal: understand processes at operations level, build data and AI skills, and produce your first proof of value.

Activity Detail Hours/week
Process depth Modules 04–06 and 08 in detail; build detailed process maps for placement, DA (bordereaux), claims, premium accounting 3
Real exposure Get inside real operations (see section 6) – shadow an operations, DA or claims team for at least 5 days in total variable
Data skills Build a CDR-aligned data model for contract, party, exposure, finance and claims; learn SQL basics and data quality measurement 3
AI build Build a bordereaux mapping and validation prototype with an LLM; measure field-level accuracy on 20+ fictional bordereaux 3
Spike choice Decide primary and secondary spike by month 6 —
Consulting craft Write one-page recommendations for 6 scenarios in Module 10; get feedback from a practitioner 2
Network 10 conversations with practitioners (underwriters, DA managers, ops leads, brokers) using the interview guide in Module 19 1–2

Artefacts by end of month 9:

  1. London market data model (entity diagram plus field list mapped to MRC and CDR concepts).
  2. Bordereaux AI prototype with an evaluation report (accuracy, failure patterns, controls needed).
  3. DA oversight framework for a fictional managing agent (risk tiering, KPIs, audit triggers).
  4. Cost-to-income diagnostic template (value chain cost split, benchmarks, levers).
  5. First point-of-view paper (2–3 pages): "After Blueprint Two – five no-regret moves for managing agents."

Exit test: run a 90-minute mock workshop with a practitioner playing a Head of DA; they should say "you understand how this actually works."

Phase 3 – Expert (months 9–18)

Goal: become known for your spike. Deliver or contribute to real work. Publish.

Activity Detail
Client work Contribute to at least one real London market engagement in your spike (as team member, secondee, or internal change role) – see section 6
Spike depth Read everything published on your spike (LMA, Lloyd's, PRA, vendors, law firms); build a library with your notes
Advanced AI Agentic workflow prototype with approval gates (e.g., submission → enrich → appetite check → draft quote note); AI governance pack aligned to the LMA toolkit principles
Business cases Build two full value cases with baseline, assumptions, sensitivity and 90-day checkpoints
Publishing One POV every 6–8 weeks; LinkedIn summaries of each; one talk at a meetup or internal forum
Finance depth Learn how syndicate plans and capital interact (SBF, uSCR, ECA); ask an actuary to review your understanding

Artefacts by end of month 18:

  1. AI governance pack (policy, inventory template, risk tiering, evaluation standard, human-in-the-loop design).
  2. Agentic workflow prototype with evaluation results and a control design.
  3. Three more POVs, for example: "Soft-market playbook for London underwriters"; "Bordereaux are a data product, not a report"; "Silent AI: the exposure London has not priced."
  4. A case study from real work (anonymised) with measured outcomes.

Exit test: a client or senior practitioner asks you for your view on your spike without you offering it.

Phase 4 – Authority (months 18–24 and beyond)

Goal: own client relationships, shape engagements, be recognised in the market.

Activity Detail
Lead Lead a workstream or engagement end to end
Sell Write proposals, shape scope, price work, win follow-on work
Speak Present at a recognised market forum (LMA, IUA, LIIBA events, Insurance Institute of London, Lloyd's Lab events, trade conferences)
Mentor Teach the guide to juniors; teaching exposes your gaps
Refresh Rebuild your POVs every 6 months – the market moves (Blueprint Two went from flagship to retired in six years)

5. Deliberate practice – the weekly operating rhythm

World-class performance comes from regular, structured practice with feedback, not from occasional intense reading.

Day Practice (30–60 min) Output
Mon Market scan: Lloyd's Market Bulletins, regulator updates, trade news 3 "so-what" notes (template in Module 19)
Tue Financial drill: syndicate teardown or results analysis Teardown sheet
Wed Process drill: pick one process step; write what can go wrong, controls, data, KPIs One-page note
Thu AI/data build: extend a prototype; run evaluation; record failure modes Evaluation log
Fri Consulting drill: mock executive conversation with an AI sparring partner or colleague; or write part of a POV Recording or draft
Monthly Practitioner conversation; POV progress; capability self-score Updated scorecard
Quarterly Six-test review (section 1); adjust plan Quarterly review note

Time commitment guessing: 8–10 hours a week alongside a full-time job in months 0–9; then mostly through real work. Less than 5 hours a week will not get you there in 24 months.

6. Getting real exposure – the hardest and most important part

Reading builds knowledge; exposure builds judgement. Use every route available to you.

Route How Value
Join or move into a London market practice Consulting firms with insurance practices serving London (Big 4, technology consultancies, specialist insurance consultancies) Fastest route to engagements
Internal change roles Business analyst / product owner / change lead in a managing agent, broker or carrier transformation programme Deep process exposure; credibility with clients later
Offshore capability centres Many London carriers and brokers run operations, DA, claims and finance centres outside the UK likely – these handle the real processes daily Excellent process and data exposure; strong entry point if you are outside London
Insurtech / vendor roles Implementation consultant at an underwriting workbench, bordereaux or claims technology firm Hands-on data and workflow; many client sites
Secondments Request client-site secondments on engagements Sit with the operations teams
Shadowing Ask contacts for a day with a DA team, a claims team, a technical accounting team Low-cost, high-value
Market bodies and events Insurance Institute of London lectures, LMA/IUA/LIIBA events, Lloyd's Lab demo days, CII events likely Network, current issues

If you cannot get client exposure for 6 months: build artefacts with public data (syndicate accounts, Lloyd's guidance, fictional documents), publish them, and use them to open doors. Artefacts are proof that substitutes for experience in early conversations.

7. Network – who you need to know

Role What you learn from them Target number by month 12
Underwriters (lead and follow) How decisions are really made; what slows them down 5
Heads of DA / DA managers Bordereaux reality, coverholder behaviour, Lloyd's oversight 5
Operations / technical accounting Bureau processing, reconciliation, aged debt 5
Claims managers Claims schemes, DCA oversight, cat surge 3
Brokers (placing, technicians) Broker side of data and placement 5
Actuaries / finance SBF, capital, reserving 3
CIO / data / AI leads Systems, data platform, AI programmes 5
Consultants and vendors in your spike Competitive landscape, what clients buy 5

How to ask: "I'm building expertise in [spike]. I've mapped how [process] works from public sources – could I spend 20 minutes checking it with you?" Bring the artefact. Practitioners respond to evidence of effort, not requests for favours.

8. Commercial understanding – how London market consulting is bought

Trigger Typical buyer What they buy Where to spot it
Lloyd's plan challenge or performance issue CEO, CUO Underwriting performance review, remediation Syndicate results, Lloyd's commentary
Soft market margin pressure CEO, CFO, COO Cost-out, operating model, automation Results announcements, rate change data
Regulatory change or PRA/FCA findings CRO, CCO Gap analysis, remediation, governance PRA/FCA letters, policy statements
Market change (e.g., Blueprint Two decision) COO, CIO Roadmap re-plan, data standards Lloyd's/LMG announcements
M&A CEO, CFO Integration, systems consolidation Deal announcements
New syndicate / MGA Founders, investors Business plan, set-up Lloyd's approvals news
Catastrophe or major loss CCO (claims), CFO Surge support, claims review Event news
AI ambition with weak governance CEO, CDO, CRO AI strategy, governance, pilots to scale Firm statements, LMA survey context

Proposal structure that wins: (1) the client's problem in their numbers; (2) your hypothesis of root causes; (3) approach in phases with decision points; (4) outcomes and how they will be measured; (5) team and relevant proof (your artefacts and case studies); (6) commercials.

9. Measuring your progress

Quarterly scorecard

Measure Q1 target Q2 Q3 Q4 Q6 Q8
Capability self-score (avg of 6 pillars, validated by a practitioner) 1.3 1.7 2.0 2.2 2.6 3.0
Artefacts completed (cumulative) 4 7 9 10 12 13+
POVs published (cumulative) 0 1 2 3 4 6
Practitioner conversations (cumulative) 5 15 25 35 50 70
Real engagement days in London market work 0 5 20 50 120 200+
Outcomes with numbers you can cite 0 0 0 1 2 3+

Self-validation rule

Do not score yourself without evidence. Every level claim needs either an artefact or a practitioner who will confirm it.

10. Mistakes that keep people average

Mistake Why it hurts Instead
Collecting certificates instead of proof Clients buy outcomes, not credentials Build artefacts and case studies
Leading with technology Executives tune out Lead with their P&L and capital problem
Generic AI talk Every competitor says the same Bring measured accuracy, controls and a London-specific use case
Avoiding underwriting and finance You stay an "IT person" in the client's eyes Learn to read syndicate accounts and talk rate adequacy
Repeating outdated market facts Instant loss of credibility (e.g., pitching Blueprint Two readiness in 2026) Weekly market scan; date every fact
Never quantifying Recommendations sound like opinions Put a number and a range on every claim
Waiting for permission to publish Reputation is built in public Publish small, publish often, correct openly

11. Your first 30 days – concrete actions

Week Actions
1 Read Modules 00–02. Draw the market map from memory. Set up a notes system with folders per pillar. List 30 target contacts.
2 Read Modules 03–04. Do your first syndicate teardown (Module 19 method). Start the daily market scan.
3 Read Modules 05–06. Do the money trace exercise. Build your first fictional MRC and run the AI extraction exercise (Module 09, week 2).
4 Read Module 07. Second syndicate teardown. Write your first "so-what" summary of Lloyd's H1 2026 results. Book your first two practitioner conversations. Score yourself on the capability model.

Then continue with Phase 1 for months 2–3 and review against the Phase 1 exit test.

Module 01

Foundations: Insurance Economics and How London Is Different
Level Beginner
Path phase Phase 1 – Foundation, week 1 (Module 18)
Time 3–4 hours reading, 2 hours exercises
Prerequisites None
You will be able to Explain why insurance exists and how insurers make money; read a combined ratio; describe the basic contract; explain why the London market exists and what makes it different; walk a risk end to end through the market

How to use this chapter

Each section follows the same pattern: the idea in plain English, a worked example, how it works in practice, and the consulting angle – what this means when you sit with a client. Read once fully. Then close the file and explain each section out loud in two minutes. If you cannot, read that section again.

1. Why insurance exists – the economics

1.1 The idea

Businesses face uncertain losses: a fire, a ship sinking, a lawsuit, a cyber attack. Uncertainty is expensive. A company that might lose £50m next year must either hold £50m of spare cash (which earns little and cannot be invested in the business) or accept that one bad event could ruin it.

Insurance solves this by turning an uncertain large loss into a certain small cost – the premium. The client pays, say, £500,000 a year and in return the insurer promises to pay the loss if it happens.

The insurer can do this because of pooling. It collects premiums from many clients with similar risks. In any year only a few will have losses. The premiums of the many pay the losses of the few.

1.2 Worked example – why pooling works

Suppose 1,000 warehouses each have a 1% chance per year of a £2m fire.

One warehouse alone Pool of 1,000 warehouses
Expected loss per year 1% × £2m = £20,000 10 fires × £2m = £20m in total = £20,000 each
Range of outcomes £0 (99% of years) or £2m (1% of years) Usually 6–14 fires a year → £12m–£28m total
Predictability Very poor Good

The individual owner cannot predict whether they will lose £0 or £2m. The pool can predict, within a reasonable range, that it will pay about £20m. This is the law of large numbers: the more similar, independent exposures you pool, the closer actual losses come to expected losses.

The insurer then charges each warehouse the expected loss (£20,000) plus amounts for expenses, the cost of holding capital for bad years, and profit.

1.3 Where pooling breaks down – and why London exists

Pooling works best when risks are many, similar and independent. Many of the risks London insures are none of these:

Feature Example Problem for pooling
Few exposures There are only a few hundred offshore oil platforms or satellites Not enough similar risks to make losses predictable
Very large values A refinery worth $3bn; a large airline fleet One loss can exceed one insurer's appetite or capital
Correlated losses A hurricane damages thousands of buildings at once; a cyber attack hits many companies Losses arrive together, so the pool does not average out
Unusual or new risks Political risk in an unstable country; AI liability; space launches Little or no loss history to price from

When pooling alone does not work, insurance needs two other things:

  1. Large amounts of capital to absorb bad years.
  2. Ways to share one large risk among many insurers, so that no single insurer is exposed to too much.

The London market – and Lloyd's especially – is built to provide both: deep capital and subscription (one risk shared by many insurers). This is the single most important idea in the chapter.

1.4 Consulting angle

When a client says "this class is volatile", translate it: they have few, large or correlated exposures, so results depend more on capital, reinsurance and underwriting judgement than on statistics. Projects in such classes focus on exposure management, reinsurance and underwriting discipline, not on volume automation.

2. How an insurer makes money

2.1 The idea

An insurer earns money in two ways:

  1. Underwriting result – premiums earned minus claims and expenses.
  2. Investment return – income from investing premiums and capital until claims are paid. The money held between receiving premium and paying claims is called the float.
Underwriting result = Earned premium − Claims incurred − Expenses
Total result       = Underwriting result + Investment return − Tax

2.2 Written vs earned premium

  • Written premium: the premium for contracts that started in the period.
  • Earned premium: the part of written premium that relates to the time that has passed.

If a £120,000 annual policy starts on 1 October, by 31 December the insurer has written £120,000 but earned only £30,000 (3 months of 12). The other £90,000 is unearned premium – held as a liability because the insurer still owes 9 months of cover.

This matters because results are measured on earned premium. A fast-growing insurer can look like it has lots of premium (written) while its earned premium, and therefore its profit, lags behind.

2.3 The combined ratio – your most-used number

Combined ratio = (Claims incurred + Expenses) ÷ Earned premium

  • Below 100% = underwriting profit.
  • Above 100% = underwriting loss (investment income may still produce an overall profit).

Worked example – Syndicate X, one year

Item £m Ratio to earned premium
Earned premium (net of reinsurance) 1,000
Attritional claims (normal, smaller losses) 480 48.0%
Large individual losses 70 7.0%
Catastrophe / major losses 60 6.0%
Prior-year reserve releases (older years developing better than expected) (30) (3.0%)
Claims ratio 580 58.0%
Acquisition costs (brokerage, commission) 250 25.0%
Operating / administrative expenses 90 9.0%
Expense ratio 340 34.0%
Combined ratio 920 92.0%
Underwriting profit 80
Investment return 45
Profit before tax 125

How to read it like an expert:

Question Answer from the example
Is the core business profitable? Look at the underlying ratio: excluding cat losses and reserve releases → 48 + 7 + 25 + 9 = 89%. Yes, but with limited margin.
How much did luck help? Reserve releases gave 3 points. A heavier cat year (say 12 points instead of 6) would push the combined ratio to 98%.
Where can a consultant help? Expense ratio (34%) – acquisition costs of 25% are high and typical of delegated business; operating expenses of 9% can often be reduced through automation and simplification.

2.4 Applying it to Lloyd's today

Metric 2025 H1 2026
Gross written premium £57.9bn £34.7bn
Combined ratio 87.6% 90.8%
Underlying combined ratio (excluding major claims) about 81.8% about 84%
Risk-adjusted rate change — −6.7%

Source: Lloyd's results releases certain for headline figures; underlying split likely.

What an advisor sees likely: headline results look strong because 2025 and early 2026 had moderate catastrophe losses and reserve releases. But the underlying ratio is rising period after period because prices are falling. That is the classic early stage of a soft market. Clients who keep growing at falling prices are building future losses.

2.5 The insurance cycle

flowchart LR
  A[Prices high,<br/>profits strong] --> B[New capital and<br/>competitors enter]
  B --> C[Prices fall, terms widen<br/>SOFT MARKET]
  C --> D[Losses rise, reserves<br/>deteriorate, capital lost]
  D --> E[Capacity withdraws,<br/>prices rise<br/>HARD MARKET]
  E --> A

Drivers: supply of capital, major catastrophes, investment returns, reserve surprises (especially US liability), and competition. In 2026 new capital is still entering Lloyd's – approved capital for 2026 rose to about £36.2bn and 13 new syndicates started on 1 January 2026 likely – which adds to price pressure.

2.6 Consulting angle

Every recommendation you make must land on one of four numbers: loss ratio, expense ratio, capital, or growth. In a soft market, clients care most about the expense ratio (they control it) and loss selection (they partly control it). Learn to say: "This change takes about 1.5 points off your expense ratio," not "This improves efficiency."

3. The insurance contract – the basics you must know

3.1 Parties and roles

Term Meaning
Insured / Assured The client protected. "Assured" is used in marine.
Insurer / Underwriter The party accepting the risk. "Underwriter" is also the person who decides.
Reinsured / Cedant An insurer buying reinsurance.
Reinsurer Insurer of insurers.
Broker Intermediary arranging the contract, normally acting for the client.
Coverholder / MGA Firm authorised to bind contracts on behalf of insurers.

3.2 Key financial terms in a contract

Term Meaning Example
Sum insured / Limit Maximum the insurer pays $100m limit
Deductible / Excess / Retention Part of each loss the insured keeps First $1m of each loss
Sub-limit Lower limit for a specific cover inside the main limit Flood limited to $10m
Aggregate limit Maximum for all losses in the period $200m in the annual aggregate
Premium Price of the cover $1.2m
Rate Premium per unit of exposure 0.15% of property value
Reinstatement Restoring a limit after it is used, often for extra premium (common in reinsurance) One reinstatement at 100%

3.3 Layers and towers

Large limits are often split into layers, each insured by different insurers at different prices. Together they form a tower.

flowchart TB
  L4["Layer 4: $250m xs $250m<br/>(rarely hit, cheapest per $ of limit)"]
  L3["Layer 3: $150m xs $100m"]
  L2["Layer 2: $75m xs $25m"]
  L1["Primary: $24m xs $1m deductible<br/>(hit most often, most expensive per $ of limit)"]
  D["Deductible: first $1m kept by insured"]
  L4 --- L3 --- L2 --- L1 --- D

"$75m xs $25m" means the layer pays losses above $25m, up to a further $75m (so it covers losses between $25m and $100m).

Worked example: a $60m loss is paid as follows. The insured pays the first $1m (deductible). The primary layer ($24m xs $1m) pays the loss from $1m up to $25m – that is $24m. Layer 2 pays from $25m to $60m = $35m. Layers 3 and 4 pay nothing. Total: $1m + $24m + $35m = $60m.

Why it matters: each layer often has its own lead underwriter, followers, price and sometimes wording. Gaps or differences between layers cause disputes – a classic contract certainty risk.

3.4 When is a claim covered? Three triggers you must not confuse

Trigger Covered if… Common in
Losses occurring (occurrence) The loss happens during the policy period Property, most general liability
Claims made The claim is first made against the insured (and notified) during the policy period, usually for acts after a retroactive date Professional indemnity, D&O, cyber, medical malpractice
Risks attaching (Reinsurance) The underlying policy started during the reinsurance period Proportional reinsurance treaties

A reinsurance treaty can be losses occurring during (LOD) or risks attaching during (RAD). Mixing them up in a reinsurance programme creates gaps in cover.

3.5 What a policy wording contains

Part Purpose
Schedule / Risk details Who, what, where, when, how much
Insuring (operative) clause What is covered
Definitions Meaning of key words (e.g., "occurrence", "damage")
Exclusions What is not covered (war, sanctions, nuclear, cyber in property, communicable disease)
Conditions Obligations of the insured (notify claims, protect property) and of the insurer
Endorsements Changes to the standard wording

London relies heavily on standard market clauses published by the LMA, IUA and the Institute clauses (marine). Each has a reference number (e.g., LMA5564 for one state-backed cyber exclusion). Knowing these numbers lets you – and AI tools – compare wordings systematically.

3.6 Key legal principles (introduction)

Principle Short meaning
Insurable interest The insured must stand to lose financially.
Fair presentation / good faith Business clients must disclose material information before cover starts (Insurance Act 2015).
Indemnity Put the insured back where they were – no profit from a loss.
Subrogation After paying, the insurer can recover from whoever caused the loss.
Contribution Insurers covering the same loss share it.
Proximate cause The dominant cause of the loss decides whether it is covered.

Detail in Module 15.

3.7 Consulting angle

Most disputes, leakage and "data problems" in London start with the contract: unclear wording, missing exclusions, layers that do not match, or data captured differently from what was agreed. When you diagnose a claims or data problem, always ask: "Show me the contract."

4. Direct insurance, reinsurance and retrocession

4.1 The idea

flowchart LR
  Client[Client] -- premium --> Insurer
  Insurer -- claims --> Client
  Insurer -- reinsurance premium --> Reinsurer
  Reinsurer -- recoveries --> Insurer
  Reinsurer -- retro premium --> Retro[Retrocessionaire]
  Retro -- recoveries --> Reinsurer
  Investors[Capital markets / ILS investors] -- capital --> Reinsurer
  • Direct insurance: insurer covers a client.
  • Reinsurance: an insurer transfers part of its risk to a reinsurer.
  • Retrocession: a reinsurer transfers part of its risk to another reinsurer.
  • Insurance-linked securities (ILS): investors (pension funds, specialist funds) provide capital for catastrophe risk through cat bonds, collateralised reinsurance and sidecars.

4.2 Why insurers buy reinsurance

Reason Example
Capacity Write a $100m line while keeping only $20m net.
Catastrophe protection Limit the net loss from one hurricane.
Stability Smooth results from year to year.
Capital relief Reinsurance reduces required capital.
Expertise Reinsurer supports entry into a new class.
Exit Transfer old liabilities (run-off, RITC, loss portfolio transfer).

4.3 In practice

London is one of the world's main reinsurance centres. Lloyd's reinsurance segment (property, casualty and specialty treaty) is one of its largest certain. Main renewal dates are 1 January (Europe and global), 1 April (Japan), 1 June (Florida) and 1 July (US, Australia) certain. In 2026, reinsurance prices are falling as capital is plentiful likely, which makes reinsurance cheaper for buyers and less profitable for sellers.

4.4 Consulting angle

Reinsurance programmes are where underwriting, capital and finance meet. Typical client problems: claims not linked to the correct treaty (missed recoveries), programmes bought without clear capital analysis, and reinsurance costs not allocated to the classes that use them.

5. Admitted, non-admitted and licensing

5.1 The idea

Every country regulates insurance on its residents and risks. An insurer can usually write a risk in a country only if:

  1. it is licensed (admitted) there, or
  2. local law allows the risk to be placed with an unlicensed (non-admitted) insurer – for example, because local insurers will not take it, or because it is reinsurance or a large commercial risk.

5.2 The US surplus lines market

  • In the US, non-admitted insurance is called surplus lines or excess and surplus (E&S).
  • A licensed US surplus lines broker places the risk and pays state surplus lines tax.
  • E&S covers risks that standard (admitted) US insurers decline: coastal property, hazardous liability, unusual risks.
  • Lloyd's is one of the largest surplus lines markets in the US, and the US and Canada provide roughly half of Lloyd's premium likely.
  • US policyholders are protected by Lloyd's US dollar trust funds certain.

5.3 Europe after Brexit

UK insurers lost the right to "passport" into the EU in 2021. Lloyd's created Lloyd's Insurance Company S.A. (Lloyd's Europe) in Brussels to write EEA risks; the policies are reinsured back to the syndicates certain.

5.4 Fronting

Where a licence is needed and the London insurer does not have one, a local licensed insurer issues the policy and reinsures the risk to London. This is called fronting. It adds cost (fronting fee) and credit risk for the fronting insurer.

5.5 Consulting angle

Licensing and tax are checked on every risk. Mistakes create regulatory breaches, wrong tax payments and, in extreme cases, invalid cover. For delegated authority business with thousands of risks, licensing and tax checks must be automated and driven by clean location data.

6. Where London sits in the global market

6.1 Four market segments

Segment Typical clients Typical channels London's role
Personal lines Individuals (motor, home) Direct, aggregators, retail brokers Small – mainly through delegated authority (e.g., US homeowners in E&S)
SME commercial Small businesses Retail brokers, MGAs Through coverholders and MGAs
Large commercial and specialty Corporations, complex assets Global and wholesale brokers Core
Reinsurance Insurers Reinsurance brokers Core

6.2 Global specialty hubs

Hub Strength
London Breadth of specialty classes, subscription market, Lloyd's licences and rating, broker concentration
Bermuda Property catastrophe reinsurance, ILS, large casualty excess
US E&S hubs Domestic specialty and surplus lines carriers competing with London
Zurich / Munich / Paris Global reinsurers' headquarters
Singapore / Dubai Regional hubs for Asia and Middle East; Lloyd's has platforms in both certain

Why London competes: breadth and depth in one place. Why it loses business: higher cost of doing business, slower processes, and growth of US domestic E&S carriers likely.

6.3 Size of London in 2026

Measure Value Tag
London market total GWP (2024) about $187bn; about 8.7% of global re/insurance premium likely – LMG London Matters 2025
Lloyd's GWP (2025) £57.9bn certain
Company market premium (2024) £49.3bn including £5.5bn written elsewhere but controlled from London likely – IUA
Lloyd's stamp capacity (2026) about £58bn likely – Insurance Insider survey
Lloyd's approved capital (2026) about £36.2bn likely

7. What makes the London market different – seven features in depth

7.1 Subscription

How it works: one risk is shared among several insurers. A lead underwriter sets the terms and price; followers accept the same terms for their own share. Each insurer is liable only for its own share (several liability).

Why it exists: the risks are too large or too volatile for one insurer.

What it costs: every change (endorsement, claim) may need agreement from several insurers; data is duplicated across all of them; processing is complex.

Consulting implication: standards that let one agreed change flow to all insurers (the General Underwriters' Agreement, claims lead arrangements, common data records) are the main levers for reducing cost.

7.2 A broker-centred market

How it works: almost all business arrives through brokers. In the open market the broker is the agent of the client. The broker prepares the submission, negotiates, documents the contract, collects premium and presents claims.

Why it exists: clients need expert help to access many specialist insurers; insurers rely on brokers for distribution.

What it costs: brokerage typically 10–25% of premium likely; data is created first in broker systems, in broker formats.

Consulting implication: a carrier cannot fix data quality alone. Data standards must work for brokers as well, or the carrier will keep re-keying.

7.3 Specialty and complex risk

How it works: London writes marine, energy, aviation, political risk, terrorism, cyber, large property, US E&S, specialty liability and reinsurance.

Why it exists: 330+ years of expertise, concentration of specialist underwriters, and capital willing to take volatile risks.

What it costs: pricing relies on judgement; models are imperfect; results are volatile.

Consulting implication: automation must support underwriters' judgement, not replace it. Lloyd's 2026 strategy explicitly says AI should strengthen human decisions likely.

7.4 Global reach

How it works: Lloyd's holds licences or eligibility in 200+ countries and territories certain; business comes from every continent.

What it costs: licensing, tax, regulatory reporting and sanctions screening across many jurisdictions.

Consulting implication: compliance and tax rules must be data-driven and automated; manual checking does not scale.

7.5 Delegated authority

How it works: insurers authorise coverholders and MGAs around the world to bind risks locally. About 45% of Lloyd's premium comes this way likely.

What it costs: loss of direct control, late and poor-quality data (bordereaux), conduct risk.

Consulting implication: DA oversight and bordereaux processing are among the largest and most persistent operational problems in the market – and one of the best areas for data and AI work. See Module 05.

7.6 A unique capital structure

How it works: Lloyd's syndicates are backed by members' capital, with a mutual Central Fund behind all of them (the chain of security). Capital comes from insurance groups, investors and private individuals (Names).

Why it matters: every Lloyd's policy carries the same financial strength rating, and capital can enter or leave each year relatively easily.

Consulting implication: at Lloyd's, business plans and capital are approved annually by the Corporation. Many strategic decisions (growth, new classes, new syndicates) go through that process. See Module 07.

7.7 Shared market infrastructure

How it works: premium and claims processing, accounting and settlement for Lloyd's and much of the company market go through a central bureau (Velonetic). Placement increasingly runs on electronic platforms (PPL, Whitespace).

What it costs: change is slow because many firms depend on the same systems. Lloyd's retired Blueprint Two – its flagship modernisation plan – in March 2026 after repeated delays likely.

Consulting implication: design client solutions that deliver value inside the firm while staying compatible with market standards. See Module 08.

8. London vs a domestic retail insurer

Dimension Domestic retail insurer (e.g., UK motor) London specialty market
Number of policies Millions Thousands to tens of thousands per carrier
Average premium Hundreds of pounds Tens of thousands to millions
Pricing Statistical, automated, real-time Judgement plus models, negotiated
Distribution Direct, price comparison sites, brokers Wholesale brokers, coverholders
Insurers per policy One Often several (subscription)
Claims High volume, standard Low volume, high value, complex, often legal
Data Structured, captured digitally Often unstructured: PDFs, spreadsheets, emails
Main regulation focus Consumer protection (Consumer Duty) Prudential strength, conduct in the distribution chain, sanctions; larger commercial clients now outside Consumer Duty (FCA PS25/21) certain
Technology priorities Digital customer journeys, pricing engines Data ingestion, workflow, standards, exposure management

Consulting implication: do not bring retail insurance solutions into London without adapting them. A real-time pricing engine is valuable in UK motor; in London marine hull the bottleneck is reading a 60-page submission and agreeing terms among six insurers.

9. A short history – and what each event explains today

Period What happened What it explains today
1688 Edward Lloyd's coffee house becomes the meeting place for ship owners, merchants and individual underwriters. Marine heritage; face-to-face culture; the idea of individuals "writing under" a risk – the origin of "underwriter".
1771 Underwriters form the Society of Lloyd's. Lloyd's as a society of members, not a company.
1871 Lloyd's Act gives the Society legal status. Lloyd's is governed by Acts of Parliament.
1906 Marine Insurance Act. Still the basis of marine insurance law in many countries.
1906 San Francisco earthquake – Lloyd's underwriters pay claims in full. London's reputation for paying claims, used in marketing to this day.
1982 Lloyd's Act 1982 creates the Council and forces brokers to separate from managing agents. Today's governance and conflict-of-interest rules.
1980s–1990s Asbestos, pollution and catastrophe losses; many Names (individual members with unlimited liability) lose heavily. Why most capital is now corporate and limited liability; why Lloyd's is strict about business planning.
1996 Reconstruction and Renewal; Equitas created to reinsure 1992-and-prior liabilities. Origin of modern legacy and run-off solutions.
2001 9/11 – the largest loss in Lloyd's history at that time. Terrorism market development; focus on aggregation and realistic disaster scenarios.
2005 Hurricanes Katrina, Rita and Wilma. Catastrophe modelling and exposure management become central.
2007 Market Reform Contract and contract certainty become standard. The MRC used on almost every placement.
2016 Placing Platform Limited (PPL) launched. Electronic placement at Lloyd's.
2018 Lloyd's "Decile 10" review targets worst-performing business. Performance management culture; tougher plan approvals.
2019 Lloyd's Europe set up in Brussels after Brexit. EEA business written through Lloyd's Europe.
2020 COVID-19 losses (event cancellation, business interruption disputes); Blueprint Two launched. Wording precision; modernisation ambitions.
2022–2023 Russia's invasion of Ukraine; aircraft stuck in Russia; Lloyd's requires state-backed cyber attack exclusions from March 2023 certain. Sanctions burden; war and cyber wording discipline.
2024 Baltimore bridge collapse; global IT outage (CrowdStrike). Large marine liability losses; non-malicious cyber aggregation.
2025 High Court finds for aircraft lessors under war-risk covers (appeal permitted in 2026) likely; new Lloyd's Chair (Sir Charles Roxburgh, May 2025) and CEO (Patrick Tiernan, June 2025) likely; FCA PS25/21 simplifies commercial conduct rules (Dec 2025) certain. Litigation risk on wording; new leadership agenda; lighter conduct rules for large clients.
2026 Lloyd's 2026–2030 strategy; Blueprint Two retired; LMA AI Adoption Toolkit; prices softening likely. The environment you are working in now.

10. End-to-end story: insuring a shipping fleet through London

This story connects every concept in the chapter. Follow it slowly.

The client. Pacific Carriers, a shipping company in Singapore, owns 25 bulk carriers worth $900m in total. It needs hull and machinery insurance (damage to the ships).

Step 1 – Local broker. Pacific Carriers' broker in Singapore cannot place $900m of hull risk locally at a good price, so it instructs a London wholesale broker.

Step 2 – Submission. The London broker gathers the fleet list (vessel names, IMO numbers, ages, flags, classification societies), the trading areas, 5 years of claims history and the current policy. It drafts a Market Reform Contract (MRC) and creates a Unique Market Reference (UMR).

Step 3 – Choosing a lead. The broker approaches a Lloyd's syndicate known as a leading marine hull underwriter. The underwriter reviews the fleet, checks sanctions (owners, vessels, trading areas), considers the claims history, and quotes a rate and deductible. It agrees to lead with a 20% line.

Step 4 – Following. The broker shows the led MRC to other syndicates and company market insurers. They write lines totalling 110%. The broker signs down each line proportionally so the total is 100%. Every insurer is liable only for its own signed line.

Step 5 – Contract certainty. All terms are agreed before the policy starts on 1 January. The broker issues documentation to the client.

Step 6 – Premium. Pacific Carriers pays the premium to its Singapore broker, who pays the London broker. The London broker deducts its brokerage and submits the premium to the bureau, which processes it and settles each insurer's share through central settlement.

Step 7 – Reinsurance. Each syndicate has its own reinsurance. The lead syndicate's marine excess of loss treaty will pay above $10m per loss.

Step 8 – The loss. In July, one ship runs aground and suffers $40m of damage. The ship's master informs the owner; the owner's broker notifies the lead underwriter through the market claims system. The leader appoints a surveyor and loss adjuster, agrees the claim with the other agreement parties under the market claims rules, and followers are bound by that agreement.

Step 9 – Payment and recovery. Each insurer pays its share of $40m through central settlement; the broker passes the money to the client. The lead syndicate's share (20%) is $8m. Its excess of loss treaty only pays above $10m per loss, so nothing is recovered on this claim – the syndicate keeps the whole $8m net. (Always check the retention before assuming a recovery.) If the grounding was caused by a faulty pilot service, insurers may pursue subrogation.

Step 10 – Results. At year end, the claim affects each syndicate's loss ratio for the 2026 year of account. The underwriter reviews the fleet's rate at renewal.

Consulting questions this story raises:

  • How many times was the fleet data re-keyed between broker, lead, followers and the bureau?
  • How long did it take to agree the claim among insurers?
  • Did each insurer's systems record the same signed line, premium and claim?
  • Were sanctions checks repeated at claim payment?

These are exactly the kinds of questions that lead to consulting work.

11. Common misconceptions

Misconception Reality
"Lloyd's is an insurance company." Lloyd's is a marketplace and regulator of that market. Syndicates take the risk.
"London is just Lloyd's." The company market is of similar size to Lloyd's in premium terms.
"The broker works for the insurer." In the open market, the broker acts for the client.
"Premium written is the same as revenue." Results are based on earned premium.
"A low combined ratio means a healthy business." Check the underlying ratio, catastrophe luck and reserve releases.
"Reinsurance is just insurance for insurers – a back-office topic." It drives capital, results and strategy.
"Technology from retail insurance will fix London." London's bottlenecks are unstructured data, subscription and judgement-based underwriting.

11A. Partner's lens – how a top-tier strategy consultant would use this chapter

This section is written in the working style of senior partners at McKinsey, Bain and BCG – hypothesis-driven, numbers-first, answer-first. It is a description of widely documented methods, not insider material from any firm.

1. Lead with the answer, then the numbers. A partner never opens a London market discussion with "Lloyd's is a marketplace of syndicates…". They open with the client's economics: "Your combined ratio is 92%, but 6 points of that is luck – lighter cats and reserve releases. Your underlying business is at 98% and moving the wrong way as rates fall 6–7% a year." Everything in this chapter exists so you can say that sentence with confidence.

2. Always size the value at stake. For a syndicate with £1bn earned premium:

Lever Typical move Value per year
1 point of loss ratio (selection, pricing discipline) 1% × £1bn £10m
1 point of operating expense ratio (automation, simplification) 1% × £1bn £10m
1 point of acquisition cost (distribution mix, facility terms) 1% × £1bn £10m
10% reduction in required capital (reinsurance, mix, model) e.g., 10% × £600m capital × 12% cost of capital about £7m

The point a partner makes: one point of combined ratio on £1bn is £10m a year, every year. That is how you justify a £2–3m programme in one sentence. Memorise this conversion.

3. Use the "three questions" filter on every client fact. For any fact in this chapter, ask: (a) So what for this client's P&L or capital? (b) Is it structural or cyclical? (c) What would the CEO do differently on Monday? Example: "London uses subscription" → so what: every change and claim needs multi-party agreement → structural → the CEO should push standards (GUA, claims lead arrangements, data records) and not expect a single-firm system to remove the cost.

4. Know the industry's "physics" before the client's problems. Top-tier partners build an industry model first: what drives value (underwriting margin, cost, capital, float), what the cycle is doing, where profit pools sit (lead vs follow, open market vs delegated, classes). Sections 1, 2 and 7 of this chapter are that model in simple form. Keep it on one page and update it every quarter.

5. Day-one hypothesis. Before the first client meeting at a London carrier in late 2026, a partner would already have a hypothesis such as: "Margins are being held up by benign cat years and reserve releases; the real issue is expense ratio and selection discipline as rates soften; the client has under-invested in data foundations and over-invested in AI pilots." The first two weeks of work exist to prove or disprove it – not to "understand the business".

6. Talk like an owner, not like a vendor. Avoid "efficiency", "digital transformation", "synergies" without numbers. Say "1.5 points of expense ratio", "£12m run-rate", "£40m of capital released", "12 days faster premium collection".

12. Key takeaways

  1. Insurance turns uncertain large losses into certain small costs through pooling.
  2. Pooling fails for few, large, correlated or new risks – these need capital and risk sharing, which is why London exists.
  3. Insurers make money from underwriting and investment; the combined ratio is the key underwriting measure.
  4. Read the underlying ratio, not just the headline – in 2026 the underlying trend is worsening as prices fall.
  5. The contract defines everything: limits, layers, triggers, exclusions.
  6. London's seven distinctive features – subscription, brokers, specialty, global reach, delegated authority, unique capital, shared infrastructure – explain both its strengths and its operational problems.

13. Check your understanding

# Question Answer
1 Why do few, large, correlated risks need a different insurance model from UK motor? Pooling cannot make their losses predictable, so they need large capital and risk sharing among insurers (subscription, reinsurance).
2 A £240,000 annual policy starts 1 April. What is earned at 30 June? 3/12 × £240,000 = £60,000.
3 Claims ratio 62%, acquisition 24%, operating expenses 11%. Combined ratio? 97%.
4 In Q3, cat losses were 9 points and reserve releases 4 points. Underlying combined ratio? 97 − 9 + 4 = 92%.
5 A $40m loss hits a tower with a $2m deductible, $18m xs $2m primary and $30m xs $20m excess. Who pays what? Insured $2m; primary $18m; excess layer $20m.
6 What is the difference between losses occurring and claims made? Occurrence: loss happens in the period. Claims made: claim first made and notified in the period.
7 Why does Lloyd's need Lloyd's Europe? UK firms lost EU passporting after Brexit; Lloyd's Europe is an EU-authorised insurer.
8 Name the seven distinctive features of London. Subscription, broker-centred, specialty, global reach, delegated authority, unique capital structure, shared infrastructure.

14. Practice tasks (link to the path)

  1. Draw the market map and the shipping story from memory on one page.
  2. Explain to a friend in three minutes why London exists (use section 1.3).
  3. Calculate the underlying combined ratio for Lloyd's 2025 using the published major claims ratio and reserve releases, and compare it with your own estimate.
  4. Start your first syndicate teardown using Module 19 §1.
  5. Write one "so-what" note on Lloyd's H1 2026 results (Module 19 §2).

Module 02

Actors, Entities and Players
Level Beginner
Path phase Phase 1 – Foundation, week 2 (Module 18)
Time 3–4 hours reading, 2 hours exercises
Prerequisites Module 01
You will be able to Name every party in the London market and explain what it does, how it earns money, who regulates it, and how it connects to the others; map the stakeholders for a consulting engagement; explain where profit is made and lost in the value chain

1. Why this chapter matters

In the first client meeting, people test you on the basics without saying so. If you call Lloyd's "an insurer", confuse a managing agent with a syndicate, or say a broker "works for the insurer", the client quietly downgrades you. If you explain how a managing agent's economics differ from its members' economics, they lean in.

This chapter gives you the full cast. For each party we cover four things: what it does, how it earns money, who regulates it, and what it means for a consultant.

2.1 Each party through three lenses

Party Carries risk? Handles money? Creates or holds data?
Insured Keeps deductible Pays premium, receives claims Original risk data
Retail / producing broker No Collects premium, passes on Client data, first submission
London broker No (but liable for its own negligence) Holds premium and claims money Submission, MRC, accounting messages
Coverholder / MGA No (acts for insurers) Collects premium, deducts commission Bound risk data, bordereaux
Managing agent No (manages for members) Manages syndicate funds Underwriting, claims, finance data
Syndicate (members) Yes Premium trust funds —
Company market insurer Yes Own accounts Own systems
Reinsurer Yes (for ceded share) Reinsurance premium and recoveries Treaty and cession data
Corporation of Lloyd's Only as last resort (Central Fund) Central Fund, central settlement Market returns, plans, capital
Velonetic (bureau) No Processes settlement Signed premium and claims records

Why this table matters: most operational problems happen at the hand-offs between columns – when money moves without matching data, or data moves without matching money.

3. Lloyd's in depth

3.1 What Lloyd's actually is

certain Lloyd's is a society of members established by Act of Parliament. Three things share the name:

"Lloyd's" can mean What it is
The Society of Lloyd's The legal body made up of all members, governed by the Lloyd's Acts 1871–1982
The Corporation of Lloyd's The organisation (staff, executive, Council) that runs, regulates and supports the market
The Lloyd's market All the syndicates, managing agents, brokers and coverholders that do business there

Lloyd's itself does not choose risks. Syndicates, run by managing agents, underwrite using capital provided by members. The exception is Lloyd's Europe (Lloyd's Insurance Company S.A.), which is a legal insurer for EEA risks and reinsures them to syndicates.

Useful analogy: Lloyd's is like a stock exchange. The exchange sets rules, admits participants, provides infrastructure and protects the system. The traders (syndicates) take the positions. Unlike a stock exchange, Lloyd's also guarantees the system through the Central Fund.

3.2 Governance

Element Role Notes
Lloyd's Act 1982 Legal basis of governance Gives the Council power to make byelaws certain
Council of Lloyd's Governing body: management and supervision of the market Includes working members (from the market), external members (independent) and nominated members; members serve three-year terms, renewable up to nine years likely
Chair Leads the Council Sir Charles Roxburgh, from 1 May 2025 likely
CEO and executive team Run the Corporation day to day Patrick Tiernan, CEO from 1 June 2025 likely
Council committees Delegated oversight Committees have included Nominations & Governance, Remuneration, Risk, Audit and Investment; special bodies include a Capacity Transfer Panel and the Lloyd's Enforcement Board likely – committee structure was revised in March 2026, check the current terms of reference
Byelaws and Market Bulletins Rules and instructions to the market Market Bulletins (numbered Y####) are the main channel for new requirements

3.3 What the Corporation does

Function What it means for managing agents
Market oversight Approves syndicate business plans (SBF), monitors performance, applies principles-based oversight, intervenes when needed
Capital Sets member capital (ECA = uSCR × 135%), runs Coming into Line, manages the Central Fund certain
Licensing and international Maintains licences and trust funds worldwide; runs international platforms
Market services Policy on placement, claims and accounting standards; sponsors market infrastructure
Delegated authority Approves coverholders; sets DA standards; registers binding authorities
Innovation Lloyd's Lab; product and capital innovation (e.g., London Bridge for ILS capital)
Data and returns Collects market data (plans, results, RDS, capital returns)

How the Corporation is funded likely: annual subscriptions and Central Fund contributions from members (set as small percentages of capacity/premium), plus charges for specific services. Clients often complain about the "cost of doing business at Lloyd's"; the 2026–2030 strategy commits to reducing it likely.

3.4 Syndicates

What a syndicate is certain:

  • One or more members joined together to underwrite. Not a legal entity – it cannot own property or sue in its own name.
  • Each member is severally liable for its share only – not for other members' shares.
  • Identified by a number (e.g., 2623, 1618, 2478).
  • Has a stamp capacity: the maximum premium income (net of acquisition costs) it may accept in a year of account, approved by Lloyd's.
  • Each year of account operates as a separate venture and is normally closed after three years by reinsurance to close (RITC). See Module 07.

Market size in 2026:

Measure Value Tag
Aggregate stamp capacity about £58bn (up about 4% on 2025) likely – Insurance Insider survey
Approved capital about £36.2bn (up 7.4%) likely
New syndicates at 1 January 2026 13 likely
Number of syndicates around 100 likely – check Lloyd's directory

Types of syndicate

Type How it works Why it exists
Standard syndicate Full syndicate managed by a managing agent with its own underwriting teams Core of the market
Syndicate-in-a-Box (SIAB) Small new syndicate with a lighter set-up, usually hosted by a third-party managing agent Lower barrier to entry for innovative businesses
Special Purpose Arrangement (SPA) Writes a fixed quota share of a host syndicate's business Lets capital providers access a host's underwriting without building a team
Capital-backed / reinsurance syndicates Syndicate backed by an investor to reinsure a group's portfolio – e.g., AIG's Talbot syndicate 2478 backed by Blackstone (2025 capacity reported at £567m) likely Brings institutional capital into Lloyd's
Follow-only / algorithmic syndicates Follow other leaders using data and rules – e.g., Ki (syndicate 1618) likely; AIG-backed syndicate 2479 managed by Talbot using AI agents from 2026 likely Low-cost capacity deployment
Consortium Several syndicates (sometimes companies) writing a class under one leader Access expertise and distribution

Partner insight likely: the growth of capital-backed and follow-only syndicates means more of Lloyd's capacity is "passive" – it follows other people's underwriting decisions. In a softening market this is where adverse selection shows up first. Expect Lloyd's oversight to focus here.

3.5 Managing agents

What they are certain: companies authorised to manage syndicates on behalf of members. They employ the underwriters and support teams and run the business.

Typical organisation of a managing agent

flowchart TB
  BOARD[Board<br/>with independent NEDs] --> CEO
  CEO --> CUO[Chief Underwriting Officer<br/>class underwriting teams]
  CEO --> CFO[CFO<br/>finance, actuarial, capital,<br/>reinsurance]
  CEO --> COO[COO<br/>operations, technical accounting,<br/>DA operations, IT, data]
  CEO --> CCO[Chief Claims Officer]
  CEO --> CRO[CRO<br/>risk, exposure management]
  CEO --> COMP[Compliance / conduct]
  BOARD --> IA[Internal audit]

How managing agents earn money

Income Description
Managing agency fee A percentage of capacity, paid by members
Profit commission A share of syndicate profit (often around 15–20% likely)
Group economics Where the managing agent belongs to an insurance group that also provides the capital (aligned capital), the group takes most of the syndicate profit directly

Ownership models likely:

Model Examples of the type
Listed specialty insurers Groups listed in London or the US with Lloyd's platforms
Divisions of global insurers and reinsurers Large groups using Lloyd's for licences and specialty access
Private / private-equity backed Independent managing agents
Turnkey / third-party managing agents Host syndicates (often SIABs and SPAs) for other capital providers and underwriting teams

Regulation: dual-regulated by the PRA and FCA, and overseen by Lloyd's. Senior managers are approved under the Senior Managers & Certification Regime.

Consulting angle: the managing agent is your usual client at Lloyd's. Its executives answer to three masters: the board/members (returns), Lloyd's (plan and standards) and the regulators. A recommendation that ignores any of the three will fail.

3.6 Members – the capital providers

Member type Description Liability Share of capital
Corporate members Companies, mostly owned by insurance groups and investors Limited Large majority likely
Names – individual members Wealthy individuals; historically unlimited liability Old unlimited Names are now few; new individual capital comes through limited liability vehicles (NameCos, Scottish limited partnerships) About 10% of market capital likely
Third-party / institutional capital Pension funds, ILS funds, private equity, via SPAs, corporate members or London Bridge (Lloyd's ILS vehicle) Limited Growing – London Bridge 2 opened 14 new cells with about $660m in Q4 2025 likely

Members' agents advise Names on which syndicates to back and manage their participations certain. Names buy and sell syndicate participations in annual capacity auctions; the price reflects expected profitability.

Aligned vs non-aligned capital

Aligned Non-aligned / third-party
Source Managing agent's own group Names, investors, other insurers
Governance concern Group may favour its own capital Fair treatment of third-party capital; conflicts in allocating risks between syndicates
Trend 2026 Still dominant Growing – Lloyd's strategy aims to attract more sophisticated capital and simplify Names processes likely

Consulting angle: when a managing agent manages several syndicates with different capital providers, decisions about which syndicate takes which risk are a conflict of interest that Lloyd's and regulators watch. Allocation rules and audit trails are a frequent governance topic.

3.7 Lloyd's international platforms

Platform Purpose
Lloyd's Europe (Brussels) EEA risks; local offices across Europe
US Surplus lines eligibility, reinsurance, US trust funds; admitted licences in a few states likely
Canada Licensed with Canadian trust funds
Lloyd's China (Shanghai), Lloyd's Asia (Singapore), Lloyd's Japan, Lloyd's Dubai (DIFC), Lloyd's India (reinsurance branches, including GIFT City) Local platforms where syndicates write through service companies or branches likely

4. The company market in depth

4.1 Who is in it

Type Description
UK-authorised insurers Companies authorised by the PRA writing specialty and wholesale business in London
UK branches of overseas groups Global insurers and reinsurers writing in London through branches
Reinsurers Global reinsurers with London operations
Hybrid groups Groups with both a company platform and a Lloyd's syndicate

The IUA (International Underwriting Association) represents the company market. It reported £49.3bn of premium for 2024, including £5.5bn written elsewhere but controlled from London likely.

4.2 Lloyd's syndicate or company platform? How groups decide

Factor Lloyd's syndicate Company platform
Licences Uses Lloyd's 200+ licences and US surplus lines eligibility Must obtain own licences or use fronting
Rating Lloyd's market rating, shared by all syndicates Own rating – depends on group strength
Capital Lloyd's capital rules (ECA, uplift), mutual Central Fund Solvency UK on own balance sheet
Capital efficiency Lloyd's argues more risk per unit of capital than elsewhere likely Depends on diversification of the group
Cost Lloyd's subscriptions, Central Fund contributions, oversight costs Own regulatory and infrastructure costs
Oversight Lloyd's plus PRA and FCA PRA and FCA
Brand Lloyd's brand helps in many territories Group brand
Flexibility Annual business plan approval More freedom to grow or change

Partner insight: for hybrid groups, the choice of platform for each class is a strategic lever for cost, capital and access. Ask the client: "What decides which platform a risk goes on – and when did you last check the economics?"

5. Brokers in depth

5.1 Types of brokers

Type Role Examples of the type likely
Global brokers Retail, wholesale and reinsurance under one group Marsh McLennan, Aon, WTW, Gallagher, Howden, Lockton
Wholesale / specialty brokers Receive business from retail brokers worldwide and place in London Specialist London wholesalers and US wholesale groups with London arms
Reinsurance brokers Treaty and facultative reinsurance Reinsurance divisions of global brokers and independents
Producing / retail broker Holds the client relationship locally Thousands worldwide
Lloyd's broker Broker accredited to place business at Lloyd's —

5.2 Inside a London broker

Team Role
Account executives / client managers Own the client or producing broker relationship
Placing brokers Present risks and negotiate with underwriters
Technicians / placement support Prepare MRCs, check contract certainty, submit data
Accounts / credit control Premium collection and settlement
Claims brokers Notify, present and negotiate claims
Facilities teams Design and run broker facilities and follow capacity
Analytics and data Market data, placement analytics, client insight

5.3 How brokers earn money

Source Note
Brokerage / commission Percentage of premium; typically 10–25% depending on class likely
Fees Agreed with clients, sometimes in place of commission
Contingent / profit commission, placement service agreements Paid by insurers; must be disclosed and conflicts managed
Facility income From pre-arranged capacity facilities
Investment income on client money Where permitted

5.4 Broker strategy themes in 2026 likely

Theme What it means
Consolidation Large acquisitions continue; scale gives leverage over carriers and data
Facilities and follow capacity Brokers build pre-agreed capacity to place risks faster and earn more; carriers worry about adverse selection and loss of underwriting control
Data ownership Brokers create the first structured data about a risk and increasingly treat it as an asset
Soft market More capacity available; brokers' leverage on terms increases

5.5 Whose agent is the broker?

certain In the open market, the broker is normally the agent of the insured: it owes duties of care and good faith to the client and must make a fair presentation on the client's behalf. In some tasks the broker acts for insurers (e.g., holding premium under a risk transfer agreement). Where a broker is also a coverholder, it binds risks as agent of the insurer – a conflict that must be managed.

Consulting angle: when a carrier asks for help with "broker data quality", remember that brokers act for clients, not carriers. Solutions must give brokers something too: faster quotes, fewer queries, quicker payment.

6. Delegated authority players (summary)

Party Role
Coverholder Firm authorised to bind Lloyd's business under a binding authority agreement
MGA Managing general agent – similar role, broader term (company market, US)
Service company Coverholder owned by a managing agent's group; Lloyd's lists about 412 likely
DCA / TPA Delegated claims administrator / third-party administrator handling claims
Lloyd's Europe coverholders Authorised to bind EEA business for Lloyd's Europe

About 45% of Lloyd's premium comes through delegated authority likely. Full detail in Module 05.

Body What it does When it appears in your projects

LIIBA Represents London brokers Any change that affects broker workflow
