LIMOSS Owned by LMA and IUA; manages market service contracts, including bureau services Bureau changes and market service costs
Velonetic Bureau service provider (part of DXC), formerly Xchanging; premium, claims, accounting and settlement services Any accounting, settlement or claims process project; refocused on incremental modernisation after Blueprint Two likely

ACORD Data standards (GRLC messages, Core Data Record) Data model and integration projects
Lloyd's Lab Insurtech accelerator Innovation scouting, AI pilots

10. The contracts that connect everyone

Relationship Contract Key terms
Client – broker Terms of business / engagement letter Services, remuneration, duties
Broker – insurer TOBA (Terms of Business Agreement) Authority, premium handling, risk transfer, documentation, claims, data
Insured – insurers MRC and policy wording Cover, limits, premium, subscription agreement
Insurer – coverholder Binding authority agreement Classes, territories, limits, GPI, commission, reporting, claims authority
Insurer – DCA/TPA Claims administration agreement Authority, loss funds, reporting
Managing agent – members Standard managing agent's agreement Management of syndicate, fees, profit commission
Member – Lloyd's Membership and capital arrangements FAL, Central Fund contributions, byelaws
Insurer – reinsurer Reinsurance contract (often also an MRC) Cover, retention, limits, premium, claims cooperation
Market firms – bureau Service agreements via LIMOSS Processing services and charges

11. Where the money is made – profit pools

Participant Main source of profit Main risk to profit 2026 direction likely
Lead underwriters Price-setting power, selection Mispricing, cycle Margins narrowing as rates fall
Follow underwriters Low-cost capacity deployment Adverse selection, dependence on leads Most at risk in soft market
Coverholders / MGAs Commission, profit commission Capacity withdrawal by carriers Continued growth but tighter scrutiny
Brokers Brokerage, fees, facility income Regulation of conflicts, client pressure Strong; consolidating
Managing agents Fees, profit commission, group returns Cost of doing business, oversight Efficiency focus
Capital providers Underwriting and investment returns Catastrophes, reserve deterioration Inflows continuing, returns falling from highs
Bureau and infrastructure Service charges Modernisation risk Incremental change

Partner insight: in a softening market, profit moves away from capital providers and followers towards distribution (brokers, MGAs) and the best leads. Clients who do not have a distinctive lead position or distribution access face shrinking margins. This is the strategic backdrop to most 2026–2027 engagements.

12. Stakeholder map for a typical engagement at a managing agent

Example: a bordereaux automation and DA oversight programme.

Stakeholder Interest What they need from you
CEO Expense ratio, Lloyd's relationship Value case, risk to plan
COO Cost, operations stability Operating model, delivery plan
CUO / class underwriters Faster, better data on their portfolios Underwriting MI, no extra admin
Head of DA Oversight quality, audit findings Framework, tooling, coverholder engagement
CFO Premium recognition, aged debt, reserving Finance data quality, reconciliation
CRO / exposure management Accurate aggregates Timely, validated risk data
Compliance Lloyd's and FCA requirements Control design and evidence
IT / data Architecture, security Integration and data model
Coverholders Simpler reporting Clear standards, fewer queries
London broker of the binders Workflow impact Early engagement
Lloyd's (indirectly) DA standards and oversight Evidence of control improvement

13. Mini case – a coverholder fails

Situation: a US coverholder writing property for four syndicates becomes insolvent. It has collected premium from insureds that it has not paid to the London broker, and it has open claims.

Who Impact
Insureds Cover remains with the syndicates (the coverholder bound on their behalf); claims must still be paid
Syndicates Unpaid premium (credit loss) unless risk transfer applies; must take over claims handling (often through a TPA); data may be incomplete
London broker Depending on TOBA and risk transfer terms, may have exposure for premium; relationship damage
Managing agents Must report to Lloyd's; oversight failures examined; possible audit findings
Lloyd's Reviews managing agents' DA oversight
Regulators FCA interest if customers harmed

Lessons: premium remittance timeliness, risk transfer terms, data completeness and exit plans are not admin details – they decide who loses money when a coverholder fails.

14. Common mistakes to avoid

Mistake Correct position
"Lloyd's paid the claim." Syndicates pay claims; the Central Fund is a last resort.
"Lloyd's broker works for Lloyd's." The broker normally acts for the client.
"A syndicate is a company." A syndicate is not a legal entity; members are severally liable.
"The managing agent owns the syndicate's profit." Members own the profit; the managing agent earns fees and profit commission (unless the group is also the capital provider).
"The coverholder is just a broker." When binding, the coverholder acts for the insurer.
"Velonetic is part of Lloyd's." Velonetic is a service provider (DXC) contracted for bureau services.
"IUA is a regulator." IUA is a trade association.

15. Partner's lens – how a top-tier strategy consultant would use this chapter

Written in the working style of senior partners at McKinsey, Bain and BCG. It describes widely documented methods, not insider material from any firm.

1. Map the value chain before the org chart. Partners start any industry by drawing the value chain and the profit pool at each step (section 11). Clients often ask "how do we grow?"; the partner asks "where in the chain is profit moving, and are you standing in the right place?" For a London follow syndicate in 2026, the honest answer may be: "Profit is moving away from you."

2. Understand incentives, not just roles. For each actor, ask: what is it paid for, and what is it not paid for? A coverholder paid on premium volume will write volume. A broker paid on facility income will steer risks to facilities. A managing agent paid on capacity fees likes growth. Most market problems are incentive problems before they are process problems.

3. Identify the decision-maker and the blocker. In every engagement, name three people: who decides (often CEO or COO), who pays (budget owner), and who can block (often the CUO or the Head of DA, because change lands on their teams). Partners spend as much time on the blocker as on the buyer.

4. Use the "three masters" test for managing agents. Every recommendation must work for (a) the board and capital providers, (b) Lloyd's, and (c) the PRA and FCA. If it fails one, it fails. Write the test into your steering committee papers.

5. Spot the structural shifts early. The 2026 shifts worth a CEO's attention: institutional capital entering through capital-backed syndicates and London Bridge; follow-only and AI-assisted capacity; broker consolidation and facilities; MGA growth; Lloyd's moving from Blueprint Two to incremental standards. A partner would turn each into a question for the client's strategy: "What happens to your business if this continues for three more years?"

6. Build the client's stakeholder map in the first week. Use section 12 as the template. Partners never wait for the client to tell them who matters.

16. Key takeaways

  1. Lloyd's is a society and a market; syndicates take risk, managing agents run them, members provide capital, the Corporation regulates and supports.
  2. The company market is of similar scale to Lloyd's; many groups run both platforms and choose per risk.
  3. Brokers sit at the centre of the market and act for clients; their data and facilities give them growing power.
  4. Delegated authority adds coverholders, MGAs and claims administrators – and most operational risk.
  5. Market bodies and the bureau shape what any single firm can change.
  6. In a softening market, profit is shifting from capital and followers towards distribution and top leads.

17. Check your understanding

# Question Answer
1 What are the three meanings of "Lloyd's"? The Society (members), the Corporation (organisation running the market), the market (all participants).
2 Why is a syndicate not liable for another member's share? Liability is several, not joint; each member is liable for its own share.
3 Name two ways managing agents earn income. Managing agency fees and profit commission.
4 What is the difference between aligned and non-aligned capital? Aligned comes from the managing agent's own group; non-aligned from third parties (Names, investors).
5 Give two reasons a group might write a class at Lloyd's rather than on its company platform. Lloyd's licences/US surplus lines access; shared Lloyd's rating; capital efficiency; brand.
6 Whose agent is a coverholder when it binds a risk? The insurer's.
7 What does LIMOSS do? Manages market service contracts (including bureau services) on behalf of LMA and IUA members.
8 In a soft market, which participants' margins are usually under most pressure? Follow underwriters and capital providers.

18. Practice tasks

  1. Draw the full market map from memory, then add money and data flows in two colours.
  2. Pick one managing agent and find from public sources: owner, syndicates, main classes, capital type (aligned or not). Write a one-page profile.
  3. Write a stakeholder map (section 12 format) for a claims transformation programme at the same managing agent.
  4. Practitioner conversation: ask a broker or underwriter to explain how facilities have changed their work in the last two years (interview guide in Module 19 §3).

Module 03

Lines of Business and Products
Level Beginner–Intermediate
Path phase Phase 1 – Foundation, week 3 (Module 18)
Time 4 hours reading, 2 hours exercises
Prerequisites Modules 01–02
You will be able to Explain what each major London class covers, who buys it, how it is placed and priced, what data and wordings matter, what typical claims look like, and what each class's leaders worry about in late 2026

1. Why this chapter matters

Each class of business is almost a separate industry, with its own data, models, wordings, brokers and experts. A consultant who treats "London specialty" as one thing gives generic advice. A consultant who knows that a hull underwriter thinks about vessel age and trading area, while a cyber underwriter thinks about technology dependency and aggregation, gives advice that lands.

For each class, this chapter covers the same eight points: what it covers, who buys, how it is placed, key data, key wordings, pricing, typical claims, and the 2026 agenda.

2. How Lloyd's groups its business

Lloyd's segment certain Main contents
Reinsurance Property treaty, casualty treaty, specialty treaty
Property Direct and facultative property, binders, terrorism, political violence
Casualty General liability, professional indemnity, D&O, financial institutions, medical malpractice, cyber
Marine, Aviation & Transport (MAT) Hull, cargo, marine liability, specie, war, aviation, space
Energy Upstream, downstream, power, renewables, energy liability
Motor UK and overseas motor
Life Term life, personal accident (small)

Property, reinsurance and casualty make up the large majority of Lloyd's premium likely. The US and Canada provide roughly half of premium likely.

3. Marine

3.1 Hull and machinery (H&M)

Point Detail
Covers Physical loss of or damage to ships (collision, grounding, fire, heavy weather); collision liability (often three-fourths in English hull clauses); general average contributions
Buyers Shipowners and operators; banks financing ships (as co-assureds or loss payees)
Placement Mostly open market subscription; large fleets led by specialist marine underwriters; Nordic market competes
Key data Fleet list with IMO numbers, vessel type, age, flag, classification society, tonnage, values, trading areas, claims history, management company
Key wordings Institute Time Clauses (Hulls), International Hull Clauses, Nordic Marine Insurance Plan certain
Pricing Rate on agreed value, adjusted for fleet size, age, type, claims record, deductibles
Typical claims Machinery breakdown (frequent), groundings, collisions, fires on car carriers and container ships
2026 agenda likely Fires on large vessels (car carriers carrying electric vehicles); sanctions compliance for vessels in the "shadow fleet"; ageing fleet; values rising with newbuild costs

3.2 Cargo

Point Detail
Covers Goods in transit by sea, air, road and rail, including storage incidental to transit; often on an annual open cover for all shipments
Buyers Traders, manufacturers, commodity houses, retailers, freight forwarders
Placement Open covers via open market or binders; large commodity traders placed by specialist brokers
Key data Commodities, packing, routes, conveyances, annual shipped values, stock storage locations and values
Key wordings Institute Cargo Clauses (A) all risks, (B) and (C) named perils certain
Pricing Rate per £100 of shipped value; stock throughput policies priced on values at risk in storage
Typical claims Theft, water damage, temperature failure (reefers), general average, port explosions or fires
2026 agenda likely Port and warehouse accumulation (one event hitting many cargo policies); supply-chain disruption; war and strikes cover in conflict zones

3.3 Marine liability and P&I reinsurance

Point Detail
Covers Liabilities of ports, terminals, charterers, ship repairers, freight forwarders. Shipowners' third-party liability (P&I) is mostly insured by mutual P&I Clubs, whose large losses are pooled and reinsured – heavily in London certain
Typical claims Cargo liability, injury, pollution, wreck removal, damage to fixed objects (e.g., bridges)
Real example The 2024 Baltimore bridge collapse involving the Dali – a large P&I and liability loss certain

3.4 Marine war, specie and others

Sub-class Detail
Marine war War, strikes, terrorism, piracy, confiscation. Additional premium for voyages into listed areas set by the Joint War Committee certain; notice of cancellation provisions allow quick re-pricing
Specie Cash in transit, vaults, precious metals, fine art and jewellery in transit and storage
Yacht Private and commercial yachts

Marine – consulting questions: "How do you track vessel movements against sanctions and listed areas in real time?" "How do you measure cargo accumulation at major ports?"

4. Aviation and space

Sub-class Covers Key points
Airline hull and liability Aircraft damage; passenger and third-party liability Very large limits; few large leads; fleet data and safety record
Aviation war War, hijack, confiscation – excluded from all risks by the AVN 48B clause and written back in war cover certain Separate panel from all risks
Lessors' contingent / possessed cover Protects lessors if the airline's cover fails or aircraft are not returned Central to the Russia litigation
Aerospace products Liability of manufacturers and component makers Long-tail product liability
Airports and general aviation Airport liability; smaller aircraft Smaller, more binder business
Space Launch, in-orbit satellite risks Few risks, huge values; cyclical

Real case likely: in June 2025 the High Court found lessors could recover under war risks cover for aircraft stuck in Russia; AerCap recovered just over $1bn; the Court of Appeal granted insurers permission to appeal in March 2026. Lesson: which panel pays depends on wording and cause.

2026 agenda likely: litigation reserves; reinsurance disputes; pricing of war covers; satellite constellations' accumulation.

5. Energy

Sub-class Covers Key points
Upstream Offshore platforms, rigs, pipelines; control of well / operators' extra expense (OEE); construction Engineering surveys; cat exposure (Gulf of Mexico windstorm)
Downstream Refineries, petrochemical plants – property damage and business interruption Engineering surveys; large BI values; maximum foreseeable loss estimates
Power and renewables Power stations, onshore and offshore wind, solar, battery storage, hydrogen New technologies; limited loss history; serial losses from component defects
Energy liability Pollution, third-party liability Long-tail

2026 agenda likely: energy transition (balancing fossil fuel exposure policies with renewables growth); serial defects in wind turbines; cyber exposure of industrial control systems; war and political violence for energy infrastructure.

Consulting question: "How do you price new energy technologies with little loss history, and how do you aggregate serial defect risk?"

6. Property

6.1 Direct and facultative (D&F) property

Point Detail
Covers Large commercial and industrial property damage and business interruption; facultative reinsurance of individual large risks
Buyers Corporations, real estate owners, manufacturers; insurers buying facultative cover
Placement Open market subscription, layered towers; growing use of broker facilities likely
Key data Statement of values (SOV): each location's address (geocoded), construction, occupancy, protection, exposure (COPE), values for buildings, contents, BI
Pricing Cat models for natural perils; attritional rating; layer pricing (rate on line)
Typical claims Fire, explosion, windstorm, flood, earthquake, wildfire; BI from supply chain
2026 agenda likely Rates falling after strong years; secondary perils (convective storms, wildfire, flood) growing; SOV data quality; valuation adequacy (under-insurance after inflation)

6.2 US E&S property through binders

Point Detail
Covers Small commercial and residential property in hard-to-place US areas (coastal, wildfire)
Placement Delegated authority: coverholders bind locally; bordereaux report risks
Key data Risk bordereaux with locations, construction, values, deductibles
2026 agenda likely Cat aggregation from DA data quality; competition from US domestic E&S carriers; softening rates

6.3 Terrorism and political violence

Point Detail
Covers Terrorism, sabotage, strikes, riots, civil commotion, insurrection, war (in political violence)
Context State schemes in some countries (e.g., Pool Re in the UK, TRIA backstop in the US) certain; London writes stand-alone covers globally
2026 agenda likely Civil unrest; conflict zones; aggregation in city centres

6.4 Business interruption

Loss of gross profit or revenue after insured physical damage; contingent BI for supplier or customer damage. The COVID-19 BI disputes showed how wording precision drives outcomes.

Property – consulting questions: "What share of your SOV is geocoded to rooftop level?" "What is your view on secondary perils, and is it in your pricing?"

7. Casualty and liability

Sub-class Covers Trigger Key points
General liability (GL) Bodily injury and property damage to third parties Usually occurrence US casualty is a large London market; excess layers above US primaries
Professional indemnity (PI) / E&O Errors by professionals (lawyers, accountants, technology firms, engineers) Claims made Retroactive dates; aggregation of related claims
Directors & officers (D&O) Claims against directors and managers Claims made Side A/B/C; securities class actions; "AI washing" claims emerging likely
Financial institutions (FI) Bankers' blanket bond (crime), FI PI, FI D&O Claims made / discovery Fraud; cyber crime; deepfake payment fraud likely
Medical malpractice Hospitals and practitioners Claims made Long tail; jurisdiction-specific
Cyber Data breach costs, ransomware, business interruption from IT outages, liability Claims made / event Systemic risk; war and state-backed attack exclusions certain

7.1 Key casualty concepts

Concept Meaning
Long tail Claims reported and settled years after the policy period
Social inflation Rising US claims costs from large jury awards, litigation funding and legal trends – the main reserve risk for US casualty certain
Retroactive date Claims-made policies cover only acts after this date
Extended reporting period Time after expiry to report claims
Aggregation clauses Whether related claims count as one claim (one limit and one deductible)

7.2 Cyber in more detail

Point Detail
First-party covers Incident response, data restoration, business interruption, extortion
Third-party covers Privacy liability, network security liability, regulatory defence
Exclusions Since 31 March 2023 Lloyd's requires exclusions for state-backed cyber attacks using approved clauses (e.g., LMA5564–5567) certain; infrastructure and war exclusions
Systemic risk One event (software flaw, cloud outage, widespread malware) can hit thousands of insureds
Data Revenue, sector, security controls, technology dependencies
2026 agenda likely Soft pricing; ransomware frequency; aggregation modelling; AI-enabled attacks

Casualty – consulting questions: "How are you reflecting social inflation in pricing and older-year reserves?" "How do you measure systemic cyber accumulation, including silent cyber in other classes?"

8. Specialty

Sub-class Covers Key points
Political risk Confiscation, expropriation, nationalisation and deprivation (CEND); currency inconvertibility; contract frustration; political violence Country risk; long policy periods; sanctions
Credit and surety Trade credit; structured credit; surety bonds Linked to economic cycle; bank capital relief deals
Kidnap and ransom (K&R) Ransom, crisis consultants, extortion Confidentiality; specialist response firms
Contingency Event cancellation, non-appearance, weather COVID-19 losses reshaped wordings (communicable disease exclusions)
Fine art, jewellery, specie High-value assets Valuation; security
Bloodstock Racehorses and breeding stock Mortality and fertility
Accident and health (A&H) Personal accident, travel, sports disability Often via coverholders and affinity programmes
Product recall and contamination Recall costs, loss of profit Food and pharma
AI liability (emerging) Affirmative cover for AI model errors – e.g., Armilla AI, Testudo likely New class; silent AI elsewhere

Specialty – consulting question: "Where are you carrying unpriced AI or political exposure in existing wordings?"

9. Motor and other smaller classes

London writes some UK and international motor (often fleets and non-standard risks) and overseas motor through coverholders. It is a smaller part of Lloyd's than in UK domestic insurers likely.

10. Reinsurance in depth

10.1 Structures

Type How it works Example
Treaty Covers a whole portfolio automatically Quota share of a cedant's US property book
Facultative One risk, negotiated individually Reinsuring one large refinery
Proportional Premium and losses shared in the same proportion; reinsurer pays ceding commission Quota share (fixed %), surplus (by lines above retention)
Non-proportional (XoL) Pays losses above a retention up to a limit Per-risk XoL; cat XoL $100m xs $50m
Aggregate / stop loss Protects annual aggregate losses or loss ratio Pays if loss ratio exceeds 75%
Retrocession Reinsurance of reinsurers
Alternative capital Cat bonds, collateralised reinsurance, sidecars, industry loss warranties Lloyd's London Bridge vehicle for ILS certain
Legacy Loss portfolio transfer, adverse development cover, RITC

10.2 Key terms

Term Meaning
Cedant Insurer buying reinsurance
Ceding commission Payment from reinsurer to cedant on proportional treaties
Reinstatement Restoring a used limit, often for extra premium
Hours clause Time window defining one "event" (e.g., 72 hours for windstorm)
Ultimate net loss (UNL) Cedant's loss after other recoveries, used to measure XoL losses
Losses occurring during (LOD) vs risks attaching during (RAD) Trigger basis of a treaty
Rate on line (ROL) Premium ÷ limit
Payback Years of premium needed to pay one full limit loss (1 ÷ ROL)

10.3 Renewal calendar certain

Date Market
1 January Europe, global programmes, much of London
1 April Japan and some Asia
1 June Florida property catastrophe
1 July US, Australia, New Zealand

10.4 Reinsurance market in 2026 likely

Plentiful capital and lighter recent catastrophe years are pushing property catastrophe prices down; casualty reinsurance remains focused on reserve adequacy for older US years. For buyers this is an opportunity to buy more protection at lower cost; for sellers, a margin squeeze.

11. Placement method by class likely

Class Open market Delegated authority Facilities / consortia
Marine hull High Low Medium
Cargo Medium Medium Medium
Aviation High Low Low
Energy High Low Medium
Large property D&F High Low High
US E&S small property Low High Medium
Casualty – large US High Medium Medium
Cyber SME Low High Medium
Cyber large corporate High Low Medium
A&H Low High Low
Treaty reinsurance High Low Low

Why it matters: open market problems are about broker–underwriter workflow, contract certainty and subscription; delegated problems are about bordereaux, oversight and data.

12. What each class's leaders worry about – conversation starters

Class Question for the class underwriter or CUO
Property "How confident are you in your SOV data and geocoding? What is your secondary peril view?"
Casualty "How are you reflecting social inflation in pricing and older-year reserves?"
Cyber "How do you measure systemic accumulation across cyber and non-cyber classes?"
Marine "How do you track vessels against sanctions and listed areas in real time?"
Aviation "What remaining exposure do you have to the Russia litigation, and how is it reserved?"
Energy "How do you price new technologies with little loss history?"
Reinsurance "With rates falling, how are you holding attachment points and terms?"
Specialty "Where do you carry unpriced AI or political exposure?"

13. Data by class – what a data model must hold

Class Critical data
Property Geocoded locations; COPE; values by coverage; deductibles; cat zones
Marine hull IMO number; vessel type; age; flag; class; values; trading areas
Cargo Commodities; routes; annual values; storage locations
Aviation Aircraft serial numbers; types; operators; lessors; values
Energy Asset type; location; values; engineering grades
Casualty Revenue; operations; jurisdictions; limits; retroactive dates
Cyber Revenue; sector; security controls; technology dependencies
Reinsurance Cedant portfolio data; exposure by zone; treaty terms

14. Partner's lens

How a senior top-tier consultant would use this chapter.

  1. Segment before you analyse. Never analyse a London portfolio as one block. The first split is usually by class; the second by channel (open market, DA, facility) or lead/follow. Many "portfolio problems" are one or two segments.
  2. Know each class's profit driver. Property is driven by cat and selection; casualty by reserving and social inflation; marine by fleet quality and sanctions; cyber by aggregation; reinsurance by attachment and terms. Your recommendation must address the driver of that class.
  3. Learn the class language fast. In the first week of a class-specific engagement, learn ten terms that class's underwriters use daily. Credibility follows immediately.
  4. Look for cross-class accumulation. Partners ask the question no single class asks: "What single event hits several of your classes at once?" (a port explosion hits cargo, property, marine liability and reinsurance). That question often leads to the most valuable work.

15. Key takeaways

  1. Each class is a different business with its own data, wordings, pricing and claims patterns.
  2. Property and reinsurance are driven by catastrophe and selection; casualty by reserving and social inflation; cyber by systemic aggregation.
  3. Marine, aviation and energy depend on specialist expertise, sanctions compliance and precise wordings.
  4. Placement channel differs by class, which shapes the operational problems you will see.
  5. In 2026, softening rates affect most classes; terms discipline and cross-class accumulation are key themes.

16. Check your understanding

# Question Answer
1 What does COPE stand for? Construction, occupancy, protection, exposure.
2 Which Institute Cargo Clause gives all-risks cover? Institute Cargo Clauses (A).
3 Which trigger is typical for D&O and PI? Claims made.
4 What is social inflation? Rising US liability claims costs from litigation trends and large jury awards.
5 Why do aviation war and all-risks panels matter? Different insurers pay depending on whether the loss is war or non-war – central to the Russia litigation.
6 A cat XoL has a 10% rate on line. What is the payback? 10 years.

17. Practice tasks

  1. Pick three classes and write a one-page "class profile" each using the eight points.
  2. Design a cross-class accumulation scenario for a major port (cargo, property, marine liability, reinsurance).
  3. Read one syndicate's annual report and classify its business by Lloyd's segment and channel.

Module 04

Placement and Underwriting: The Open Market Lifecycle
Level Intermediate
Path phase Phase 1 (late) and Phase 2 (Module 18)
Time 4–5 hours reading, 3 hours exercises
Prerequisites Modules 01–02
You will be able to Walk through every stage of an open market placement; read and critique an MRC; explain lead/follow, written and signed lines, the GUA and facilities; explain how underwriters price and control risk; diagnose where placement and underwriting lose time, money and data

1. Why this chapter matters

Placement is where the contract, the price and the first data about a risk are created. Every downstream problem – premium queries, reconciliation breaks, claims disputes, poor exposure data – can usually be traced back to something that happened, or did not happen, during placement. Underwriting is where most of the value is created or destroyed: a 5% mispricing on a £10m risk costs more than a year of process improvement in many teams.

2. Three routes into the market

Route Who decides on each risk Share of Lloyd's premium Economics
Open market Underwriter at syndicate or company, risk by risk, with a broker About half likely Lower acquisition cost than DA; high underwriting effort per risk
Delegated authority Coverholder/MGA within limits set by the insurer About 45% likely High acquisition cost (commission); low underwriting effort per risk for the insurer; high oversight cost
Facilities and consortia A leader or algorithm decides; followers bound by pre-agreed rules Growing; inside the two figures above Very low effort for followers; risk of adverse selection

This chapter covers the open market and facilities. Delegated authority is in Module 05.

3. The placement lifecycle – stage by stage

sequenceDiagram
  autonumber
  participant C as Client / Retail broker
  participant B as London broker
  participant L as Lead underwriter
  participant F as Follow underwriters
  participant P as Platform (PPL / Whitespace)
  participant V as Bureau (Velonetic)
  C->>B: Instructions and risk information
  B->>B: Market strategy, submission, draft MRC, UMR
  B->>L: Present risk
  L->>L: Assess, price, refer, sanctions and licensing checks
  L-->>B: Quote
  B->>C: Recommendation
  C->>B: Firm order
  B->>L: Request lead line
  L->>P: Write and stamp line
  B->>F: Show led MRC
  F->>P: Write follow lines
  B->>B: Lines ≥ 100%, sign down
  B->>C: Confirm cover, documentation
  B->>V: Premium and risk data
  V->>L: Signed line, SND
  V->>F: Signed line, SND
  C->>B: Gross premium
  B->>V: Net premium
  V->>L: Settlement
  V->>F: Settlement

Stage 0 – Renewal planning (90–120 days before expiry)
What happens Broker reviews the account: claims, changes in the client's business, last year's terms, market conditions. Sets a renewal strategy and timetable.
Data and documents Renewal information request to client; claims history; prior MRC and wording
Failure modes Late client information → rushed placement → contract certainty failures and extensions
Metric % renewals with complete information 30 days before inception

Stage 1 – Client engagement, KYC and classification
What happens Broker confirms the client, its ownership, sanctions status and regulatory classification (commercial size thresholds after FCA PS25/21 certain). Agrees terms of business.
Controls Sanctions and KYC screening; client classification; conflicts check
Failure modes Wrong classification → wrong conduct rules applied; incomplete sanctions screening

Stage 2 – Submission preparation
What happens Broker builds the submission: schedules (e.g., statement of values, fleet list), loss history, financials, engineering or survey reports, prior wording. Drafts the MRC and creates the Unique Market Reference (UMR).
Data and documents Submission pack (often 20–200 pages), spreadsheets, MRC draft
Failure modes Data in inconsistent formats; missing values; outdated schedules; the fair presentation is incomplete
Metric % submissions accepted by the lead without information requests

Fair presentation: under the Insurance Act 2015, the client (through the broker) must disclose every material circumstance, or enough to put the insurer on notice to ask, in a reasonably clear and accessible way certain. "Data dumping" – sending thousands of pages without structure – can fail the "clear and accessible" test.

Stage 3 – Market strategy
What happens Broker decides: which lead (expertise, claims reputation, appetite, rating), which followers, whether to use a facility, which platform, layering structure.
Considerations Client's security requirements (minimum ratings); broker's facility arrangements; conflicts of interest
Failure modes Steering business to facilities that suit the broker more than the client; concentration on a few leads

Stage 4 – Presentation to the lead
What happens Broker presents the risk – on a platform, by email, or face to face (still common at Lloyd's underwriting "boxes").
Failure modes Underwriters receive far more submissions than they can review properly; prioritisation is informal
Metric Time from submission received to first underwriter review

Stage 5 – Underwriting assessment
What happens Underwriter checks appetite and authority, assesses risk quality, runs pricing and cat models, checks sanctions and licensing, may refer to senior underwriters or peer review.
Outputs Decision (decline, quote, quote with conditions); technical price; underwriting file note
Failure modes Pricing outside guidelines without referral; weak audit trail; inconsistent decisions between underwriters
Detail Section 9 of this chapter

Stage 6 – Quote and negotiation
What happens Lead proposes premium, deductibles, limits, conditions, exclusions, line size, subjectivities (conditions to be satisfied, e.g., "subject to satisfactory survey within 30 days"). Broker negotiates.
Legal point A quote is usually an offer that can be accepted while valid; wording decides certain
Failure modes Multiple versions of terms; unclear which is final; subjectivities not tracked after binding

Stage 7 – Firm order and binding
What happens Client instructs the broker to bind. Lead writes its line – stamps and signs (or accepts electronically).
Legal point Each written line creates a binding contract with that insurer for its share, even before the placement is fully subscribed (General Reinsurance Corp v Forsakringsaktiebolaget Fennia Patria, 1983) certain
Failure modes Binding while key terms are "to be agreed" → contract certainty failure

Stage 8 – Following
What happens Broker shows the led MRC to follow markets, who write their lines on the leader's terms. Followers may add limited conditions (e.g., their own sanctions or choice-of-law requirements).
Failure modes Followers adding conditions that create different terms for different insurers; delays reaching 100%
Metric Time from lead line to 100% placed

Stage 9 – Signing down

If written lines exceed 100%, all lines are reduced proportionally so they total 100% (section 6.2).

Stage 10 – Contract certainty and documentation
What happens All terms finalised before inception; broker issues evidence of cover and final contract documentation to the client promptly.
Controls Contract certainty checks by broker and insurer; subjectivities tracked
Metric % contracts certain at inception; days to issue final documentation

Stage 11 – Premium and data processing
What happens Broker submits premium and contract data to the bureau (or directly to non-bureau insurers). Bureau validates and signs, allocating a Signing Number and Date (SND) to each entry. Premium settles through central settlement.
Failure modes Bureau queries due to data errors; mismatches between broker, bureau and insurer records
Detail Module 06 Part A

Stage 12 – Mid-term changes

Endorsements (additions, deletions, extensions, premium adjustments) agreed according to the subscription agreement and the General Underwriters' Agreement (GUA) (section 6.5), then processed through the bureau.

Stage 13 – Renewal

Treated as a new contract with a fresh duty of fair presentation certain. The incumbent lead has an advantage but must re-price.

4. The Market Reform Contract (MRC) in depth

4.1 What it is

The MRC is the standard London placing document and contract format, introduced in 2007 as part of the contract certainty reforms certain. It is used for open market insurance, reinsurance and binding authorities (MRC binding authority format). It can be paper/PDF or electronic on a placing platform.

4.2 The six sections

Section Contents What goes wrong
Risk Details UMR; type; insured; period; interest; situation; limits and sub-limits; deductibles; conditions; exclusions; premium; payment terms; choice of law and jurisdiction; taxes; recording, transmitting and storing information; notices Vague terms ("as expiring"); wordings referenced but not attached; missing exclusions
Information Information presented to insurers or reference to it Unclear what was presented → fair presentation disputes
Security Details Insurers' liability (several not joint); order hereon; basis of written lines; basis of signed lines; signing provisions; each insurer's stamp, line and reference Signed line basis not stated; lines "to stand" ignored
Subscription Agreement Slip leader; agreement parties for contract changes (GUA); claims agreement parties; claims administration; rules for followers' conditions; expenses Agreement parties unclear → disputes on changes and claims
Fiscal and Regulatory Tax payable by insurers; country of origin; regulatory information (e.g., US surplus lines details); allocation of premium to coding Wrong tax or regulatory codes → bureau queries, tax errors
Broker Remuneration and Deductions Brokerage and other deductions Mismatched brokerage between systems

4.3 Sample Risk Details extract (fictional)

UNIQUE MARKET REFERENCE:  B9999ABC2026001
TYPE:                     Property Damage and Business Interruption
INSURED:                  Atlas Logistics Ltd and subsidiaries
PERIOD:                   From 01 January 2027 to 31 December 2027, both days inclusive,
                          local standard time at the insured's address
INTEREST:                 All real and personal property ... and business interruption
SITUATION:                As per schedule of locations attached (Appendix A)
SUM INSURED / LIMIT:      GBP 250,000,000 any one occurrence
SUB-LIMITS:               Flood GBP 25,000,000 any one occurrence and in the aggregate
DEDUCTIBLE:               GBP 1,000,000 each and every loss
CONDITIONS:               As per wording attached (Appendix B) including
                          LMA3100 Sanction limitation and exclusion clause
EXCLUSIONS:               As per wording, including war, terrorism, cyber (LMA5400)
PREMIUM:                  GBP 1,850,000 (100%)
PAYMENT TERMS:            Premium payment clause LSW3001 – 60 days
CHOICE OF LAW:            England and Wales; exclusive jurisdiction of English courts
SUBJECTIVITIES:           Survey of Site 4 within 45 days of inception

likely Clause numbers shown are illustrative of real LMA clauses (LMA3100 is the market sanctions clause certain); always check the current clause library.

Critique exercise: what would you check? (1) Is Appendix A attached and dated? (2) Is the flood sub-limit consistent with the cat model output? (3) Is the subjectivity tracked with an owner? (4) Is the cyber exclusion appropriate for business interruption exposure?

4.4 Unique Market Reference (UMR)

Format: "B" + 4-digit broker code + broker's own reference certain. The UMR is the primary key across placement, accounting and claims. Duplicate or wrong UMRs break reconciliation downstream – check this first in any data quality review.

5. Contract certainty

Aspect Detail
Definition All terms agreed by all parties before inception; contract documentation provided promptly after certain
Origin Regulator pressure (FSA) in 2004–2007 after widespread late documentation and disputes
Elements to check Complete MRC sections; wording attached or precisely referenced; no "to be agreed" items; subjectivities with deadlines; lines and signing basis stated; tax and regulatory information complete
Consequences of failure Coverage disputes; delayed claims; regulatory criticism; reinsurance disputes (reinsurer may not follow uncertain terms)
Measures % contracts certain at inception; % documentation issued within agreed days; number of post-inception changes to core terms

Consulting angle: automated MRC completeness and wording checks are among the highest-value, lowest-risk AI use cases in London (Module 09).

6. Subscription mechanics in depth

6.1 Lead selection

Brokers choose leads for: class expertise, claims reputation, line size, rating, willingness to lead, speed, and relationship. Clients often specify approved security. Partner insight: lead position is a competitive asset – leads see more risks first, influence terms, and attract follow capacity. Lead share is one of the best indicators of a syndicate's market standing.

6.2 Written vs signed lines – worked example

Insurer Written line Signed line (× 100/120)
Syndicate A (lead) 25% 20.83%
Syndicate B 20% 16.67%
Company C 30% 25.00%
Syndicate D 25% 20.83%
Company E 20% 16.67%
Total 120% 100%
  • A line marked "to stand" must not be signed down without that insurer's agreement.
  • The basis of signed lines in the MRC states how signing down works.
  • Why brokers overplace: to give the client a margin if a market withdraws, and to keep competitive tension.
  • Data issue: insurers often book the written line first and correct to the signed line later. Mismatches between written and signed lines are a common reconciliation break.

6.3 Several liability

Each insurer is liable only for its own signed share certain. If one insurer becomes insolvent, the others do not pay its share. This is why brokers' security committees vet each insurer.

6.4 Layered and subscribed – both at once

Large programmes combine layering and subscription: each layer has its own lead and followers, and sometimes its own price and wording.

Layer Limit Lead Followers Price (rate on line)
Primary £25m xs £1m Syndicate A 5 insurers 12%
1st excess £75m xs £26m Company C 7 insurers 4%
2nd excess £150m xs £101m Syndicate F 9 insurers 1.5%

Risk: excess layers may "follow form" the primary wording but with different exclusions – a common source of gaps.

6.5 The General Underwriters' Agreement (GUA)

Aspect Detail
Purpose Defines which contract changes the slip leader can agree on behalf of all followers, and which need agreement from other agreement parties certain
How it works Changes are categorised – e.g., administrative and minor changes can be agreed by the leader alone; material changes (e.g., significant changes to risk, limits or premium) need the leader plus other named agreement parties, or all insurers. The subscription agreement section of the MRC states the arrangement
Why it matters Without it, every change would require every follower's agreement – slow and costly
Consulting angle Track which endorsements needed all-insurer agreement and how long they took – a measure of subscription friction

7. Facilities, consortia and follow capacity

Structure How it works Benefit Risk
Lineslip Broker-arranged contract under which a lineslip leader (sometimes with a second lead) agrees individual declared risks for a class, binding all followers Speed; lower cost for followers Followers depend on the leader; adverse selection
Consortium Several insurers write a class together; a consortium leader binds for all Access to expertise and distribution Concentrated decision-making
Broker facility / portfolio facility Pre-committed follow capacity that automatically follows selected leads across many risks Lower friction; broker leverage; certainty of capacity for clients Conflict of interest; limited data for followers; margin pressure
Algorithmic / data-driven follow A syndicate decides follow lines using data and rules, often in minutes – e.g., Ki (syndicate 1618) likely Speed and low cost Model risk; in soft markets can follow underpriced risks

7.1 Why facilities matter in 2026 likely

  • Capacity is plentiful and rates are falling, so brokers have leverage to place more through facilities.
  • Followers gain volume at low cost but may lose independent pricing and risk-level data.
  • Lloyd's strategy emphasises leading underwriting performance, suggesting more scrutiny of passive follow business.

7.2 How to analyse facility performance (consulting method)

Analysis Question
Loss ratio by channel (open market lead / open market follow / facility) at similar rate levels Is facility business worse because of selection, or only mix?
Rate adequacy by channel Are facility rates below technical price?
Lead quality Which leads does the facility follow, and how do those leads perform?
Data availability Does the follower get risk-level data or only summaries?
Exit terms Can the follower withdraw mid-term?

8. Electronic placing

Platform Notes
PPL (Placing Platform Limited) Market placing platform launched 2016; moved to a "Next Gen" platform after delays likely
Whitespace Electronic placing platform widely used in London likely

What e-placing changes: a single shared contract record, timestamps and audit trail, electronic stamps and lines, structured data for parts of the MRC, and messages between broker and underwriters.

What it does not change by itself: submissions are still largely unstructured documents; underwriters' own systems still need data entry or integration; the bureau still processes the accounting.

Consulting angle: the value of e-placing depends on integration – pulling structured contract data from the platform into the insurer's underwriting workbench, policy system and finance without re-keying.

9. Underwriting inside the insurer

9.1 The underwriting control framework

flowchart TB
  A[Business plan - SBF<br/>approved by Lloyd's] --> B[Underwriting strategy<br/>and risk appetite]
  B --> C[Underwriting authorities<br/>per underwriter]
  C --> D[Line guides and<br/>pricing guidelines]
  D --> E[Risk decision]
  E --> F[Referral and peer review]
  F --> G[Bound risk recorded]
  G --> H[Underwriting audit]
  H --> I[Portfolio monitoring:<br/>rate, mix, aggregates]
  I --> B
Control Purpose Typical weakness
Business plan (SBF) Premium, classes, loss ratios approved by Lloyd's Plan not cascaded into team targets
Underwriting authority letters What each underwriter may write: classes, max line, territories, exclusions Not checked systematically at bind
Line guide Maximum line by class and risk quality Exceptions not recorded
Pricing guidelines / models Technical price Spreadsheet models with no version control
Referrals Risks outside authority go to senior underwriters Informal referrals by email
Peer review Second review of samples or high-risk decisions Done after binding; little feedback loop
Exposure management Aggregates, PML, RDS, cat model outputs Monthly updates; no real-time view at bind
Sanctions and licensing checks Legal permission to write and pay Manual screening; not repeated at claims
Underwriting audit Evidence that process was followed Findings repeat year after year

9.2 Building a technical price – worked example

Component Amount
Expected loss (from experience and exposure rating, including cat load) £600,000
Cost of capital: capital allocated £1.5m × 12% £180,000
Acquisition cost (brokerage) 25% of premium
Internal expenses 8% of premium
Technical price = (600,000 + 180,000) ÷ (1 − 0.25 − 0.08) ≈ £1,164,000

If the market price is £1,050,000, rate adequacy = 1,050 ÷ 1,164 ≈ 90% – the risk is about 10% underpriced against the target return.

9.3 Risk-adjusted rate change (RARC) – worked example

Item Expiring Renewal
Premium £1,000,000 £1,050,000
Exposure (e.g., insured values) 100 110
Deductible change effect on expected loss — −3%
  1. Adjust expiring premium for exposure growth: £1,000,000 × 110/100 = £1,100,000.
  2. Adjust for the cover change (higher deductible lowers expected loss 3%): £1,100,000 × 0.97 = £1,067,000.
  3. RARC = £1,050,000 ÷ £1,067,000 − 1 ≈ −1.6%.

The headline premium rose 5%, but on a like-for-like basis the price fell 1.6%. This is why Lloyd's and underwriters track risk-adjusted rate change. Lloyd's reported −6.7% for H1 2026 certain.

9.4 Pricing and portfolio vocabulary

Term Meaning
Burning cost Historical losses ÷ exposure, adjusted for trend and development
Exposure rating Price using exposure and benchmark loss curves
Experience rating Price using the client's own loss history
Hit ratio Bound ÷ quoted
Retention ratio Renewals retained ÷ renewals offered
Rate adequacy Actual price ÷ technical price
PML Probable maximum loss from an event
RDS Lloyd's Realistic Disaster Scenarios
Line size Share written on a risk

9.5 The underwriting workbench

A modern underwriting workbench brings together: submission intake, triage and prioritisation, risk data enrichment, pricing tools, referral workflow, authority checks, decision record, and links to the policy system and exposure tools. likely Many London firms have workbench projects; value depends on integration and adoption by underwriters, not on features.

10. Cycle management – underwriting in a soft market

Action What good looks like
Know your technical price Every risk priced against a technical price; rate adequacy reported monthly
Separate rate from exposure and cover changes RARC tracked by class and channel
Hold terms, not just price Deductibles, exclusions, sub-limits monitored as carefully as rate
Manage follow and facility share Caps and performance triggers
Walk away Underwriters rewarded for profitable portfolio, not premium volume
Reduce cost Expense ratio improvements protect margin when price cannot
Use capital wisely Buy cheaper reinsurance where it improves return on capital

The PRA's 2026 priorities letter warns that softening markets may cause internal models to overstate profitability and understate capital likely. Expect regulators and Lloyd's to challenge 2027 plans that assume rate adequacy holds.

11. Endorsements, cancellations and renewals

Process Detail Common issue
Endorsement Prepared by broker in MRC endorsement format; agreed per GUA; processed with additional or return premium Agreed by the wrong parties; processed late
Cancellation Under the cancellation clause; pro-rata or short-period return premium Disputes about notice dates
Extension Short extension while renewal terms are agreed Repeated extensions hide late placement
Adjustable premium Deposit premium adjusted on actual exposure at expiry Adjustments never collected
Renewal New contract; fresh fair presentation "As expiring" terms copied without review

12. Data and systems in placement and underwriting

System Owner Purpose
Broker placing system Broker Client data, submissions, MRC, accounting
Electronic placing platform Market/vendor Shared contract record, lines, audit trail
Underwriting workbench Insurer Intake, triage, pricing, referral, decision record
Pricing and cat models Insurer Technical price, PML, aggregates
Policy administration system Insurer Contract record, lines, endorsements, premium
Bureau systems (Velonetic) Market Premium processing, signing, settlement

Key data points that must match across systems: UMR, insured, period, written and signed line, premium (gross, net, currency), brokerage, tax, class/risk code, territory, deductibles and limits, layer, endorsements.

13. KPIs for placement and underwriting

KPI Why
Time from submission to first review Underwriter capacity
Quote turnaround time Competitiveness
Hit ratio / retention ratio Market position
Rate adequacy and RARC by class and channel Profitability
Lead share (% of premium led) Market standing
% contracts certain at inception Control
Referrals outside authority / breaches Control
Bureau query rate Data quality
Endorsement cycle time Subscription friction

14. Consulting opportunities

Problem Symptoms Solution
Slow quote turnaround Underwriters re-key submission data Submission ingestion and triage; workbench
Weak pricing discipline Wide variation in rate adequacy Pricing tools, rate dashboards, governance
Contract certainty failures Disputes, late documentation Automated MRC checks, wording comparison
High cost per risk Many manual touches; duplicate keying Process redesign; integration with platforms
Poor follow performance Facility business worse in soft market Channel analytics; facility renegotiation
Weak exposure visibility Aggregates calculated monthly in spreadsheets Exposure platform; data quality at intake

15. Mini case – the slow property team

Situation: a property team at a managing agent receives 300 submissions a week, quotes 30%, binds 10%. Quote turnaround averages 6 days; brokers complain. Underwriting assistants spend most of their time keying SOV data into models.

Diagnosis questions:

  1. How much time per submission goes on data entry vs analysis?
  2. What share of declines could be made in under 10 minutes with basic appetite rules?
  3. What is the rate adequacy and loss ratio of risks bound with turnaround under 3 days vs over 6 days?

Likely answer: fast declines plus automated SOV extraction cut turnaround in half; underwriters spend more time on the top 30% of submissions; hit ratio on preferred risks rises.

16. Partner's lens

Top-tier strategy consulting methods applied to this chapter.

  1. Find where value is created, not where work is done. In placement, most effort is administrative, but most value is in two decisions: which risks to write and at what price. A partner sizes both before proposing process automation. A 2-point improvement in rate adequacy on £300m of property premium is about £6m a year – usually far more than the cost saving from faster processing.
  2. Separate lead economics from follow economics. Ask every client for performance split by lead, follow and facility. If they cannot produce it, that is the first finding.
  3. Treat time-to-quote as a revenue lever, not an efficiency metric. Faster quotes on preferred risks win better business. Frame workbench and AI projects as selection improvement, then cost reduction.
  4. Trace one contract end to end. In week one, take five real UMRs and trace them from submission to bureau signing to the policy system. Count re-keying points and mismatches. This one exercise usually produces the most persuasive slide in the diagnostic.
  5. Use the cycle. In a soft market, the CEO's question is "how do we avoid writing tomorrow's losses?" Position underwriting work as cycle management – rate adequacy, terms discipline, facility caps – not as "pricing transformation".

17. Key takeaways

  1. Placement creates the contract, the price and the first data; most downstream problems start here.
  2. The MRC's six sections are the backbone of the London contract; the UMR is the key that links everything.
  3. Each written line is a binding contract; signed lines may be reduced; liability is several.
  4. The GUA lets leaders agree changes for followers; facilities let followers deploy capacity cheaply – at a risk.
  5. Underwriting value comes from selection and price; measure rate adequacy and RARC by class and channel.
  6. In 2026's soft market, cycle management is the main underwriting agenda.

18. Check your understanding

# Question Answer
1 Name the six MRC sections. Risk Details; Information; Security Details; Subscription Agreement; Fiscal and Regulatory; Broker Remuneration and Deductions.
2 Written lines total 125%. A 30% written line signs to? 24%.
3 When is an insurer bound on an open market placement? When it writes its line (subject to any conditions) – each line is a separate contract.
4 Expected loss £400k, capital charge £100k, acquisition 20%, expenses 10%. Technical price? 500 ÷ 0.70 ≈ £714k.
5 Premium up 4%, exposure up 10%, no cover change. RARC? 1.04 ÷ 1.10 − 1 ≈ −5.5%.
6 Why can facility business perform worse than led business at the same rate? Adverse selection – followers take risks they did not select, sometimes without risk-level data.
7 What does the GUA do? Sets which changes the leader can agree alone and which need other agreement parties.

19. Practice tasks

  1. Draft a fictional MRC Risk Details section for a marine cargo open cover; critique it against section 4.
  2. Calculate technical price, rate adequacy and RARC for three fictional renewals.
  3. Trace the data flow of one contract across five systems; list every point where the same data is entered again.
  4. Interview an underwriter or broker about how facilities have changed their work (guide in Module 19 §3).

Module 05

Delegated Authority: Coverholders, MGAs and Bordereaux
Level Intermediate
Path phase Phase 2 – and your recommended secondary spike (Module 18 §3)
Time 4–5 hours reading, 3 hours exercises
Prerequisites Modules 01, 02, 04
You will be able to Explain how delegated authority (DA) works contractually, operationally and financially; read a binding authority; design a bordereaux process and a risk-based oversight framework; size the value of DA improvement; advise MGAs and carriers

1. Why this chapter matters

About 45% of Lloyd's premium comes through delegated authority likely. Lloyd's own DA pages cite around $26bn of annual premium and about 412 service companies likely. DA is where London reaches small and medium risks worldwide – and where most of the market's operational, data and conduct problems sit. If you want one area where a consultant can deliver measurable value quickly, this is it.

2. What delegated authority is

An insurer (a managing agent for its syndicates, or a company) gives a third party the authority to underwrite and bind risks – and sometimes to handle claims – on its behalf, within agreed limits.

2.1 Why insurers use it

Reason Explanation
Reach Access local, small and medium risks (US E&S property, Canadian commercial, European SME, A&H) that cannot economically be placed one by one in London
Expertise MGAs often have specialist underwriting talent and local knowledge
Scale at low fixed cost Premium grows without growing underwriting headcount
Speed for the customer Local binding in minutes, local documents, local claims service

2.2 Why it is risky

Risk Explanation
Control The insurer carries the risk but does not see each decision when made
Data Information arrives later (bordereaux), often incomplete
Conduct Mis-selling, unfair claims handling or sanctions breaches by the coverholder are the insurer's problem
Incentives Coverholder paid mainly on premium volume; insurer bears the losses
Credit Premium collected by the coverholder may not reach the insurer

3. The DA chain and its contracts

flowchart LR
  I[Insured] --> RB[Retail broker / agent]
  RB --> CH[Coverholder / MGA<br/>binds locally]
  CH -- binding authority placed by --> LB[London broker]
  LB --> MA[Managing agents / insurers<br/>lead + followers on the binder]
  CH -- bordereaux --> LB
  CH -- bordereaux --> MA
  CH -- premium net of commission --> LB --> MA
  I -- claim --> DCA[Coverholder or DCA / TPA]
  DCA -- claims bordereaux --> MA
  MA -- loss fund / claim payments --> DCA
Contract Between Purpose
Binding authority agreement (BAA) – placed on an MRC binding authority format Insurers and coverholder (placed by a London broker) Grants authority and sets its limits
Lloyd's coverholder approval Lloyd's and coverholder Permission to act for Lloyd's syndicates
TOBA London broker and insurers / coverholder Premium handling, documentation, service terms
Claims administration agreement Insurers and DCA/TPA Claims authority, loss funds, reporting
Letter of authority Managing agent and coverholder Lloyd's has issued guidance on DA agreements and letters of authority in 2026 likely
Certificates / policies Coverholder (for insurers) and insureds The individual insurance contracts bound under the BAA

Key point: the BAA is itself placed like any London risk – with a UMR, a lead and followers. Many binders are co-led (several managing agents share leadership); Lloyd's now requires claims arrangements for co-lead binders to be agreed using a model clause likely.

4. Types of delegated arrangements

Type Authority Typical use
Full binding authority Coverholder underwrites, binds and issues documents within limits Most DA business
Prior submit Coverholder must get insurer approval before binding each risk New or higher-risk coverholders; larger risks
Claims authority Coverholder or DCA/TPA settles claims up to a limit Volume claims, local service
Service company Group-owned coverholder of a managing agent Local platforms (e.g., in Asia, Europe)
Multi-carrier MGA MGA with binders from several carriers Specialist MGAs in US, UK, Europe
Fronted MGA programme Licensed fronting insurer issues policies; most risk reinsured to London and other reinsurers US programme business
Lloyd's Europe coverholder Binds EEA risks for Lloyd's Europe EU distribution
Lineslip / consortium London leader binds for followers Usually counted as open market, not DA

5. The DA lifecycle in depth

Stage What happens Key controls Common failures Metric
1. Strategy and appetite Managing agent decides which classes, territories and partners to pursue through DA DA strategy in business plan; risk appetite limits Growth targets without capacity to oversee DA premium vs plan
2. Due diligence Review the prospective coverholder: ownership, financials, underwriting experience, systems, data capability, compliance, key staff, sanctions, conduct, business continuity Due diligence standards; risk rating Box-ticking; no data capability test Days to complete; findings
3. Approval Lloyd's approval of coverholder (via Lloyd's DA systems such as ATLAS likely); Lloyd's Europe approval for EEA business Lloyd's requirements Delays; incomplete applications Approval cycle time
4. Contract placement BAA placed with lead and followers using standard LMA binding authority wordings; defines all limits Standard wordings; legal review; registration with Lloyd's before inception likely Non-standard terms; registration after inception % registered pre-inception
5. Binding Coverholder binds risks, issues certificates or policies Authority limits in coverholder systems; referral rules Risks outside territory or class; exceeding GPI Breaches per month
6. Reporting Risk, premium and claims bordereaux sent (usually monthly) Reporting standards; deadlines in BAA Late, incomplete, inconsistent % on time; data quality score
7. Premium remittance Coverholder collects premium, deducts commission, pays London broker, who pays insurers Settlement terms in BAA; credit control Late remittance; unreconciled cash Aged debt > 90 days
8. Claims Coverholder or DCA handles claims within authority; larger claims referred Claims authority levels; loss fund controls; claims audits Leakage; poor reserving; late claims data Claims cycle time; leakage
9. Oversight Monitoring, audits, breach management, reporting to the board Risk-based oversight framework Same effort for all coverholders regardless of risk Oversight cost per £m premium
10. Renewal or exit Review performance; renew, change terms or terminate; run-off plan Renewal review standard; exit plans Renewing poor performers to keep premium Loss ratio of renewed vs exited binders

6. The binding authority agreement – key terms

Term What it defines Why it matters
Classes and covers What the coverholder can write Prevents writing outside appetite
Territories Where risks may be located Licensing, tax, cat exposure
Maximum limits per risk Largest sum insured or limit Controls severity
Gross premium income (GPI) limit Maximum premium in the period Controls volume; breach is a key monitoring trigger
Rates and rating guidelines Minimum rates, rating tools Price discipline
Policy wordings Approved wordings and clauses Contract certainty; conduct
Commission Coverholder's commission; sometimes profit commission Incentives; acquisition cost
Premium remittance terms When and how premium is paid Credit risk; cash flow
Reporting requirements Bordereaux content, format and deadlines Data quality
Claims authority Settlement limits; referral; loss funds; DCA appointment Claims control
Referral conditions Risks needing insurer approval Control of unusual risks
Sanctions, data protection, financial crime Compliance obligations Legal risk
Audit and inspection rights Insurer access to records Oversight
Sub-delegation Whether coverholder can delegate further Lloyd's expects managing agents to prevent unapproved sub-delegation likely
Cancellation and run-off Notice; responsibilities after termination Exit risk

The LMA reports a project revising 400+ binding authority wordings, with final wordings and a data model planned in 2026 likely.

7. Bordereaux in depth

7.1 Types and content

Bordereau Typical fields Used for
Risk Certificate/policy number, insured, address and geocode, occupancy/class, inception and expiry, sum insured/limit, deductible, premium, tax, coverholder reference, UMR of binder Exposure management, aggregates, pricing review, regulatory and tax reporting
Premium Gross premium, commission, taxes, net due, instalments, currency, payment status Accounting, credit control
Claims Claim reference, policy reference, date of loss, cause, paid, outstanding, recoveries, status, cat code Reserving, claims oversight, reinsurance recoveries

7.2 Example risk bordereau row (fictional)

Binder UMR: B9999XYZ2026010 | Cert: FLH-26-004512 | Insured: J Smith Holdings LLC
Address: 120 Bay St, Tampa FL 33602 | Lat/Long: 27.9475,-82.4584 | Occupancy: Retail
Construction: Masonry | Year built: 1998 | TIV: USD 4,200,000 | Deductible: 2% wind
Inception: 2026-06-01 | Expiry: 2027-06-01 | Gross premium: USD 38,400
Commission: 25% | Surplus lines tax (FL): 4.94% | Coverholder ref: CH-0457

Checks a validation engine should run: territory allowed under the BAA? TIV under maximum per risk? Wind deductible meets minimum? Rate above minimum? Address geocoded? Tax rate correct for state? Cumulative GPI within limit? Sanctions screening on insured?

7.3 Standards

Standard Status
Lloyd's Coverholder Reporting Standards Define required fields for risk, premium and claims bordereaux likely
Delegated authority Core Data Record Market consultation in 2026 on a DA data standard likely
LMA binding authority data model Part of the wording revision project likely

7.4 Why bordereaux are a chronic problem

Problem Business impact
Late arrival (30–60+ days after month end) Exposure and reserves always behind reality; cat aggregation understated
Many formats (each coverholder different) Heavy manual mapping
Missing or wrong fields Cat model inaccuracy; tax and regulatory errors
No reconciliation to cash Premium booked but not received
Thin claims detail Poor reserving; missed reinsurance recoveries
Each carrier on a binder processes the same data Market-wide duplicated cost

7.5 Target-state bordereaux process

flowchart LR
  A[Coverholder system] --> B[File or API]
  B --> C[Ingestion and mapping<br/>AI-assisted]
  C --> D[Validation against BAA,<br/>sanctions, tax, data rules]
  D --> E{Exceptions?}
  E -- Yes --> F[Exception workflow:<br/>query drafted, SLA tracked]
  F --> B
  E -- No --> G[Data platform -<br/>single DA data store]
  G --> H[Finance postings and<br/>premium reconciliation]
  G --> I[Exposure and cat models]
  G --> J[Claims and reserving]
  G --> K[Oversight MI and<br/>audit triggers]

Design principles:

  1. One standard internal data model – map every coverholder to it once.
  2. Validate at ingestion against the BAA, not weeks later.
  3. Treat exceptions as a workflow with owners and service levels.
  4. Feed everything from one store – finance, exposure, claims, oversight.
  5. Share across carriers on a binder where possible (lead processes, followers consume) to remove duplication.

8. Oversight of coverholders

8.1 What Lloyd's expects likely

  • Managing agents design and operate oversight so delegated business stays within appetite and complies with requirements, with regular board reporting.
  • All binding authorities, coverholder appointments and service company agreements are registered before inception.
  • Key staff should be employed directly by the coverholder; managing agents prevent unapproved sub-delegation.
  • Coordinated audits where several managing agents share a lead binder (guidance issued May 2026).
  • Retrospective endorsements (backdated changes) may be reviewed by Lloyd's.

8.2 Risk-based tiering – worked model

Factor Weight Coverholder A score (1 low risk – 5 high risk)
Premium size 20% 4
Class risk (e.g., cat-exposed property, liability) 20% 5
Loss ratio vs plan 20% 3
Data quality and timeliness 15% 4
Audit findings open 15% 2
Conduct exposure (retail/SME customers) 10% 3
Weighted score 3.7 → Tier 1 (high)
Tier Oversight intensity
Tier 1 (high) Monthly performance review; annual full audit; quarterly data quality review; senior relationship owner
Tier 2 (medium) Quarterly review; audit every 18–24 months or risk-triggered
Tier 3 (low) Semi-annual review; desk-based audit; automated monitoring with triggers

Why this matters: many managing agents spread oversight effort evenly. Risk-based tiering moves effort to where risk is and is consistent with Lloyd's direction towards risk-based oversight likely.

8.3 Monitoring KPIs

KPI Trigger example
Bordereaux on time < 90% on time for 2 months → escalate
Data quality score < 95% valid mandatory fields → remediation plan
GPI utilisation > 90% of limit → review before further binding
Loss ratio vs plan > plan + 10 points → pricing review
Aged premium > 90 days > 5% of premium → credit action
Breaches of authority Any material breach → immediate review
Complaints (retail/SME) Rising trend → conduct review
Claims within authority vs referred Unusual pattern → claims audit

8.4 Audit

Area What auditors check
Underwriting Risks within authority; rating guidelines followed; files complete
Documentation Approved wordings; certificates issued correctly
Premium handling Trust/client money; remittance timeliness
Claims Handling within authority; reserves; payments; files
Compliance Sanctions; financial crime; data protection; licensing; complaints
Systems and data Data security; business continuity; data accuracy
Conduct Product governance and customer outcomes where retail/SME customers are involved

8.5 Conduct after FCA PS25/21

Since December 2025, larger commercial customers (above DISP SME thresholds) and contracts of large risks are outside ICOBS, PROD and the Consumer Duty; consumers and SMEs remain in scope certain. Co-manufacturers can nominate a single lead for PROD 4 duties, but an intermediary such as an MGA cannot be the lead certain. Practical issue: bordereaux often do not capture customer size, so firms cannot easily prove which risks are in or out of scope.

9. Claims under delegated authority

Topic Detail
Who handles Coverholder (if granted claims authority) or a DCA/TPA appointed by the insurers
Authority levels None (all referred); limited settlement authority; full authority within a limit
Loss funds Money advanced to the claims handler to pay claims locally; must be reconciled
Claims bordereaux Monthly reporting of claims movements
Co-lead binders Claims lead arrangements must be agreed; default rules apply until agreed likely
Cat events Claims volume surges; DCA capacity and oversight become critical
Common issues Late claims data; reserves not updated; leakage; missed reinsurance recoveries

See Module 06 Part B.

10. MGAs and market structure

Theme Detail
Growth MGAs have grown strongly in the US, UK and Europe as capital seeks distribution and specialist talent likely
Capacity models Lloyd's binders; company market binders; fronting carrier plus reinsurance; multi-year capacity deals
Capacity risk If carriers withdraw at renewal, an MGA can lose its business overnight – multi-carrier panels and longer capacity commitments reduce this
Incentive alignment Profit commission, retained risk ("skin in the game") and multi-year terms align interests
Regulation FCA authorisation in UK; PS25/21 co-manufacturing rules; Lloyd's coverholder standards
Valuations MGAs attract private equity because they earn fee income without holding capital likely

11. DA economics – worked example

A £50m gross premium US property binder:

Line Carrier view (£m) Coverholder view (£m)
Gross premium 50.0 50.0 bound
Coverholder commission (25%) (12.5) 12.5 income
London broker brokerage on binder (5%) (2.5) —
Net premium to carrier 35.0 —
Claims (loss ratio 55% of gross) (27.5) —
Carrier internal costs including DA oversight (4% of gross) (2.0) —
Reinsurance cost (allocated) (3.0) —
Carrier underwriting result 2.5 (5% of gross) —
Profit commission to coverholder (e.g., 15% of profit above threshold) possibly (0.4) possibly 0.4
Coverholder operating costs — (8.0)
Coverholder profit — ≈ 4.5–4.9

Insights:

  • Acquisition costs take 30% of premium before any claims. The carrier's margin is thin and sensitive: a 5-point rise in loss ratio wipes out the profit.
  • The coverholder earns a stable margin regardless of loss ratio (except through profit commission).
  • Levers for the carrier: better selection of coverholders; profit-linked commission; lower oversight and processing cost per £m premium; faster data for exposure management.

12. Data and technology in DA

Capability Purpose
Coverholder management Approvals, contracts, due diligence, audit records
Contract register BAA terms in structured form (limits, territories, GPI) – needed for automated validation
Bordereaux ingestion and validation Mapping, rules, exceptions
DA data store Single source of DA risk, premium and claims data
Finance integration Premium postings, cash matching
Exposure integration Feed cat models with validated locations
Oversight MI Tiering, KPIs, triggers, board reporting
AI Mapping unknown layouts; anomaly detection; drafting queries; analysing coverholder performance (Module 09)

Most common gap: the BAA terms are in PDF only. Without a structured contract register, no system can check risks against authority automatically. Fix this first.

13. Consulting opportunities

Opportunity Value lever Typical size
Bordereaux automation and DA data platform Operations cost; faster exposure data 0.1–0.3 points of expense ratio for a large DA book guessing
Risk-based oversight redesign Oversight cost; regulator and Lloyd's confidence Fewer audits; better focus
Coverholder performance analytics Loss ratio through better partner selection and exits Often the largest prize – 1+ point of loss ratio on DA books guessing
Commission and incentive redesign Acquisition cost; alignment Contract renewal cycle
DA data standard readiness Data quality; future market alignment Foundation
MGA launch / capacity strategy Growth for MGA; distribution for carrier Transaction-based
Claims oversight (DCA/TPA) Leakage; reserving Claims spend

14. Mini case – the late Florida bordereaux

Situation: a Florida coverholder writing homeowners' business for three syndicates has not sent risk bordereaux for three months. Hurricane season starts in four weeks.

Step Action
Immediate Lead managing agent formally requests data within 10 days; estimate exposure from last data plus growth trend; inform exposure management and reinsurance teams
Short term Coordinated audit; restrict authority (e.g., no new coastal business) until data received; check premium remittance
Structural Automated bordereaux tracking with triggers; late data raises the coverholder's risk tier; BAA renewal adds data and API requirements

Partner question: "How many of your coverholders could be in this position today without you knowing?"

15. Partner's lens

Top-tier strategy consulting methods applied to this chapter.

  1. Selection beats processing. The biggest value in DA is usually which coverholders you work with and on what terms, not how fast you process their bordereaux. Start every DA diagnostic with a coverholder profitability league table (loss ratio, growth, data quality, oversight cost). The bottom 10% of coverholders often generate most of the losses and most of the oversight effort.
  2. Follow the incentives. Commission paid on premium encourages volume. Ask: what share of commission is linked to profit, and what share of coverholders have retained risk? Incentive redesign is a lever most clients have not used.
  3. Make the contract machine-readable. Without structured BAA terms, no automation or AI can check authority. Position the contract register as the first, unglamorous step that unlocks everything else.
  4. Price oversight as a cost of goods. Express oversight and processing cost per £m of DA premium by coverholder. Executives immediately see which partners are too expensive to manage.
  5. Think market, not firm. Each binder may have several carriers processing the same bordereaux. A partner asks: "Could the lead process once and followers consume?" That is a market-level idea that wins attention from Lloyd's, the LMA and CEOs.

16. Key takeaways

  1. DA is about 45% of Lloyd's premium and carries most of the market's operational, data and conduct risk.
  2. The BAA defines authority; it must be registered before inception and should be held in structured form.
  3. Bordereaux are late, inconsistent and duplicated – validation at ingestion against the BAA is the core fix.
  4. Oversight should be risk-based: tier coverholders and focus effort.
  5. DA economics are thin for carriers: acquisition costs of around 30% leave little room for loss ratio slippage.
  6. The biggest value lever is partner selection and incentives; processing efficiency comes second.

17. Check your understanding

# Question Answer
1 Whose agent is the coverholder when binding? The insurer's.
2 What must happen before a binder incepts at Lloyd's? It must be registered on Lloyd's systems.
3 Name the three bordereaux types. Risk, premium, claims.
4 Why is a GPI limit important? It caps the premium volume the coverholder can write; breaches signal loss of control.
5 What is a loss fund? Money advanced to a claims handler to pay claims locally.
6 After PS25/21, can an MGA be the lead co-manufacturer under PROD 4? No.
7 Why can't many firms automate authority checks? Binding authority terms are held only in PDF documents, not as structured data.

18. Practice tasks

  1. Build a structured contract register template for 10 BAA terms (section 6).
  2. Design validation rules for the example bordereau row in section 7.2.
  3. Score five fictional coverholders using the tiering model in section 8.2.
  4. Model the DA economics in section 11 in a spreadsheet; test loss ratios of 50%, 60% and 70%.
  5. Write a two-page POV: "Bordereaux are a data product, not a report" (Module 19 §4).
