Module 06

Accounting, Settlement, Claims and Reinsurance
Level Intermediate
Path phase Phase 2 (Module 18)
Time 5 hours reading, 3 hours exercises
Prerequisites Modules 01, 02, 04
You will be able to Trace premium and claims money end to end; explain bureau processing, signing and settlement; diagnose aged debt and reconciliation problems; explain how claims are agreed on subscription business under the Lloyd's Claims Lead Arrangements; run the outwards reinsurance cycle; size value in finance and claims operations

1. Why this chapter matters

CFOs and COOs judge consultants on whether they understand how money actually moves. In London, premium and claims money pass through brokers, a central bureau and many insurers, in many currencies, with many taxes. The cost of making this work – technical accounting, credit control, reconciliation, claims administration – is one of the largest controllable costs in a carrier. And claims is where the insurer's promise is tested and where leakage quietly erodes the loss ratio.

Part A – Accounting and settlement

A1. Why London accounting is complex

  1. One contract has many insurers, each with a different signed line.
  2. Premium flows client → retail broker → London broker → insurers, with deductions at each step.
  3. Instalments, adjustments, endorsements and return premiums create many transactions per contract.
  4. Many currencies and many tax regimes.
  5. Some insurers settle through the bureau; others directly.
  6. Written lines are often booked first and corrected later to signed lines.

A2. Bureau and non-bureau

Model How it works Who uses it
Bureau Broker submits premium and claims transactions to a central bureau (Velonetic), which validates, records ("signs") and settles them for all participating insurers All Lloyd's business; many company market insurers certain
Non-bureau (direct) Broker deals with each insurer directly – separate accounting messages and payments Company market insurers outside the bureau

Mixed placements (Lloyd's plus non-bureau company insurers) mean the broker processes the same contract twice in different ways – a structural source of cost and error.

A3. Premium flow step by step

flowchart TB
  A[Contract bound - MRC,<br/>signed lines] --> B[Broker prepares premium<br/>submission: gross, brokerage,<br/>tax, instalments, currency]
  B --> C[Submission to bureau<br/>with supporting documents]
  C --> D[Bureau validation against MRC,<br/>lines, coding, tax]
  D -- query --> B
  D --> E[Signing: SND allocated<br/>per insurer share]
  E --> F[Accounting entries to each<br/>syndicate / company]
  F --> G[Central settlement: broker pays<br/>net; bureau distributes]
  G --> H[Lloyd's: premium into<br/>premium trust funds]
  H --> I[Insurer reconciles bureau data<br/>to own ledger and policy system]

A4. Key terms

Term Meaning
Premium advice / LPAN Broker's premium submission to the bureau (historically "London Premium Advice Note") certain
Signing Number and Date (SND) Reference the bureau allocates to each processed transaction; the Original SND (OSND) links later transactions back to the original premium entry certain
De-linked processing Signing (recording) is separated from cash settlement, so processing is not held up by payment timing likely
Settlement Due Date (SDD) Date by which premium should be paid, set by the contract's premium payment terms
Premium payment clause / warranty Contract term setting payment terms and consequences of non-payment (e.g., insurer's right to cancel)
Terms of Trade (TOT) Credit periods agreed between brokers and insurers
Central settlement Net settlement between brokers and insurers via the bureau in main currencies likely
Premium trust funds (PTF) Trust funds holding Lloyd's syndicate premiums; US situs business goes to US trust funds (e.g., Lloyd's American Trust Fund) certain
Client money (CASS 5) Brokers hold premium and claims money under FCA client money rules
Risk transfer Agreement that money paid to the broker counts as paid to the insurer – protects clients if the broker fails
AP / RP Additional / return premium
Deposit and adjustment premium Paid up front; adjusted on actual exposure at expiry

A5. Taxes and charges

Tax Applies to
UK Insurance Premium Tax UK risks: standard rate 12%, higher rate 20% for certain products certain
US Federal Excise Tax Premiums to non-US insurers for US risks: 4% direct, 1% reinsurance certain
US surplus lines taxes State taxes paid by US surplus lines brokers
Overseas premium taxes and levies Vary by country; Lloyd's provides guidance through Crystal
Fire brigade charges, pool levies Specific countries

Data point: tax depends on risk location, insured location, class and sometimes the insurer's status. A data model must hold these separately; tax errors are common in DA business.

A6. Credit control and aged debt

Aged premium debt = premium past its settlement due date. It reduces cash and investment income, creates credit risk, and can affect capital.

Root cause Example Fix
Late documentation Contract not certain → premium not submitted Contract certainty discipline
Bureau queries Data errors rejected Validation before submission
Disputes Disagreement on premium or terms Faster query resolution
DA remittance delays Coverholder holds premium Remittance terms, monitoring
Client non-payment Retail broker or client delay Premium payment clause enforcement
Unallocated cash Cash received but not matched Automated cash matching

Worked example: a syndicate has £1.2bn annual premium and 14% of receivables over 90 days, equal to about £60m. Collecting £40m of that 60 days earlier at 4% investment yield adds about £0.26m a year in income – small – but it also removes credit risk and the operational cost of chasing. The bigger prize is usually the effort spent managing debt and the credit and capital effect, not interest.

A7. Reconciliation – the hidden cost

Insurers must reconcile three records of the same transactions:

  1. Their own policy and finance systems (what they think they wrote).
  2. Bureau records (what was signed and settled).
  3. Broker statements and cash (what was paid).

Example of a break:

Record Line Premium (USD)
Insurer policy system 12.5% (written) 125,000
Bureau signing 10.0% (signed) 100,000
Cash received 10.0% 98,500 (FX difference on GBP leg)

Three different numbers for one contract. Causes: written vs signed line, FX, timing. Multiply by tens of thousands of transactions and you have a large, often offshore, reconciliation team.

A8. What the technical accounting team does

Activity Description
Premium booking Record premium, instalments, adjustments
Signing reconciliation Match bureau signings to policy records
Cash allocation Match cash to premium and claims entries
Credit control Chase overdue premium; manage broker relationships
Claims accounting Record claims payments and recoveries
Reinsurance accounting Outwards premiums and recoveries
Month-end support Earned premium, unearned premium reserve, deferred acquisition costs, accruals

Consulting angle: technical accounting is often the largest operations team in a managing agent and the least automated. Reconciliation automation and validation before bureau submission are reliable, measurable savings.

Part B – Claims

B1. The claims process

flowchart TB
  A[Loss occurs] --> B[Insured notifies broker / DCA]
  B --> C[Broker notifies lead via market<br/>claims systems, linked to UMR]
  C --> D[Lead triages: standard or complex;<br/>checks coverage, sanctions]
  D --> E[Experts appointed: loss adjuster,<br/>lawyers, forensic accountants]
  E --> F[Reserve set; followers informed]
  F --> G{Agreed by claims<br/>agreement parties?}
  G -- Query / dispute --> H[Investigation, negotiation,<br/>coverage position]
  H --> G
  G -- Agreed --> I[Payment via bureau to broker<br/>to insured]
  I --> J[Recoveries: reinsurance,<br/>subrogation, salvage]
  J --> K[Claim closed; data to<br/>reserving and MI]

B2. Who agrees a claim – the Lloyd's Claims Lead Arrangements

The Lloyd's Claims Lead Arrangements (LCLA) replaced the earlier Lloyd's Claims Scheme (Combined) likely. In simple terms:

Element Rule likely – check the current LCLA text
Standard claims Handled and agreed by the Lloyd's lead managing agent alone; followers are bound
Complex claims Handled jointly by the Lloyd's lead and a second Lloyd's lead (the claims agreement parties)
Complexity thresholds (per Lloyd's share) First-party insurance £2m; third-party insurance £1m; non-proportional treaty reinsurance £5m – or other complexity criteria
Dynamic triage The lead re-triages a claim as standard when complex criteria no longer apply
Second lead identification The second lead should be clearly identifiable in the contract
Disputes A defined process applies if lead and second lead cannot agree, or if followers holding a defined share ask for it
Co-lead binders A claims lead agreement (model clause LMA9186) is required; default rules apply until agreed

Company market and mixed placements: company market insurers apply their own market claims agreement practices; on mixed placements, the MRC's subscription agreement section sets out who agrees claims for each part of the market likely.

Why it matters: clear claims agreement rules reduce cost and delay for followers and clients. Unclear agreement parties in the MRC cause disputes when a large claim arrives.

B3. Claims systems and records likely

System / term Purpose
ECF – Electronic Claims File Market electronic claims file for sharing documents and agreements
CLASS Lloyd's bureau claims system recording claims movements
IMR – Insurers' Market Repository Shared document repository
UCR / TR Unique Claim Reference; Transaction Reference for each movement
Insurer claims system Each insurer's own system for handling, reserving and payment

B4. Claims roles

Role Responsibility
Claims broker Notifies, presents and negotiates for the client; collects payment
Lead claims handler Triage, coverage, experts, reserves, agreement for followers
Second lead (complex claims) Joint agreement with lead
Follower claims handler Reviews and monitors; bound by agreed decisions
Loss adjuster Investigates and quantifies for insurers
Loss assessor Acts for the insured
DCA / TPA Handles delegated claims
Coverage lawyers Advise on policy response and disputes

B5. Claims vocabulary

Term Meaning
FNOL First notification of loss
Case reserve / outstanding Estimate of remaining cost on a known claim
Incurred Paid + outstanding
IBNR / IBNER Incurred but not reported / not enough reported (actuarial)
Ultimate Final expected cost
Coverage position / reservation of rights Insurer's position on policy response
Ex gratia Payment without admitting liability
Subrogation / salvage / contribution Recoveries and sharing of losses
Aggregation / event Whether losses count as one event (key in reinsurance)
Leakage Money paid that should not have been

B6. Reserving – the link to finance

Concept Meaning
Case reserves Set by claims handlers
IBNR Set by actuaries using methods such as chain ladder and Bornhuetter-Ferguson
Reserve adequacy Whether total reserves cover ultimate claims
Prior-year development Releases (favourable) or strengthening (adverse)
Statement of Actuarial Opinion Lloyd's requires an independent opinion on each syndicate's reserves certain

Consulting angle: inconsistent case reserving (some handlers reserve optimistically, others prudently) distorts IBNR and reserve releases. Reserve-setting guidelines and AI-supported file reviews improve consistency.

B7. Catastrophe and major loss events

Stage Activity
Event coding Assign event/catastrophe codes so losses aggregate across contracts and reinsurance
Initial estimates Syndicates estimate gross and net losses; report to Lloyd's on major loss returns likely
Surge Loss adjusters and DCAs scale up; triage separates simple from complex
Reinsurance Notify cat XoL reinsurers; calculate reinstatement premiums
Liquidity Fund large payments; possible cash calls
Wording issues Event definitions, hours clauses, exclusions

B8. Claims leakage – where money is lost

Source Example
Coverage Paying claims excluded by wording
Quantum Overpaying the amount of loss
Process Duplicate payments; late payments causing interest or damages (Enterprise Act 2016 certain)
Recoveries Missed reinsurance, subrogation or salvage
Expenses Uncontrolled adjuster and legal fees
Fraud Inflated or false claims, increasingly with AI-generated documents

Leakage is usually measured by file reviews – reviewing a sample of closed files against best practice and estimating overpayment.

B9. Claims KPIs

KPI Purpose
Time to acknowledge / first contact Service
Cycle time (notification to settlement) Efficiency and client experience
Reserve accuracy Financial reliability
Leakage rate Cost control
Litigation rate Dispute management
Expense per claim Efficiency
Recoveries achieved Net loss reduction
Broker and client satisfaction Franchise value – Lloyd's targets top-tier NPS likely

Part C – Reinsurance operations

C1. The outwards reinsurance cycle

flowchart LR
  A[Risk appetite and<br/>capital model] --> B[Programme design:<br/>quota share, XoL, cat, aggregate]
  B --> C[Placement via RI broker<br/>1 Jan, 1 Jun, 1 Jul]
  C --> D[Wording and security review]
  D --> E[Cessions: allocate inwards<br/>risks to treaties]
  E --> F[RI premium accounting]
  F --> G[Losses: identify recoverable<br/>claims; collections]
  G --> H[Disputes, commutations]
  H --> A

C2. Worked recovery – cat XoL with reinstatement

Programme: £80m xs £20m cat XoL; annual premium £8m (rate on line 10%); one reinstatement at 100% additional premium, pro rata to amount.

Event: hurricane causes £65m net loss to the syndicate.

Step Amount
Retention £20m kept by syndicate
Recovery £65m − £20m = £45m
Reinstatement premium £8m × 45/80 = £4.5m payable to reinsurers
Net benefit of recovery £45m − £4.5m = £40.5m
Remaining limit after reinstatement £80m restored for a second event

Common errors: claims not coded to the event; recoveries calculated on gross instead of net; reinstatement premium not accrued; recoveries filed late.

C3. Inwards reinsurance accounting

Inwards treaty business is usually accounted quarterly. The cedant (or broker) sends technical accounts showing premium, claims, commissions and balance due. Reinsurers book estimated premium income (EPI) at inception and adjust when actual accounts arrive. Late statements and differences between estimates and actuals are common reserving and reconciliation challenges.

C4. Reinsurance pain points

Process Pain point
Programme design Weak link between capital model and buying decisions
Cessions Inwards risks not allocated to the correct treaty
Recoveries Missed or late recoveries; manual matching of claims to treaties
Credit risk Reinsurer disputes or failure; collateral monitoring
Commutations Valuing future liabilities for a lump-sum settlement

Part D – End-to-end money trace

Premium: £1,000,000 gross; Syndicate A signed 40%, Company B signed 60%; brokerage 20%; no tax.

Step Amount
Client pays broker £1,000,000
Broker deducts brokerage £200,000
Net premium to insurers £800,000
Syndicate A (40%) → premium trust fund via bureau £320,000
Company B (60%) → via bureau or directly £480,000

Claim: £2,000,000 agreed.

Step Amount
Syndicate A pays 40% £800,000
Company B pays 60% £1,200,000
Paid through bureau → broker → client £2,000,000
Syndicate A recovers 30% under its quota share £240,000

If you can explain both tables to a CFO without notes, you understand the core of London market finance.

Part E – Consulting opportunities

Opportunity Value lever
Validation before bureau submission Fewer queries; faster settlement
Reconciliation automation Smaller teams; fewer write-offs
Cash matching and credit control analytics Lower aged debt and credit risk
Claims triage and segmentation Right effort per claim; faster cycle times
Leakage review and claims controls Loss ratio
Reserve consistency Reserving quality; fewer surprises
Reinsurance recovery automation Fewer missed recoveries
Cat claims playbook Surge readiness

Part F – Mini case: the growing aged debt

Situation: a managing agent's premium over 90 days has grown from 6% to 14% of receivables in 18 months. The CFO suspects brokers; the COO suspects the bureau.

Approach:

  1. Segment aged debt by broker, channel (open market, DA, facility), class, currency and root cause.
  2. Sample 50 items and trace each from contract to cash.
  3. Typical finding guessing: 40% DA remittance delays from a few coverholders; 25% bureau queries from data errors; 20% unallocated cash; 15% genuine client delays.
  4. Actions: coverholder remittance enforcement; pre-submission validation; automated cash matching; broker scorecards.

Part G – Partner's lens

Top-tier strategy consulting methods applied to this chapter.

  1. Follow the money and count the touches. Take ten real contracts and trace every premium and claims transaction through every system. Count manual touches per £1m of premium. This is the most persuasive single fact for an operations case.
  2. Separate value into four buckets for the CFO: cost (FTE, outsourcing), cash (aged debt), credit and capital (exposure to brokers, coverholders and reinsurers), and loss ratio (leakage, missed recoveries). CFOs trust analyses that keep these separate.
  3. Claims is a loss-ratio lever, not an operations function. A 2% leakage reduction on £500m of claims is £10m a year – often more than the entire claims operating budget. Frame claims work in loss-ratio points.
  4. Recoveries are found money. Missed reinsurance recoveries and subrogation are rarely measured. A recovery audit often pays for the whole engagement – a classic partner "quick win" that builds trust.
  5. Respect market rules. Many accounting and claims processes are defined by market agreements (bureau rules, LCLA). Solutions must work within them – or explicitly propose market change through the LMA and IUA.

Key takeaways

  1. All Lloyd's business goes through the bureau; the SND links every accounting entry.
  2. Written vs signed lines, FX and timing create three versions of every transaction; reconciliation is a major hidden cost.
  3. Aged debt has a few common root causes; fix them at source.
  4. Under the Lloyd's Claims Lead Arrangements, the lead agrees standard claims; lead and second lead agree complex claims; followers are bound.
  5. Leakage and missed recoveries are loss-ratio issues worth more than most operational savings.
  6. Reinsurance operations connect claims, finance and capital; errors mean lost recoveries.

Check your understanding

# Question Answer
1 What does the OSND do? Links later transactions (endorsements, claims) to the original premium entry.
2 Name three causes of aged premium debt. Late documentation, bureau queries, DA remittance delays, unallocated cash, client non-payment.
3 Who agrees a complex claim on a Lloyd's subscription contract? The Lloyd's lead and the second Lloyd's lead.
4 £100m xs £25m cat XoL, premium £10m, one reinstatement at 100%. Net loss £75m. Recovery and reinstatement premium? Recovery £50m; reinstatement premium £10m × 50/100 = £5m.
5 Why is reconciliation hard in London? Written vs signed lines, FX, timing, multiple insurers and systems, bureau and non-bureau processing.
6 Name four sources of claims leakage. Coverage, quantum, process (duplicates, delays), missed recoveries, expenses, fraud.

Practice tasks

  1. Trace the money for a fictional mixed Lloyd's/company placement with two instalments and one return premium.
  2. Build a three-way reconciliation table for five fictional transactions with different break causes.
  3. Calculate recoveries and reinstatement premiums for two events under one cat XoL.
  4. Design a claims triage model (standard vs complex) using the LCLA thresholds.

Module 07

Capital, Finance and Regulation
Level Advanced
Path phase Phase 2 (late) and Phase 3 (Module 18)
Time 5–6 hours reading, 3 hours exercises
Prerequisites Modules 01, 02, 04, 06
You will be able to Explain the Lloyd's annual venture, year of account and RITC; draw the chain of security; walk through capital setting (SBF, uSCR, ECA, FAL, Coming into Line); read Lloyd's and syndicate results; explain the main reporting bases; explain PRA, FCA and Lloyd's regulation in late 2026; link operational change to capital and return on capital

1. Why this chapter matters

CFOs, CROs and Lloyd's-facing executives decide the biggest questions: how much to grow, which classes to write, how much reinsurance to buy, whether to close a year of account, whether to launch a syndicate. All of these run through capital, financial reporting and regulation. A consultant who can connect an operational recommendation to capital and return on capital speaks at board level. One who cannot stays at the operations level.

Part A – Lloyd's financial architecture

A1. The annual venture and the year of account

certain Each syndicate underwrites as an annual venture. All policies incepting in a calendar year belong to that year of account (YOA). Members can change their participation each year, so each YOA can have different capital providers. Results are measured per YOA as well as per calendar year.

Worked example – how premium on the 2026 YOA is earned

A syndicate writes £120m of 12-month policies spread evenly through 2026.

Calendar year Premium earned from the 2026 YOA Why
2026 about £60m On average, policies have run half their term by 31 Dec 2026
2027 about £60m The remaining half of the cover
2028 — Claims still developing (especially liability)

So the 2026 YOA's result is only known after its policies have expired and claims have developed – which is why Lloyd's historically used three-year accounting.

A2. Closing a year: reinsurance to close (RITC)

timeline
  title Life of the 2026 year of account
  2026 : Policies written. YOA open.
  2027 : Remaining premium earned. Claims develop.
  2028 (31 Dec) : 36 months. Year normally closed by RITC.
  2029 : Profit distributed to members, or losses funded.
Concept Meaning
RITC certain At closure, the members of the closing YOA pay a premium to the members of a later YOA (usually the next YOA of the same syndicate, or another syndicate) to take over all remaining liabilities. The old members exit with a final result
Open year If liabilities are too uncertain to price an RITC, the YOA stays open. Members cannot exit; capital stays tied up
Third-party RITC A run-off specialist takes over the closing YOA's liabilities
Distribution / cash call Profit paid to members; losses funded from Funds at Lloyd's or new cash

Worked example – closing the 2023 YOA at end-2025

Item £m
Premiums (net) for 2023 YOA 500
Claims paid to date (220)
Expenses (160)
Investment return 25
RITC premium paid to 2024 YOA (best estimate of remaining claims + margin) (110)
Result for 2023 members 35 profit

The RITC premium is the critical number: too low and the 2024 members (who may be different people) subsidise 2023 members; too high and the reverse. This is a fairness and conflict issue that managing agents must govern carefully.

A3. The chain of security

flowchart TB
  L1[Link 1: Syndicate level assets<br/>Premium trust funds for that syndicate's claims]
  L2[Link 2: Members' Funds at Lloyd's - FAL<br/>Each member's capital, held in trust]
  L3[Link 3: Central assets<br/>Central Fund + Corporation assets<br/>+ subordinated debt; mutual]
  L1 --> L2 --> L3
Link Description
1. Syndicate assets Premiums and investment income in premium trust funds; first source for claims
2. Funds at Lloyd's Each member's capital (cash, securities, letters of credit), sized by Lloyd's; used if syndicate assets are insufficient
3. Central assets Mainly the Central Fund, funded by member contributions; available at the Council's discretion to meet valid claims if a member cannot pay. This mutualisation gives every Lloyd's policy the same financial strength rating

Ratings (2026): S&P AA- (Very Strong), Fitch AA- (Very Strong), Kroll Bond Rating Agency AA-, all stable; AM Best A+ (Superior), upgraded from A in August 2024 and affirmed in 2026 likely – these market ratings are the floor of security for every policy written at Lloyd's.

2025 figures: total capital, reserves and subordinated debt £49.8bn; central solvency ratio 496%; return on capital 22.0% likely. Skadden's summary refers to a $160bn chain of security likely.

A4. How capital is set

flowchart LR
  A[SBF: business plan<br/>for next YOA] --> B[Syndicate internal model:<br/>one-year SCR and ultimate SCR]
  B --> C[Lloyd's Capital Return]
  C --> D[Lloyd's review and challenge]
  D --> E[ECA = uSCR x 135%]
  E --> F[Member FAL = share of ECA,<br/>adjusted for diversification]
  F --> G[Coming into Line:<br/>FAL in place - usually late Nov]
  G --> H[Underwriting starts 1 January]
Term Meaning
SBF Syndicate Business Forecast – the plan for the next YOA: premium by class, loss ratios, expenses, reinsurance
One-year SCR Capital for a 1-in-200 loss over one year (Solvency UK)
uSCR Capital for a 1-in-200 loss to ultimate (full run-off of the business) – used for member capital
ECA certain Economic Capital Assessment = uSCR + 35% uplift; protects the Central Fund and Lloyd's rating
LCR Lloyd's Capital Return – the capital submission
Coming into Line (CIL) Deadline for members to provide FAL for the coming year certain
Lloyd's internal model Market-wide model approved by the PRA for the Society's central capital

Worked example – a corporate member's FAL

Syndicate Member's capacity share Syndicate uSCR as % of capacity Member's uSCR
Syndicate 1 £200m 45% £90m
Syndicate 2 £100m 60% £60m
Total (before diversification) £300m £150m
Diversification credit (illustrative 10%) (£15m)
Member uSCR £135m
ECA = 135% × £135m £182m FAL required

FAL as a percentage of capacity ≈ 61%. Consulting point: anything that lowers uSCR (better reinsurance, more diversified mix, lower reserve risk, fewer operational risk events) reduces FAL by 1.35 times that amount.

A5. Members' economics

Item Description
Profit or loss Paid or funded at YOA close
Managing agency fee Percentage of capacity paid to the managing agent
Profit commission Share of profit paid to the managing agent
Central Fund contributions and subscriptions Set annually by Lloyd's
Members' agent fees For Names
Capacity value Names trade syndicate participations at annual auctions

A6. Third-party capital routes

Route How it works
Corporate member Investor sets up a corporate member to back one or more syndicates
SPA Quota share of a host syndicate's business
Capital-backed syndicate E.g., Talbot syndicate 2478 backed by Blackstone (2025 capacity reported at £567m) likely
London Bridge Lloyd's protected cell company for ILS investors; London Bridge 2 opened 14 cells with about $660m of new capital in Q4 2025 likely
Names Individuals via limited liability vehicles; about 10% of market capital likely

Lloyd's 2026–2030 strategy aims to attract more sophisticated capital, simplify Names processes and tailor oversight to different capital providers likely.

Part B – Reading Lloyd's and syndicate results

Metric 2024 2025 H1 2026 Tag
GWP £55.5bn £57.9bn £34.7bn certain
Combined ratio 86.9% 87.6% 90.8% certain
Underlying (ex major claims) combined ratio 79.1% 81.8% 84.0% likely
Major claims ratio 7.8% 5.8% 6.8% likely
Prior-year releases benefit — 1.7 pts (£721m) 3.5 pts (analyst) likely
Risk-adjusted rate change — — −6.7% certain
Profit before tax — £10.6bn £3.5bn certain
Return on capital — 22.0% — likely
2026 forecast — GWP £64bn ±5%; CR 90–95%; 3% investment return — likely

How an advisor reads this likely:

  1. The underlying ratio has risen about 5 points in two years as rates fall.
  2. Headline results depend on benign catastrophe experience and reserve releases.
  3. Lloyd's own 2026 forecast (CR 90–95%) already expects deterioration.
  4. Implication for clients: 2027 plans that assume stable margins will be challenged by Lloyd's and the PRA.

Part C – Financial reporting bases

Basis Who Key features
Syndicate annual accounts Each syndicate UK GAAP (FRS 102 / FRS 103); technical and non-technical accounts; annual (calendar year) basis likely
Underwriting year (YOA) results Managing agents, members Results by YOA; drives distributions and RITC
Lloyd's aggregate results Society of Lloyd's Pro forma aggregation of syndicates plus Society accounts
IFRS 17 Listed groups owning managing agents Effective from 2023 certain
Solvency UK Syndicates, Lloyd's, companies Balance sheet on Solvency basis; QRTs, SFCR, RSR, ORSA

C1. IFRS 17 in plain English

Concept Meaning
Premium Allocation Approach (PAA) Simplified method used for most short-term P&C contracts – similar to unearned premium accounting
Liability for remaining coverage Like unearned premium
Liability for incurred claims Claims reserves, discounted for time value of money, plus an explicit risk adjustment
Onerous contracts Groups of contracts expected to be loss-making must recognise the loss immediately
Insurance revenue Replaces "gross written premium" as the main revenue measure

Practical challenge: the same syndicate result appears differently under UK GAAP (syndicate), YOA (members), IFRS 17 (group) and Solvency UK (regulator). Reconciling these bases is a recurring finance transformation task – and a source of confusion in board discussions. Always ask: "Which basis is this number on?"

Part D – Reserving and reserve risk

D1. Chain ladder – simple example

Year 12 months 24 months 36 months
2023 100 150 165
2024 120 180 ?
2025 110 ? ?

Development factors: 12→24 = (150 + 180) ÷ (100 + 120) = 1.50; 24→36 = 165 ÷ 150 = 1.10.

Year Projected ultimate Reported IBNR
2024 180 × 1.10 = 198 180 18
2025 110 × 1.50 × 1.10 = 181.5 110 71.5

D2. Key reserving issues in 2026

Issue Detail
Social inflation Rising US liability claims from litigation trends – the main reserve risk for US casualty certain
Reserve releases Supported results in 2025–2026; may reduce as soft-market years mature likely
Statement of Actuarial Opinion Independent opinion on each syndicate's reserves required by Lloyd's certain
Data quality Late DA claims data and inconsistent case reserving distort IBNR

Part E – Capital modelling for non-actuaries

E1. What drives capital

Risk type Examples How operations affect it
Premium risk Next year's claims worse than planned Pricing discipline; underwriting controls
Reserve risk Existing claims develop worse Reserving quality; claims handling
Catastrophe risk Hurricanes, earthquakes, cyber events Exposure data quality; aggregation control; reinsurance
Credit risk Reinsurers, brokers, coverholders fail to pay Credit control; reinsurer security; remittance terms
Market risk Investment losses, FX Asset management
Operational risk Process failures, fraud, systems, DA breaches, cyber Controls; automation; resilience

E2. Worked example – reinsurance and capital

A syndicate considers buying an extra £100m xs £150m cat layer costing £6m a year.

Without layer With layer
uSCR £800m uSCR £720m (cat risk falls)
ECA = £1,080m ECA = £972m
Capital released £108m
Cost of capital at 12% on £108m £13m a year saved
Reinsurance cost (£6m)
Expected recoveries (modelled) about £2m
Net benefit ≈ £9m a year

In a softening reinsurance market, this calculation favours buying more protection likely. This is "capital efficiency" in practice.

E3. The PRA's concern in 2026

The PRA warns that, as rates soften, internal models may overstate underwriting profitability and understate capital, and will engage more with firms whose assumed and actual profitability differ most likely. Practically: if the plan assumes a 92% combined ratio but actual business is trending to 96%, capital is understated.

Part F – Regulation in late 2026

F1. Three layers

flowchart TB
  PRA[PRA - prudential] --> LLO[Lloyd's - market oversight<br/>under Lloyd's Act 1982]
  FCA[FCA - conduct] --> LLO
  LLO --> MA[Managing agents and syndicates]
  PRA --> MA
  FCA --> MA

F2. PRA

Area Requirement / 2026 focus
Objectives Safety and soundness; policyholder protection; secondary objectives on competition and competitiveness and growth
Fundamental Rules Eight high-level rules (integrity, skill, prudent risk management, adequate resources, risk strategy, control, openness with regulators, orderly resolution) certain
Solvency UK Reforms from 31 Dec 2024: lower risk margin, reporting changes, internal model changes likely
Internal models Focus on overstated profitability in a softening market likely
DyGIST Dynamic General Insurance Stress Test – semi-live three-week crisis simulation in May 2026; results expected late 2026 likely
Operational resilience Important business services within impact tolerances (March 2025 deadline) certain
Outsourcing and third parties Critical outsourcing including cloud, bureau, TPAs, AI vendors
Solvent exit planning Plans for orderly exit likely
Climate SS5/25 (3 December 2025) replaced SS3/19: climate treated as a financial risk within underwriting, risk management, capital, ORSA and internal models; firms expected to complete a gap review by 3 June 2026 likely
AI Governance, model risk and accountability likely
SM&CR Named senior managers accountable

F3. FCA

Area Position
PS25/21 (9 Dec 2025) Larger commercial customers and contracts of large risks excluded from ICOBS, PROD and Consumer Duty; co-manufacturers can nominate a lead for PROD 4; bespoke contracts excluded from PROD 4; intermediaries cannot be lead co-manufacturer certain
Consumer Duty Applies to retail customers and SMEs below thresholds; FCA reviewing core definitions in 2026 likely
PROD, ICOBS, CASS 5 Product governance; conduct of business; client money
Financial crime and sanctions Screening, controls, reporting; Russia sanctions burden in marine, aviation, energy
Culture and non-financial misconduct Ongoing focus

F4. Lloyd's oversight

Tool Description
Business plan approval Most powerful lever – Lloyd's can cap growth, require remediation or increase capital loading
Principles-based oversight Managing agents assessed against principles; intensity tailored to maturity and performance likely
Minimum standards and requirements Claims, DA, reserving, capital, conduct
Market Bulletins Official instructions
2026 strategy commitments More predictable, risk-based oversight; streamlined reporting; lower cost of doing business likely

Part G – Lloyd's 2026–2030 strategy

Driver (Lloyd's wording) certain Meaning likely Consulting implication
Leading underwriting performance Attract top underwriters; reward lead capability; discipline on poor performers Portfolio management; lead/follow strategy
Efficient and flexible marketplace Lower cost of doing business; risk-based oversight; streamlined reporting; incremental modernisation and common data standards after Blueprint Two Cost-out; data standards; operating models
Maximised capital advantage Most capital-efficient marketplace for complex risk by 2031; return on capital >12% across the cycle; more institutional capital; simpler Names processes Capital modelling; reinsurance optimisation; third-party capital
A Lloyd's to be proud of Culture, talent, data, technology and AI strategies; AI to support human decisions Talent and AI capability

Part H – Run-off and legacy

Tool Description
Third-party RITC Closing YOA transferred to a run-off syndicate
Loss Portfolio Transfer (LPT) Reinsurer takes over existing reserves for a premium
Adverse Development Cover (ADC) Protection against reserves developing worse than expected
Part VII transfer Court-approved transfer of a book between UK entities
Commutation Lump-sum settlement of reinsurance obligations

Why it matters now likely: social inflation on older US casualty years and soft-market pressure push clients to clean up legacy and free capital.

Part I – Consulting opportunities

Opportunity Value lever
Capital efficiency review (reinsurance, mix, model) Capital released; return on capital
Plan challenge preparation (SBF) Lloyd's approval; realistic plans
Finance transformation (UK GAAP / IFRS 17 / Solvency UK alignment) Faster close; lower cost; better MI
Reserving process and data quality Fewer surprises; capital
Legacy strategy (RITC, LPT, ADC) Capital freed; open years closed
Regulatory programmes (DyGIST lessons, operational resilience, AI governance) Compliance; regulator confidence
Third-party capital structures Growth capacity

Part J – Mini case: freeing capital for growth

Situation: a managing agent wants to grow a profitable specialty class by £150m but its capital provider will not add FAL.

Options and effects:

Option Effect on capital Trade-off
Buy more cat reinsurance (cheap in 2026) Lower cat risk → lower uSCR Reinsurance cost
ADC on deteriorating casualty years Lower reserve risk → lower uSCR Premium cost; counterparty risk
Exit an unprofitable class Frees capital and improves loss ratio Lost premium and relationships
Bring in an SPA or third-party capital Adds capital Shares profit; governance
Improve exposure data quality Reduces modelled uncertainty in cat risk guessing Data investment

Partner answer: combine an ADC and an exit of the weakest class; the released ECA funds the specialty growth with a higher return on capital, and the story is stronger for the 2027 SBF.

Part K – Partner's lens

Top-tier strategy consulting methods applied to this chapter.

  1. Return on capital is the board's language. Translate every initiative into return on capital: profit change ÷ capital change. An initiative that adds £5m profit but needs £60m more capital may destroy value at a 12% cost of capital.
  2. Capital is set by models, and models are fed by data. Poor exposure data, late bordereaux and inconsistent reserving all add uncertainty, which can add capital. This link turns "data quality" from an IT topic into a CFO topic.
  3. Know the calendar. Lloyd's business timetable (plans, capital returns, Coming into Line) drives client decisions. A recommendation that misses the SBF window waits a year. Plan engagements backwards from these dates.
  4. Name the basis. In every board paper, label numbers as YOA, calendar year (UK GAAP), IFRS 17 or Solvency UK. Mixed bases are the most common source of confusion – and of lost credibility.
  5. Anticipate the regulator. In 2026 the PRA is testing whether plans and models are too optimistic. Advise clients to show conservative, evidence-based assumptions – it speeds approval rather than slowing it.

Key takeaways

  1. Lloyd's works in annual ventures; years of account close by RITC, usually at 36 months.
  2. The chain of security – syndicate assets, FAL, central assets – underpins every Lloyd's policy.
  3. Member capital = ECA = uSCR × 135%; lowering uSCR lowers capital by 1.35 times.
  4. Results look strong, but underlying ratios are rising as rates fall; plans and models face challenge.
  5. Different reporting bases (YOA, UK GAAP, IFRS 17, Solvency UK) must be named and reconciled.
  6. PRA, FCA and Lloyd's form three layers of oversight; 2026 priorities are cycle risk, resilience, AI and simpler conduct rules for large clients.

Check your understanding

# Question Answer
1 Why does an RITC price create a fairness issue? Members of the closing and receiving years may differ; a wrong price transfers value between them.
2 uSCR £400m. ECA? £540m.
3 Name the three links of the chain of security. Syndicate assets; Funds at Lloyd's; central assets.
4 What does IFRS 17 add to claims reserves compared with traditional accounting? Discounting and an explicit risk adjustment.
5 What is the PRA's main GI concern for 2026? Internal models overstating profitability and understating capital as markets soften.
6 A layer costs £5m and releases £50m of capital at 12% cost of capital, with £1m expected recoveries. Net benefit? £6m + £1m − £5m = £2m a year.

Practice tasks

  1. Calculate FAL for a fictional member on three syndicates.
  2. Model a reinsurance-for-capital trade-off for two layer options.
  3. Read one syndicate's annual accounts and identify: calendar-year combined ratio, YOA commentary, reserve releases and reinsurance strategy.
  4. Write a one-page board briefing: "What the PRA's 2026 priorities mean for our 2027 plan."

Module 08

Modernisation, Data Standards and Technology
Level Advanced
Path phase Phase 2–3; core to your recommended spike (Module 18 §3)
Time 4–5 hours reading, 3 hours exercises
Prerequisites Modules 04–06
You will be able to Explain why London modernisation has repeatedly struggled; explain the Blueprint Two story and where things stand after March 2026; describe the main data standards; design a target architecture and data strategy for a London carrier; run a vendor selection; advise on "what now" after Blueprint Two

1. Why this chapter matters

Technology and data spend is one of the largest discretionary costs for London carriers, and one of the most frequent consulting topics. It is also where consultants most often lose credibility: by pitching yesterday's market programme, by recommending tools without fixing data, or by ignoring the constraints of a shared market. This chapter gives you the history, the standards, the architecture and the current position so you can advise with authority.

2. Twenty-five years of modernisation – and the lessons

Period Initiative Outcome Lesson
2000–2006 Kinnect – Lloyd's electronic trading platform Abandoned after heavy spend likely Central platforms fail without benefits for each firm
2000s Project Rüschlikon – ACORD messaging for reinsurance accounting and claims Partial adoption by large reinsurers and brokers likely Standards succeed where big players gain directly
2004–2007 Contract certainty and the MRC Successful Regulator pressure plus a simple standard works
2016 PPL electronic placing Adopted, partly through Lloyd's mandates; later moved to a "Next Gen" platform after delays likely Adoption needs mandates and usability
2016–2019 London Market Target Operating Model (TOM) Folded into Lloyd's programme Market-wide governance is slow
2019–2020 Future at Lloyd's – Blueprint One and Two Ambitious digital processing vision; cutover moved repeatedly from 2024 into 2025 and 2026 likely Single cutover across hundreds of firms is very high risk
Mar 2026 Blueprint Two name and original vision retired; Velonetic refocused on incremental modernisation; common data standards retained; re-platforming not expected before 2028 likely Shift to incremental, standards-led change Standards and incremental delivery over "big bang"

2.1 Why market-wide change is so hard

Barrier Explanation
Collective action problem Everyone benefits if everyone changes, but each firm pays its own costs and gains only when others change too
Uneven costs and benefits Brokers often carry the cost of capturing structured data; carriers and the bureau gain most
Shared legacy Bureau systems are decades old and embedded in every firm's processes
Unstructured data at source Submissions and contracts are documents, so automation downstream is limited
Spread decision rights Lloyd's, LMA, IUA, LIIBA, LIMOSS, Velonetic, platforms and vendors all have a say
Always live The market cannot stop to migrate; old and new must run together
Long tail Contracts and claims from old years of account stay open for years – old data and processes must be supported

3. Blueprint Two – the full story

3.1 The original vision likely

Component Purpose
Core Data Record (CDR) Structured contract data captured at placement so accounting, settlement, tax and regulatory reporting can be automated
Digital Gateway Validate placement data against the CDR before downstream processing, for Lloyd's and company market business
Digital processing services New accounting, settlement and claims services replacing legacy bureau systems
Delegated authority data services Standardised DA contract and bordereaux data
Placement standards Lloyd's stopped building its own placement platform; set standards for third-party platforms

3.2 What happened

Date Event Tag
2020 Blueprint Two launched certain
2023–2024 Phase 1 cutover targeted for mid/late 2024 likely
2024–2025 Cutover pushed into 2025, then 2026 likely
March 2026 Lloyd's Council approves retiring the name and original vision after a review; CEO says expected benefits were not delivered likely
2026 Lloyd's strategy refers to transitioning away from Blueprint Two, refocusing Velonetic on incremental modernisation, open architecture and common data standards likely
2026 CDR continues; extensions (e.g., treaty reinsurance, delegated authority) consulted on guessing – confirm with ACORD/LMG
2028+ Bureau re-platforming not expected before 2028 likely

3.3 How to talk about it with clients

Client says Your position
"We spent millions getting ready – was it wasted?" "Not if it improved your own data. CDR-aligned data, clean master data and integration capability are still needed. What is at risk is spend tied only to the specific cutover."
"Should we pause modernisation until the market decides?" "No. Waiting costs more than the market's uncertainty. Focus on changes that pay back inside your firm within 18–24 months and stay standards-aligned."
"Will the bureau be replaced?" "Not quickly. Plan for incremental change through at least 2028 and integrate through standard messages and APIs."
"Who owns market modernisation now?" "Lloyd's sets direction, but the work is spread across Velonetic, LIMOSS, the LMA, IUA and LIIBA, platforms and standards bodies. Engage through the associations."

4. Data standards you must know

Standard / reference What it covers
MRC Structure of the contract (six sections)
CDR – Core Data Record Minimum structured data for processing: contract and parties, risk details, insurers and lines, premium and instalments, tax and regulatory, broker remuneration and deductions likely
ACORD GRLC messages Global Reinsurance and Large Commercial standards for placement, accounting (e.g., technical accounts), claims and documents
Lloyd's Coverholder Reporting Standards Bordereaux fields
Lloyd's risk codes Classification of business for reporting and capital
Clause references LMA/IUA/Institute clause numbers – enable wording analytics
Market references UMR (contract), UCR (claim), TR (claim transaction), SND/OSND (accounting entries)

Rule for data models: a London carrier's model must hold UMR, MRC sections, signed lines by insurer, CDR fields, risk codes, tax and regulatory data, and the links between premium entries (SND/OSND) and claims (UCR/TR).

5. Reference architecture for a London carrier

flowchart TB
  subgraph In[Inputs]
    E[Broker email and documents]
    P[Placing platforms]
    BX[Bordereaux]
    API[Broker / MGA APIs]

    ING[Document and data ingestion<br/>extraction, mapping, validation]
  end

    UWB[Underwriting workbench]
    PRC[Pricing models]
    PAS[Policy administration]
    EXP[Exposure and cat modelling]
  end

    ACC[Technical accounting]
    CLM[Claims]
    RI[Outwards reinsurance]
    GL[General ledger and reporting]
  end

    BUR[Bureau - Velonetic]
  end

    DP[Data platform]
    MI[MI, analytics, AI]
  end
  E --> ING
  BX --> ING
  API --> ING
  P --> UWB
  ING --> UWB
  UWB <--> PRC
  UWB --> PAS

5.1 Layer by layer

Layer Capabilities Build or buy (typical)
Ingestion Email and document capture; extraction (AI); mapping to standard model; validation; exception workflow Buy platform + configure; AI models increasingly commoditised
Underwriting workbench Triage, enrichment, pricing integration, referrals, authority checks, decision record Buy or build; differentiation in workflow and data
Pricing and rating Technical price models by class Build (actuarial IP) on standard tools
Policy administration Contract record, lines, endorsements, premium, financial postings Buy (specialty PAS)
Exposure and cat Aggregation, cat models, RDS Buy models; build aggregation views
Technical accounting Premium, claims and RI accounting; reconciliation; credit control Buy + automation
Claims FNOL, handling, reserving, payments, recoveries Buy
Outwards reinsurance Programme, cessions, recoveries Buy or specialist build
Finance and reporting GL, UK GAAP, IFRS 17, Solvency UK Buy
Data platform Golden records, history, data quality, lineage Cloud platform + build data products
MI and AI Dashboards, models, AI services, governance Build on platform

5.2 Vendors and tools likely – verify before naming in proposals

Capability Examples
Specialty policy administration Sequel Eclipse, DXC Genius / Assure, Guidewire, Duck Creek, Sapiens, Insurity
Underwriting workbench Send, hx Renew, Federato, Concirrus, Artificial Labs, in-house builds
Submission ingestion Cytora and other AI document platforms
Placing platforms PPL, Whitespace
Cat models Moody's RMS, Verisk, KCC, Cotality
Data platforms Snowflake, Databricks, Microsoft Fabric/Azure, AWS, Google Cloud
Finance SAP, Oracle, Workday, IFRS 17 engines

6. Common architecture problems and their root causes

Problem Root cause Effect
Several policy systems M&A without consolidation No single exposure view; high run cost
Data keyed 3–5 times No integration between platforms, PAS and bureau Errors, cost, delay
Spreadsheet pricing and exposure Under-investment; key-person dependence Weak audit trail; model risk
Bureau data not reconciled Different line bases, FX, timing Large reconciliation teams
Insurtech sprawl Point solutions bought by separate teams Many tools, little integrated value
Weak master data No owner for party, broker, coverholder, location records Duplicate records; sanctions risk; poor analytics
Old years on old systems Long-tail claims keep legacy alive Decommissioning blocked

7. Data strategy for a London carrier

7.1 Five data domains

Domain Golden record contains Typical owner
Party Insureds, brokers, coverholders, reinsurers – with identifiers (LEI, company numbers) and sanctions status COO / compliance
Contract UMR, MRC sections, lines, layers, wordings, clauses, dates CUO
Exposure Geocoded locations, values, vessels (IMO), aircraft, cyber footprint CRO / exposure management
Financial Premium, instalments, tax, brokerage, SND, settlement status, FX CFO
Claims UCR, event codes, reserves, payments, recoveries, documents Chief Claims Officer

7.2 Data quality – measure it like a business KPI

Dimension Example measure Target example
Completeness % of CDR mandatory fields populated at bind ≥ 98%
Accuracy % of sampled records matching source documents ≥ 97%
Timeliness % of bordereaux processed within 10 days of month end ≥ 90%
Consistency % of contracts where PAS, bureau and finance agree on line and premium ≥ 95%
Uniqueness Duplicate party records per 1,000 < 5
Validity % of locations geocoded to street level ≥ 90% (property)

7.3 Data governance – who does what

Role Responsibility
Data owner (business executive) Accountable for a domain's quality and use
Data steward (operations) Defines rules; fixes issues; monitors quality
Data custodian (IT/data team) Runs platforms; implements controls
Data council Prioritises issues; approves standards

7.4 Data products

A data product is a dataset managed like a product: a clear owner, defined users, quality targets, documentation and service levels. Example: "DA risk data product" – all bound DA risks, validated against binder terms, geocoded, refreshed within 10 days of bordereaux receipt, used by exposure management, finance and oversight. Treating bordereaux as a data product rather than a report is a strong POV for your spike.

8. Integration patterns

Pattern Use London example
APIs (real time) Interactive workflows Workbench requests enrichment data; MGA binds via carrier API
Standard messages (ACORD) Market exchanges Technical accounts, claims messages
Event-driven React to changes across systems "Risk bound" event updates exposure and finance
File/batch Large periodic loads Bordereaux, bureau extracts
Platform integration Pull structured contract data from placing platforms MRC data into PAS without re-keying

Principle: design for open architecture – integrate through standards so that market changes (bureau, platforms) do not require rebuilding internal systems. Lloyd's strategy now also refers to open architecture likely.

9. Cloud, resilience and third-party risk

Topic Requirement
Operational resilience Identify important business services (e.g., paying claims, binding cover) and stay within impact tolerances certain
Outsourcing and third parties PRA expectations on due diligence, contracts, exit plans for material outsourcing and cloud certain
Cyber security Protect client and market data; supplier security
Data protection Personal data in claims, A&H, DA bordereaux; international transfers
AI vendors Treat as material third parties where they support important services (Module 09)

10. Build vs buy and vendor selection

10.1 When to build and when to buy

Build when Buy when
The capability differentiates you (pricing models, underwriting insights) The capability is standard (accounting, policy admin, claims)
No product fits London specifics Mature London-specific products exist
You have engineering capacity to maintain it You want vendor-funded upgrades

10.2 Vendor selection method

Step Activity
1. Requirements Business capabilities and London specifics (subscription, lines, bureau, YOA, DA)
2. Long list → short list Market scan; RFI
3. Scripted demonstrations Vendors run your scenarios (e.g., a mixed Lloyd's/company placement with a signing down and an endorsement)
4. Fit-gap Score against requirements; estimate configuration and custom work
5. Reference checks Speak to London clients of the vendor
6. Total cost of ownership Licence, implementation, run, internal team, data migration – over 5–7 years
7. Risk assessment Vendor viability, delivery capacity, exit options

Example scoring weights: London functional fit 35%; technology and integration 20%; total cost 20%; vendor strength and delivery 15%; references 10%.

10.3 London-specific scenarios to script in demos

  • Placement with written lines above 100% and signing down.
  • Lloyd's and non-bureau company insurers on the same contract.
  • Endorsement requiring only the leader under the GUA, and one requiring all insurers.
  • Instalment premium with a return premium.
  • Binder with monthly bordereaux and a claims bordereau.
  • Claim on a complex subscription contract with lead and second lead.
  • Year-of-account and calendar-year reporting from the same data.

11. Designing a modernisation programme

Principle Explanation
Value first Sequence by value and dependency, not by system
Data foundation early Master data and the data platform unlock later use cases
Incremental releases Deliver value every quarter; avoid big-bang cutovers (the Blueprint Two lesson)
Plan for the long tail Decide how open years of account and old claims will be handled on new systems or retained platforms
Test with London scenarios Multi-insurer, multi-currency, signing down, endorsements, bureau messages
Change management Underwriters and operations must see benefit, or adoption fails
Decommission Budget and track legacy decommissioning to realise run-cost savings

12. No-regret moves after Blueprint Two likely

# Move Why it holds
1 Align the internal contract data model to the CDR Standards survived the March 2026 decision
2 Fix master data (party, broker, coverholder, location) Every analytics, AI and regulatory use case depends on it
3 Invest in ingestion (submissions, bordereaux) Removes the largest controllable manual cost
4 Build API and standard-message integration Protects against future market platform changes
5 Consolidate policy and data platforms after M&A Single exposure view; lower run cost
6 Automate reconciliation Fast payback; lower aged debt and operational risk
7 Set up AI governance before scaling AI Regulators and the LMA toolkit expect it

13. Technical consulting skills to build

Skill What to learn
Process modelling BPMN and swimlanes for placement, DA, claims
Data modelling Entity models for contract, party, exposure; mapping to CDR and ACORD
Requirements User stories with London acceptance criteria (UMR, lines, SND)
Integration APIs, ACORD messages, event-driven basics
Data platforms Modern data stack; data quality tooling; lineage
Vendor selection Fit-gap, total cost of ownership, scripted demos
Testing Test data for multi-insurer, multi-currency placements
AI Extraction, retrieval-augmented generation, evaluation, model risk (Module 09)

14. Mini case – consolidating policy systems after M&A

Situation: after two acquisitions, a managing agent runs three policy administration systems (£6m a year run cost). Exposure reports are built by hand from three extracts. Each system holds open years of account and active claims.

Option Pros Cons
A. Migrate everything to one system Single view; lowest run cost High migration risk for old years and claims
B. New business on one system; old years run off on legacy Lower risk Legacy run cost continues for years
C. Data platform first; consolidate later Fast single exposure view Two-step programme

Recommended: C then B – build the data platform for a single view within 6–9 months; move new business to the target system by class over 12–18 months; migrate open years only where claims volumes justify it; decommission the rest as years close.

15. Partner's lens

Top-tier strategy consulting methods applied to this chapter.

  1. Never lead with technology. Lead with the business outcome (expense ratio, exposure accuracy, faster quotes), then show technology as the means. Boards approve outcomes, not platforms.
  2. Learn from Blueprint Two in every proposal. Show the client how your plan avoids big-bang risk: incremental releases, value every quarter, standards-aligned, independent of a single market cutover date. This answers the CEO's unspoken fear.
  3. Price the cost of doing nothing. Calculate the annual cost of re-keying, reconciliation and duplicate systems. It is often larger than the programme budget over three years.
  4. Make data a CFO and CRO topic. Link data quality to capital (exposure uncertainty), reserving (claims data) and reporting risk. Data programmes funded by finance and risk last longer than those funded by IT.
  5. Insist on decommissioning. Many programmes add systems and never remove old ones. Partners make decommissioning a named benefit with an owner.

16. Key takeaways

  1. London modernisation has repeatedly failed when it relied on single market-wide cutovers.
  2. In March 2026 Lloyd's retired Blueprint Two; common data standards (including the CDR) and incremental modernisation continue.
  3. Every London data model must handle UMR, lines, MRC sections, CDR fields and bureau references.
  4. Architecture problems mostly come from M&A, point solutions and weak master data.
  5. Measure data quality like a business KPI and assign business owners.
  6. No-regret moves: CDR alignment, master data, ingestion, integration, consolidation, reconciliation automation, AI governance.

17. Check your understanding

# Question Answer
1 Give three reasons market-wide modernisation is hard. Collective action problem; uneven costs and benefits; shared legacy; unstructured data at source; spread decision rights; always-live market; long tail.
2 What survived the Blueprint Two decision? Common data standards including the CDR; incremental modernisation of bureau services.
3 Which references link premium and claims records? UMR, SND/OSND, UCR/TR.
4 What is a data product? A dataset managed with an owner, users, quality targets, documentation and service levels.
5 Name three London scenarios to script in a vendor demo. Signing down; mixed bureau/non-bureau; GUA endorsements; instalments and RP; binder bordereaux; complex claim; YOA reporting.
