Module 09

AI in the London Market
Level Advanced
Path phase Phase 2–3; your recommended primary spike (Module 18 §3)
Time 5 hours reading; ongoing hands-on practice
Prerequisites Modules 04–08
You will be able to Separate AI value from hype in London; explain how modern AI works in business terms; map use cases across the lifecycle; design and evaluate an AI solution; set up governance consistent with UK regulation and the LMA toolkit; advise on AI as an insured risk; build a business case and an AI programme

1. Reality check – where the market is in 2026

Evidence Tag
LMA survey on AI risk management (April 2026): 39 responses covering over 60% of market stamp capacity; 93% have an AI governance framework in place (72%) or in development (21%). Market described as at an early stage; general AI assistants drive most current use. Use cases mentioned include marine sanctions tracking, wording analytics for slip-level exclusions, and risk reporting linked to external data likely
LMA AI Adoption Toolkit with Barnett Waddingham (23 April 2026): principles-based; start with lower-risk use cases; embed controls from the outset; scale gradually likely
Lloyd's 2026–2030 strategy: AI should strengthen human decision-making, not replace it; Lloyd's will define data, technology and AI strategies likely
Syndicate 2479 (AIG-backed, managed by Talbot) began underwriting 1 January 2026, reportedly using AI agents to assess diversification and appetite fit likely
PRA 2026 priorities highlight insurers' increasing use of AI likely

Advisor view likely: most London firms have moved past experiments but few have AI at scale in core workflows. The binding constraint is data and workflow, not model capability. Firms that win will combine AI with data standards, clean master data, redesigned processes and governance.

2. How modern AI works – what a consultant must understand

You do not need to be a data scientist. You do need to explain these ideas clearly to executives.

Concept Plain explanation Why it matters in London
Machine learning (ML) Models learn patterns from historical data to predict outcomes Works where structured history exists (claims severity, propensity to bind); limited for rare, complex risks
Large language model (LLM) A model trained on very large amounts of text that can read, summarise, compare, extract and draft text London's work is document-heavy: submissions, MRCs, wordings, claims files
Context window How much text the model can read at once Long submissions and wordings may need splitting or retrieval
Hallucination The model produces confident but wrong content Unacceptable for coverage, pricing or reserving without checks
Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) The model answers using documents retrieved from a controlled library (e.g., wordings, guidelines) and cites them Grounds answers in the firm's own approved sources
Structured extraction Model fills a defined schema (fields) from documents Turns submissions and bordereaux into data
Agents AI that plans and carries out multi-step tasks using tools, with approval points Submission-to-quote, exception handling – needs strong controls
Evaluation Measuring accuracy on a test set of real examples The difference between a demo and a production system
Fine-tuning vs prompting Adjusting a model with extra training vs instructing it Most London use cases succeed with good prompting, retrieval and validation rather than fine-tuning likely

3. Use-case map across the lifecycle

Maturity: Now = in production at several London firms; Emerging = pilots and early production; Future = limited evidence. Judgements are likely unless noted.

Stage Use case Value lever Maturity Risk tier
Broking Submission pack creation; market selection support Broker productivity Now / Emerging Low–Medium
Intake Extract data from emails, SOVs, loss runs Remove manual keying Now Low
Triage Score appetite fit and priority; route to underwriter Selection; hit ratio Now / Emerging Medium
Underwriting assistant Summarise risk; compare to guidelines; draft referral notes Underwriter capacity Emerging Medium
Pricing ML factors; external data enrichment Rate adequacy Now (data-rich classes) Medium–High
Wording Compare MRC to standards; flag missing or changed clauses Contract certainty Now / Emerging Medium
Follow decisions Data-driven follow lines Low-cost capacity Emerging High
Exposure Geocoding; property attributes from imagery; clash detection Cat capital, pricing Now Medium
Sanctions Screen parties, vessels, voyages; AIS tracking Avoid breaches Now Medium–High
Delegated authority Map and validate bordereaux; detect breaches; coverholder analytics Cost; exposure speed Now / Emerging Low–Medium
Accounting Cash matching; break classification; late-payment prediction Aged debt; ops cost Emerging Low
Claims FNOL and triage Extract notices; classify complexity; route Speed Now Medium
Claims file review Summarise reports; draft reserve rationale; coverage checklist Handler productivity; reserve consistency Emerging Medium
Leakage and fraud Detect anomalies, duplicates, missed recoveries; spot AI-generated documents Loss ratio Emerging Medium–High
Reinsurance Match claims to treaties; check cessions Recoveries Emerging Low–Medium
Actuarial Data preparation; anomaly detection; narrative drafting Productivity Emerging Medium
Regulatory reporting Draft narratives; lineage checks Reporting cost Emerging / Future Medium
Agentic workflows Submission-to-quote; bordereaux exception agent Step change in cost per transaction Emerging / Future High

4. Deep dives

4.1 Submission intake and triage

flowchart LR
  A[Broker email and<br/>attachments] --> B[Extraction to schema:<br/>insured, class, limits,<br/>SOV, loss history]
  B --> C[Enrichment: company data,<br/>geocoding, sanctions,<br/>cat scores]
  C --> D[Appetite and priority score]
  D --> E{Fit?}
  E -- No --> F[Fast decline with reason]
  E -- Yes --> G[Workbench: pre-filled risk,<br/>pricing inputs]
  G --> H[Underwriter decision -<br/>human accountable]
  H --> I[Feedback improves<br/>extraction and scoring]
Aspect Detail
Value Underwriters review more of the right risks; faster declines; quicker quotes
Measures Field-level extraction accuracy; time to first review; quote turnaround; hit ratio on high-score risks; adoption
Pitfalls Weak accuracy on complex SOVs; scores trained only on past acceptance (copying past bias); no feedback loop
Design tip Train or tune scoring on profitability, not just on past bind decisions

4.2 Wording analytics and contract certainty

  • Compare each MRC and wording against approved standards and mandatory clauses (sanctions, state-backed cyber exclusions, communicable disease, war).
  • Flag missing, changed or unusual clauses before binding; explain the difference in plain English.
  • Why it matters: the Russia aircraft litigation shows how much money depends on precise wording.
  • Guardrails: evaluate on a test set of real wordings reviewed by lawyers; show the clause text, not just the AI's summary; underwriter decides.

4.3 Bordereaux processing

  • AI proposes mappings from each coverholder's layout to the standard model; humans confirm once; mappings are reused.
  • Validation rules check every row against structured binder terms (Module 05 §7).
  • AI drafts queries to coverholders for exceptions and classifies exception causes.
  • Measures: mapping accuracy; exceptions per 1,000 rows; days from receipt to validated data; effort per bordereau.

4.4 Claims file summarisation and triage

  • Structured summary of long files: facts, coverage issues, reserve drivers, next actions, deadlines.
  • Triage by complexity (aligned to the Lloyd's Claims Lead Arrangements thresholds for complex claims).
  • Guardrail: reserve and coverage decisions stay with handlers; AI output logged and reviewable.

4.5 Exposure and geospatial

  • Improve geocoding of SOVs and bordereaux; extract building attributes from aerial imagery; detect clashes (same location on several policies).
  • After catastrophes, compare imagery to exposures to estimate losses quickly.
  • Value: better cat model inputs → better pricing and possibly lower capital uncertainty.

4.6 Marine sanctions and voyage monitoring

  • Combine vessel tracking (AIS), ownership data and sanctions lists to flag vessels with suspicious behaviour (signal gaps, ship-to-ship transfers, port calls in sanctioned areas).
  • Mentioned as an emerging use case in the LMA survey likely.

4.7 Agentic and algorithmic underwriting

  • An agent performs steps (extract, enrich, check appetite, propose line) and stops at approval gates.
  • Suitable first for follow business, small risks and unchanged renewals.
  • Risks: correlated errors across many risks; adverse selection in a soft market; accountability under SM&CR; Lloyd's scrutiny of plans.

5. How to evaluate an AI solution (the skill most consultants lack)

5.1 Build a test set

Step Detail
Collect real examples e.g., 200 submissions, 50 bordereaux, 100 claims files (anonymised where needed)
Create the "gold standard" Experts record the correct answers (fields, classifications, flags)
Cover the hard cases Different brokers, layouts, languages, classes, poor scans
Keep it separate Do not tune the system on the test set

5.2 Measures

Measure Meaning Example
Field-level accuracy % of fields extracted correctly 96% of TIV values correct
Precision Of the items flagged, how many were right 80% of sanctions alerts were genuine concerns
Recall Of the true items, how many were flagged 99% of true sanctions matches were caught
Straight-through rate % processed with no human change 70% of bordereaux rows pass validation
Human correction rate % of AI outputs changed by users 12% of claim summaries edited
Time saved Before vs after 40 → 15 minutes per submission

Trade-off example – sanctions screening: high recall matters most (missing a sanctioned party is serious), so accept lower precision (more false alerts) and manage the review workload. For submission triage, balance both – too many false declines lose good business.

5.3 Production readiness checklist

  • Accuracy above agreed threshold on the test set, by segment (not just on average).
  • Clear human review points and override logging.
  • Monitoring of accuracy and drift after go-live.
  • Security, data residency and confidentiality controls agreed.
  • Fallback process if the AI service is unavailable (operational resilience).
  • Owner and accountable senior manager named.

6. Reference architecture for AI in a carrier

flowchart TB
  U[Users: underwriters, DA, claims,<br/>finance - in their workflow tools] --> APP[AI-enabled applications<br/>workbench, bordereaux, claims]
  APP --> GW[AI gateway: access control,<br/>prompt and output logging,<br/>data masking, model routing]
  GW --> M[Models: LLMs, ML models,<br/>document AI]
  APP --> RAG[Retrieval: approved wordings,<br/>guidelines, contracts, claims files]
  RAG --> DP[Data platform and<br/>document stores]
  APP --> RULES[Validation rules and<br/>structured contract terms]
  GW --> MON[Monitoring and evaluation:<br/>accuracy, drift, incidents]
  MON --> GOV[AI governance: inventory,<br/>risk tiering, approvals]

Design principles: AI inside the workflow (not a separate chatbot); controlled retrieval from approved sources; validation rules around AI outputs; full logging; model choice can change without rebuilding applications.

7. AI as a risk to insure

Theme What is happening Tag
Affirmative AI liability Lloyd's-backed products covering AI model errors, e.g., Armilla AI (limits reported up to $25m) and Testudo (generative AI liability, launched January 2026) likely
Silent AI Existing PI/E&O, D&O, cyber, product liability and crime policies may respond to AI-related losses never priced – like "silent cyber" a decade ago likely
Exclusions Some markets have introduced generative AI exclusions; London wordings under review guessing on market-wide adoption
Aggregation Many insureds relying on the same model or vendor → correlated losses likely
Deepfake fraud AI-generated voice/video used in payment fraud; also used in fraudulent claims certain as a phenomenon
D&O "AI washing" Claims against companies overstating AI capabilities likely

Silent AI exposure review – method:

  1. Inventory wordings across classes; identify where AI-related losses could trigger cover.
  2. Map insureds' AI use (sector, dependency on specific vendors/models).
  3. Build scenarios (e.g., a widely used model produces faulty outputs for 30 days).
  4. Estimate exposure; decide: exclude, affirm with sub-limits and pricing, or accept.

8. Governance and regulation

8.1 What applies

Area Requirement
UK approach No single AI law; regulators apply existing rules certain
PRA Model risk, governance, operational resilience and third-party risk apply to AI; SS1/23 (model risk) formally applies to banks but insurers often use it as a benchmark likely; internal model governance where AI affects capital
FCA Consumer Duty and fair value for retail/SME; PS25/21 removes larger commercial customers from Consumer Duty, ICOBS and PROD certain; financial crime and SM&CR still apply
SM&CR Named senior manager accountable for AI risks in their area
UK GDPR / DPA 2018 Lawful basis, minimisation, rights around automated decisions with significant effects on individuals
EU AI Act (Lloyd's Europe, EU operations) AI for risk assessment and pricing in life and health insurance for individuals is high-risk; most commercial P&C uses are not. Under the Digital Omnibus provisional agreement of 7 May 2026, high-risk (Annex III) obligations move to 2 December 2027 and Annex I (embedded) to 2 August 2028; confirm formal adoption likely
LMA toolkit Principles including governance and accountability, risk tiering, data protection, security and intellectual property likely
Confidentiality Broker submissions and client data are confidential; contractual and technical controls needed for external models

8.2 Minimum viable governance framework

Element Description
AI policy Board-approved; acceptable use, roles, risk appetite
Use-case inventory Owner, purpose, data, model, vendor, risk tier for every use case
Risk tiering Low (internal productivity) → Medium (decision support) → High (automated decisions affecting customers, capital or reporting)
Controls by tier Low: training, logging. Medium: test set evaluation, human review, monitoring. High: independent validation, senior approval, ongoing audit
Human-in-the-loop Humans accountable for binding, reserving and claims decisions
Third-party management Vendor due diligence; data residency; exit plans
Training AI literacy for staff and board

8.3 Risk tiering examples

Use case Tier Why
Drafting internal emails and meeting notes Low No decision impact
Bordereaux field mapping with human confirmation Low–Medium Validated by rules and humans
Submission triage scores Medium Influences which risks are reviewed
Claims summaries supporting reserving Medium Influences financial estimates
Automated follow-line decisions High Binds capital without individual human review
Pricing factors used in capital model High Affects capital and plan

9. Building an AI programme

flowchart LR
  A[1. Ambition and value pool] --> B[2. Data and process readiness]
  B --> C[3. Use-case portfolio:<br/>value x feasibility x risk]
  C --> D[4. Governance before scale]
  D --> E[5. Pilot 2-3 use cases<br/>with test sets and KPIs]
  E --> F[6. Scale: workflow redesign,<br/>integration, change management]
  F --> G[7. Value tracking]

9.1 Prioritisation example

Use case Value Feasibility Risk Priority
Property SOV extraction High High Low 1
Bordereaux mapping and validation High High Low 1
Claims file summarisation Medium High Medium 2
Wording comparison High Medium Medium 2
Triage scoring High Medium Medium 2
Agentic follow underwriting High Low High 3
Automated claims settlement Medium Low High 3

9.2 Business case – illustrative guessing; replace with client data

Driver Assumption Value
60 underwriting assistants 50% of time on data entry 30 FTE of data entry
Extraction removes half of that 50% 15 FTE released
Cost per FTE £75k £1.1m a year capacity
Faster quotes on preferred risks +2 points hit ratio on top-quartile submissions Growth/loss ratio upside – show separately
Costs Platform, integration, change, run £0.6–0.9m year 1; £0.3m run

Present cost savings and underwriting benefits separately – CFOs discount the second.

9.3 AI operating model

Model Description When it fits
Central AI centre of excellence Central team builds and governs Early stage; small firms
Hub and spoke Central platform and governance; business teams own use cases Most London managing agents at scale likely
Fully federated Each business builds its own Large groups with mature data capability

Roles needed: AI product owners in the business; data and ML engineers; evaluation specialists; risk and compliance partners; change managers.

9.4 Adoption – the overlooked success factor

  • Involve underwriters and handlers in designing outputs.
  • Show the AI's sources and confidence; never hide how it reached a result.
  • Measure and publish time saved and quality improvements.
  • Change incentives and procedures – otherwise people work around new tools.

9.5 Maturity model

Level Description
1. Ad hoc Individuals use general AI tools; no policy
2. Governed experiments Policy; inventory; a few pilots
3. Embedded use cases Several use cases in production, integrated, value tracked
4. AI-enabled operating model Processes redesigned around AI; data foundations and standards in place
5. AI-native Agentic workflows with oversight across the lifecycle

likely Most London managing agents sit at levels 2–3 in late 2026.

10. Failure modes

Failure Prevention
Pilot purgatory Fund integration and workflow change with the pilot; set scale criteria up front
Garbage in Fix master data and validation; measure data quality
No baseline Measure current performance before the pilot
Shadow AI Provide approved tools; train staff; monitor
Over-automation in a soft market Keep human decisions for binding; monitor rate adequacy
Ignoring brokers Work with brokers on data standards and shared benefits
No accountability Named owner and senior manager for each use case

11. Mini case – AI bordereaux pilot

Situation: a managing agent with 120 coverholders wants to "use AI for bordereaux".

Step Action
1 Baseline: effort per bordereau, days to validated data, exceptions per 1,000 rows
2 Build structured binder terms for the top 30 coverholders (prerequisite)
3 Test set: 3 months of bordereaux from 15 coverholders with expert-validated outputs
4 Pilot AI mapping and validation; measure accuracy and straight-through rate by coverholder
5 Scale criteria: ≥ 98% mapping accuracy on key fields; ≥ 60% straight-through; exception workflow live
6 Governance: Low–Medium tier; human confirmation of new mappings; monthly accuracy report

Expected outcome guessing: 40–60% less effort on mapping and validation; validated exposure data weeks earlier.

12. Partner's lens

Top-tier strategy consulting methods applied to this chapter.

  1. Start from the value pool, not the technology. A partner sizes where AI can move the P&L – expense ratio, selection, leakage, recoveries – before discussing models. "Where is the £10m?" comes before "Which LLM?".
  2. Workflow beats model. The same model gives very different value depending on whether it is embedded in the underwriter's workbench or sits in a separate chat window. Redesign the workflow; the model is replaceable.
  3. Evaluation is credibility. Bring test-set results by segment, not demos. Executives and regulators trust numbers like "97% field accuracy on 2,000 real SOV rows".
  4. Governance is an accelerator. Firms with clear risk tiering approve low-risk use cases in weeks. Position governance as the way to move faster safely – consistent with the LMA toolkit and Lloyd's stance.
  5. Bring the other side of the coin. Boards also need to understand AI as an insured risk (silent AI, aggregation, new products). Advising on both the operational and underwriting sides of AI is rare – and differentiating.

13. Build your own AI skills

Week Exercise Skill
1 Use an LLM to explain glossary terms; check against Module 12 Prompting, verification
2 Write a fictional MRC; extract Risk Details into a schema; measure field accuracy Extraction and evaluation
3 Create 10 fictional bordereaux with different layouts; map them to a standard; record failure patterns Mapping, error analysis
4 Build a small RAG assistant over public Lloyd's/LMA guidance; test with 20 questions; score answers RAG, evaluation design
5 Draft an AI governance pack for a fictional managing agent (section 8.2) Governance design
6 Write a business case with assumptions and sensitivities (section 9.2) Value case design
7 Design an agentic submission workflow with approval gates and controls Agent design

Rule: never put real client data into public AI tools; use fictional or public data for practice.

14. Key takeaways

  1. London AI adoption is real but early; data and workflow are the constraints.
  2. LLMs fit London's document-heavy work; hallucination requires grounding, validation and human decisions.
  3. Highest current value: intake, wording checks, bordereaux, claims summarisation, exposure, sanctions.
  4. Evaluate with test sets and segment-level metrics; precision and recall trade-offs depend on the use case.
  5. Governance by risk tier enables speed; humans stay accountable for binding, reserving and claims decisions.
  6. AI is also a new insured risk – silent AI, aggregation and affirmative products.

15. Check your understanding

# Question Answer
1 What is RAG and why use it? Retrieval-augmented generation – answers grounded in an approved document library, reducing hallucination and enabling citations.
2 For sanctions screening, which matters more: precision or recall? Recall – missing a true match is serious; manage the extra false alerts.
3 Name three items in a production readiness checklist. Test-set accuracy by segment; human review and override logging; monitoring; security; fallback; named owner.
4 Which tier is automated follow-line decision-making? High.
5 What is silent AI? Unpriced AI-related exposure in existing policies not designed for it.

Module 21

Lloyd's End to End: How the Market Functions, Every Process, Every Stakeholder
Level Intermediate → Advanced (master reference)

Time 6–8 hours reading; use as a reference afterwards

You will be able to Explain how Lloyd's works as a system: its stakeholders, annual cycle, syndicate life, underwriting, placement, accounting and settlement, claims, delegated authority (coverholders, bordereaux, DCAs), reinsurance, capital and oversight, systems and international platforms – and how they connect

How this chapter relates to the others: it brings everything about Lloyd's into one place, adds Lloyd's-specific detail, and links to the deeper chapters (04–07) for worked examples. If you only have one chapter to read before meeting a Lloyd's client, read this one.

In one sentence: clients' risks reach Lloyd's through brokers and coverholders; underwriters employed by managing agents accept them on behalf of syndicates; members' capital (with the Central Fund behind it) backs the promises; the bureau moves the money and records; the Corporation supervises the market under the oversight of the PRA and FCA.

3. The Lloyd's annual cycle

Timing likely – check Lloyd's business timetable Activity
1 January New year of account begins; underwriting under the approved plan
January Reinsurance renewals (1/1); PRA priorities letter
Monthly / quarterly Performance returns to Lloyd's; bordereaux cycles; Lloyd's market messages
March Lloyd's full-year results; syndicate annual reports
Q2 Mid-year planning guidance; 1 June Florida renewals
June–November North Atlantic hurricane season
Q3 Syndicate Business Forecasts (SBF) and capital returns submitted; Lloyd's half-year results; capacity auctions for Names
Q3–Q4 Lloyd's reviews and challenges plans; plan approvals
Late November Coming into Line – members' FAL in place
31 December Year of account three years old normally closes by RITC; accounts prepared
Annually Coverholder annual compliance; Statement of Actuarial Opinion; ORSA; audits

Consulting point: design engagements backwards from this calendar – a recommendation that misses the SBF window waits a year.

4. The life of a syndicate

Stage What happens Key requirements
1. Proposal Sponsor (group, investor, underwriting team) decides on a syndicate, SIAB, SPA or capital-backed structure Strategy, underwriting edge, capital source
2. Application Business plan, capital, people, governance, outsourcing submitted to Lloyd's Lloyd's approval; managing agent authorised (or turnkey agent used)
3. Capital uSCR, ECA, FAL provided by members Coming into Line
4. Operation Writes business each year of account within approved capacity Plan monitoring; returns; principles
5. Annual planning SBF and capital for the next year Lloyd's approval
6. Closing years Each YOA closed by RITC (usually at 36 months) Fair RITC pricing; SAO
7. Run-off or exit Stop writing; years run off or transfer to a run-off syndicate Run-off plans; legacy transactions

Structures in 2026: standard syndicates; Syndicates-in-a-Box; SPAs; capital-backed syndicates (e.g., investor-backed reinsurance syndicates); follow-only and AI-assisted syndicates. Thirteen new syndicates started on 1 January 2026 likely. See Module 02 §3.4.

5. Underwriting at Lloyd's

5.1 The chain of underwriting authority

flowchart TB
  L[Lloyd's approves syndicate plan - SBF] --> B[Managing agent board sets<br/>risk appetite and strategy]
  B --> C[CUO allocates classes,<br/>budgets and authorities]
  C --> U[Class underwriters: authority letters,<br/>line guides, pricing guidelines]
  U --> D[Risk-level decisions:<br/>open market, facilities, binders]
  D --> M[Monitoring: rate change,<br/>rate adequacy, aggregates, plan vs actual]
  M --> L

5.2 What a Lloyd's underwriter does every day

Activity Detail
Reviews submissions From brokers, often at the "box" in the Lloyd's building or on platforms
Decides lead or follow Sets terms as lead, or follows on the leader's terms
Prices Technical price, rate adequacy, risk-adjusted rate change (Module 04 §9)
Checks permissions Licensing and tax by territory (Lloyd's Crystal), sanctions, authority limits
Manages aggregates Cat exposure, Realistic Disaster Scenarios
Writes lines Stamps and signs on the MRC or platform
Agrees changes As slip leader under the GUA
Reviews binders Approves and monitors coverholder business in their class
Handles referrals From coverholders and junior underwriters
Reports Plan vs actual to CUO and Lloyd's

5.3 Lloyd's-specific underwriting controls

Control Lloyd's element
Business plan (SBF) Approved by Lloyd's; writing materially outside plan triggers intervention
Principles and minimum standards Lloyd's assesses managing agents' underwriting controls under principles-based oversight likely
Realistic Disaster Scenarios Lloyd's-prescribed scenarios measure gross and net exposure to major events
Risk codes Lloyd's classification for reporting and capital
Licensing (Crystal) Territory permissions and tax rules
Capacity Stamp capacity limits premium for each YOA

6. Placement at Lloyd's

Step Lloyd's detail
1. Broker accreditation Brokers placing at Lloyd's must be accredited by Lloyd's
2. Submission Broker prepares submission and MRC; UMR created
3. Lead selection Specialist Lloyd's lead; face-to-face in "the Room" or electronic
4. Lines Lead and followers write lines; total may exceed 100%
5. Signing down Lines reduced proportionally to 100%
6. Contract certainty All terms agreed before inception
7. Processing Premium submitted to Velonetic; signed with SND; settled centrally
8. Changes GUA governs who agrees endorsements

Full detail: Module 04.

7. Accounting and settlement at Lloyd's

Element Lloyd's detail
Central accounting All Lloyd's premium and claims go through the bureau (Velonetic) certain
Signing Each entry gets a Signing Number and Date; OSND links later entries
Central settlement Net settlement between brokers and syndicates in main currencies likely
Premium trust funds Syndicate premiums held in trust; US situs business in US trust funds (Lloyd's American Trust Fund); Canadian trust funds certain
Taxes IPT for UK; US Federal Excise Tax (4% direct, 1% reinsurance); overseas taxes guided by Crystal certain
Terms of trade Broker payment periods; settlement due dates
Reconciliation Managing agents reconcile bureau, ledger and broker records

Full detail: Module 06 Part A.

8. Claims at Lloyd's

8.1 The Lloyd's Claims Lead Arrangements (LCLA)

The LCLA replaced the Lloyd's Claims Scheme (Combined) likely.

Element Rule likely – verify against current LCLA text
Standard claims Agreed by the Lloyd's lead managing agent alone; followers bound
Complex claims Agreed jointly by the Lloyd's lead and the second Lloyd's lead
Complex thresholds (per Lloyd's share) First-party £2m; third-party £1m; non-proportional treaty reinsurance £5m – or other complexity criteria
Triage Lead triages and re-triages dynamically
Second lead Must be identifiable in the contract
Disputes Defined process if lead and second lead disagree or followers request it
Co-lead binders Claims lead agreement required (model clause LMA9186)

8.2 The Lloyd's claims process

sequenceDiagram
  autonumber
  participant I as Insured
  participant B as Lloyd's broker
  participant L as Lloyd's lead
  participant S as Second lead (complex)
  participant F as Followers
  participant E as Experts / adjusters
  participant V as Velonetic
  I->>B: Notify loss
  B->>L: Claim notification (UMR, details)
  L->>L: Triage standard or complex, check coverage and sanctions
  L->>E: Appoint experts
  L->>S: Involve second lead if complex
  L->>F: Advise reserve
  E-->>L: Reports
  L->>S: Agree (complex)
  L->>B: Agreement / coverage position
  B->>V: Claim payment request
  V->>L: Settlement from each syndicate share
  V->>F: Settlement from each share
  V->>B: Funds
  B->>I: Payment

8.3 Claims under delegated authority at Lloyd's

Element Detail
Claims authority Granted to coverholders or approved DCAs within limits
DCA approval and oversight Managing agents approve and audit DCAs; coordinated audits encouraged likely
Loss funds Advanced for local payments; reconciled
Claims bordereaux Monthly reporting
Co-lead binder claims Claims lead agreement (LMA9186) required likely

8.4 Catastrophe events at Lloyd's

Event coding; initial loss estimates and major loss reporting to Lloyd's likely; DCA surge; reinsurance notifications and reinstatements; liquidity planning; Lloyd's publishes market loss estimates. See Module 06 Part B.

9. Delegated authority at Lloyd's – end to end

9.1 Types of delegated arrangements at Lloyd's

Type Description
Open market coverholder Independent firm (often an MGA or broker) with binding authorities from one or more managing agents
Service company Coverholder owned by a managing agent's group
Branches / additional locations Each trading location needs separate approval likely
Lloyd's Europe coverholder Binds EEA risks for Lloyd's Europe
DCA / TPA Delegated claims handling
Full binding vs prior submit Full authority within limits, or approval needed before binding

Scale: about 45% of Lloyd's premium; about 412 service companies likely.

9.2 Becoming a Lloyd's coverholder

flowchart LR
  A[Find a Lloyd's broker and/or<br/>managing agent sponsor] --> B[Due diligence by<br/>managing agent]
  B --> C[Application on ATLAS]
  C --> D{Referral criteria met?<br/>v16, April 2026}
  D -- Yes --> E[Internal Lloyd's review]
  D -- No --> F[Standard processing]
  E --> G[Coverholder Undertaking signed]
  F --> G
  G --> H[Lloyd's approval]
  H --> I[Binding authority placed<br/>within 6 months of approval]
  I --> J[BAA registered before inception]
  J --> K[Binding begins]
Requirement likely Detail
ATLAS Lloyd's system for coverholder applications and records
Referral criteria Certain applications need internal Lloyd's review before submission – current version 16 (April 2026)
Coverholder Undertaking Coverholder commits to Lloyd's standards
Conditional approval Approval lapses unless a binding authority with a managing agent is entered within six months
Locations Each additional trading location needs separate approval
Data standards Lloyd's data standards are mandatory for new coverholders; bordereaux to Lloyd's reporting standards (v5.2 cited)
Annual compliance Annual compliance returns; outdated ATLAS records can restrict new binding authorities

9.3 The binding authority lifecycle at Lloyd's

Stage Owner Lloyd's element
Strategy and partner selection Managing agent DA strategy within SBF
Due diligence Managing agent Lloyd's expectations on oversight
Approval Lloyd's ATLAS, referral criteria
BAA placement Lloyd's broker, lead and followers Standard LMA wordings; registration before inception
Binding Coverholder Within classes, territories, limits, GPI
Reporting Coverholder → managing agents Bordereaux to Lloyd's standards
Premium remittance Coverholder → broker → bureau Settlement terms
Claims Coverholder / DCA Authority levels; LCLA for co-leads
Oversight Managing agents Risk-based monitoring; audits; coordinated audits
Changes Managing agent Changes to permissions on ATLAS; retrospective endorsements may be reviewed
Renewal / exit Managing agent Performance review; run-off

9.4 Oversight expectations likely

  • Oversight designed so DA business stays within appetite and complies; regular board reporting.
  • Key staff employed directly by the coverholder; no unapproved sub-delegation.
  • Coordinated audits where several managing agents share a lead binder (guidance May 2026).
  • Lloyd's Delegated Oversight Manager touchpoints may review practices such as retrospective endorsements.
  • Annual compliance and up-to-date ATLAS records.

Detail and frameworks: Module 05.

10. Bordereaux at Lloyd's – the complete picture

10.1 The bordereaux cycle

flowchart LR
  A[Coverholder binds risks<br/>during month] --> B[Prepare risk, premium,<br/>claims bordereaux]
  B --> C[Send to Lloyd's broker<br/>and managing agents]
  C --> D[Managing agent ingestion<br/>and mapping]
  D --> E[Validation vs BAA,<br/>Lloyd's standards, tax, sanctions]
  E --> F{Clean?}
  F -- No --> G[Queries to coverholder]
  G --> B
  F -- Yes --> H[Finance: premium booking,<br/>cash reconciliation]
  F -- Yes --> I[Exposure: aggregates,<br/>RDS, cat models]
  F -- Yes --> J[Claims and reserving]
  F -- Yes --> K[Oversight MI, audit triggers,<br/>Lloyd's reporting]

10.2 What each bordereau feeds

Bordereau Feeds If late or wrong
Risk Exposure management, RDS, cat models, reinsurance purchase, tax and regulatory reporting, licensing checks Cat exposure understated; wrong capital; tax errors
Premium Premium booking, credit control, reconciliation Aged debt; unreconciled cash
Claims Reserving, claims oversight, reinsurance recoveries Reserve surprises; missed recoveries

10.3 Standards and timing

Item Position
Lloyd's reporting standards Define fields for risk, premium and claims bordereaux; v5.2 cited likely
Delegated authority CDR Market consultation on a DA data standard in 2026 likely
LMA binder wording and data model Revision of 400+ wordings; data model planned 2026 likely
Typical timing Monthly; due within an agreed number of days after month end (set in the BAA)

10.4 Common errors and controls

Error Control
Missing or invalid addresses Geocoding validation at ingestion
Risks outside territory or class Automated BAA rule checks
Limits above maximum Rule checks; referral flags
GPI exceeded Cumulative tracking; alerts at 80/90/100%
Wrong tax rates Tax rule engine by location
Duplicate certificates Uniqueness checks
Premium not matching cash Cash reconciliation by binder
Claims without policy match Policy-claim linking rules
Late submission Timeliness tracking; tier escalation

11. Reinsurance at Lloyd's

Lloyd's syndicates are both sellers and buyers of reinsurance.

11.1 Syndicates as reinsurers (inwards)

Element Detail
Scale Reinsurance is one of Lloyd's largest segments (property, casualty and specialty treaty) certain
Placement Reinsurance brokers place treaties and facultative risks with Lloyd's leads and followers, usually on MRCs
Renewal dates 1 January, 1 April, 1 June, 1 July
Accounting Treaty business accounted quarterly through technical accounts; estimated premium income booked then adjusted
Claims Non-proportional treaty claims are "complex" above £5m per Lloyd's share under LCLA likely

11.2 Syndicates as buyers (outwards)

flowchart LR
  A[SBF and capital model<br/>uSCR, RDS] --> B[Outwards programme:<br/>quota share, risk XoL,<br/>cat XoL, aggregate]
  B --> C[Placed by reinsurance brokers;<br/>security review of reinsurers]
  C --> D[Cessions: allocate inwards<br/>risks to treaties]
  D --> E[Recoveries on claims;<br/>reinstatement premiums]
  E --> F[Credit risk monitoring;<br/>disputes; commutations]
  F --> A
Element Detail
Link to capital Reinsurance lowers uSCR and therefore ECA (× 1.35) – a key capital efficiency lever (Module 07 §E2)
Related-party reinsurance Reinsurance between syndicates or with group companies creates conflicts and is subject to Lloyd's requirements and scrutiny likely
Security Reinsurer ratings and collateral monitoring
2026 market Reinsurance is cheaper as capital is plentiful – an opportunity to buy more protection likely

11.3 Lloyd's-specific reinsurance structures

Structure What it is
RITC Reinsurance to close – transfers a closing year's liabilities to a later year or another syndicate
SPA A special purpose arrangement writes a quota share of a host syndicate – in effect a reinsurance relationship
Capital-backed reinsurance syndicates Investor-backed syndicates reinsuring a group's portfolio likely
London Bridge Lloyd's protected cell vehicle for ILS capital (cat bonds, collateralised reinsurance) certain
Legacy LPTs and ADCs on older years

12. Capital and oversight in brief

Element Summary Detail
Annual venture and YOA Each year separate; closed by RITC Module 07 §A1–A2
Chain of security Syndicate assets → FAL → central assets Module 07 §A3
Capital setting SBF → uSCR → ECA (× 1.35) → FAL → Coming into Line Module 07 §A4
Ratings S&P AA-, Fitch AA-, KBRA AA-, AM Best A+ likely —
Oversight Plan approval, principles-based oversight, minimum standards, Market Bulletins; oversight functions combined into one division (Nov 2026) to reduce friction and cost likely Module 07 Part F
Strategy 2026–2030 Underwriting performance; efficient marketplace (ambition of 1% incremental cost of operating at Lloyd's likely); capital advantage (RoC > 12% across the cycle); culture and talent Module 22

13. Systems and data landscape at Lloyd's

System / tool Owner Purpose
ATLAS Lloyd's Coverholder applications and records
Crystal Lloyd's Licensing, tax and regulatory information by territory
Lloyd's returns Lloyd's Plans, performance, capital, RDS submissions
Bureau systems (e.g., CLASS, ECF, IMR) Velonetic Claims processing, electronic claims files, document repository likely
Placing platforms PPL, Whitespace Electronic placement
Managing agent systems Each firm PAS, workbench, claims, finance, DA/bordereaux, exposure, data platform
Coverholder systems Each coverholder Binding, documents, bordereaux production

Data standards: MRC, CDR, ACORD GRLC, Lloyd's reporting standards, risk codes, UMR/UCR/SND. See Module 08.

14. Lloyd's international platforms

Platform Purpose
Lloyd's Europe (Brussels) EEA business; Lloyd's Europe coverholders
United States Surplus lines and reinsurance; US trust funds; some admitted licences likely
Canada Licensed; Canadian trust funds
Lloyd's China, Lloyd's Asia (Singapore), Lloyd's Japan, Lloyd's Dubai (DIFC), Lloyd's India (incl. GIFT City) Local platforms through service companies or branches likely

15. End-to-end story: a US property binder through a hurricane

Stage What happens at Lloyd's
Planning Syndicate X's SBF includes £200m of US property binder premium; Lloyd's approves; capital set
Coverholder A Florida MGA is approved on ATLAS, signs the Coverholder Undertaking; Syndicate X leads a binder with two following syndicates; BAA registered before 1 June inception
Binding MGA binds 4,000 homeowners and small commercial risks over six months within territory, limits and GPI
Bordereaux Monthly risk, premium and claims bordereaux; Syndicate X validates and feeds exposure models and RDS
Premium MGA remits net premium to the Lloyd's broker; bureau signs and settles to each syndicate's trust funds (US situs)
Reinsurance Syndicate X's cat XoL protects net exposure
Event A hurricane strikes in September; event code assigned; initial estimates to Lloyd's
Claims Approved DCA handles volume claims with a loss fund; large commercial claims referred; co-lead binder claims follow the agreed claims lead arrangement
Recoveries Cat XoL recoveries claimed; reinstatement premiums accrued
Capital and plan Loss affects 2026 YOA; capital adequacy reviewed; 2027 plan re-priced
Oversight Post-event review of exposure data quality and DCA performance; audit if needed
Close 2026 YOA closes by RITC at end-2028 if reserves are reliable

16. Common Lloyd's mistakes – and the correct position

Mistake Correct position
"Lloyd's underwrites the risk." Syndicates do; Lloyd's oversees the market (Lloyd's Europe is the exception for EEA).
"A coverholder is approved once and for all." Approval is conditional, location-specific, and maintained through annual compliance and ATLAS records.
"The binder can be registered after inception." It must be registered before inception.
"Followers agree every claim." Under LCLA, the lead agrees standard claims; lead and second lead agree complex ones.
"Bordereaux are an admin report." They feed exposure, capital, reserving, finance and oversight.
"Reinsurance is a cost centre." It is a capital and earnings-volatility lever, priced against ECA.
"Each syndicate has its own rating." All share the Lloyd's market ratings.
"The year's result is known at year end." YOA results are known only when the year closes, normally at 36 months.

17. Partner's lens

  1. See Lloyd's as a system. Most Lloyd's problems cross boundaries: a bordereaux problem is also an exposure, capital, finance and oversight problem. Map the flow across stakeholders before proposing a fix.
  2. Know the calendar and the gatekeepers. SBF approval, Coming into Line, coverholder approvals and binder registration are hard gates. Recommendations that ignore them stall.
  3. Lloyd's is changing its own model. The 2026 strategy and the November 2026 oversight restructuring aim to reduce friction and cost. Clients should prepare to benefit (simpler interactions) and to be held to higher performance standards.
  4. Use the stakeholder register in week one. Identify every party touched by the client's problem and what each measures. It prevents recommendations that work for one stakeholder and fail for another.

18. Key takeaways

  1. Lloyd's is a system: brokers and coverholders bring risk; managing agents underwrite for syndicates; members and the Central Fund provide security; the bureau moves money; the Corporation oversees.
  2. The annual cycle (plan, capital, Coming into Line, YOA, RITC) drives decisions.
  3. Delegated authority runs through ATLAS approvals, registered binders, bordereaux and oversight.
  4. Claims are agreed under the Lloyd's Claims Lead Arrangements – lead for standard, lead and second lead for complex.
  5. Syndicates sell and buy reinsurance; RITC, SPAs and London Bridge are Lloyd's-specific structures.
  6. In late 2026 Lloyd's is simplifying its oversight and targeting a lower cost of doing business.

19. Check your understanding

# Question Answer
1 Which system handles coverholder applications? ATLAS.
2 Within what period must an approved coverholder enter a binding authority? Six months (approval otherwise lapses) likely.
3 When must a binder be registered? Before inception.
4 Who agrees a £3m first-party claim on a Lloyd's share? Lead and second lead (complex; above £2m).
5 Name two Lloyd's-specific reinsurance structures. RITC; SPA; London Bridge; capital-backed reinsurance syndicates.
6 What happens at Coming into Line? Members must have FAL in place for the next year.
7 What did Lloyd's change in November 2026? Combined its oversight functions into one division to reduce complexity and cost likely.

  4. Walk through the hurricane story (section 15) and list every data hand-off and control.

Module 20

Market Landscape, Strategic Trends and Outlook 2027–2030
Level Advanced / Champion
Path phase Phase 3–4; refresh every quarter (Module 18)
Time 3 hours reading; 1 hour quarterly refresh
Prerequisites Modules 01–11
You will be able to Explain where the London market cycle stands in late 2026; describe the structural forces reshaping the market; run a scenario-based strategy discussion with a client executive team; identify what each type of player should do; know the signposts to watch in 2027

Date of this view: November 2026. Market views age quickly – update this chapter every quarter using the "so-what" notes in Module 19 §2.

1. Why this chapter matters

Executives do not buy process fixes in isolation. They buy help with where the market is going and what it means for them. A partner-level conversation starts with the industry's direction, then narrows to the client. This chapter gives you that industry view, with evidence and confidence tags, so you can open a CEO conversation with a point of view rather than a question.

2. Where the cycle stands – late 2026

Indicator Evidence Tag
Pricing Lloyd's prices fell about 3.7% in aggregate in 2025 (first negative year since the soft market that ended around 2017); risk-adjusted rates fell 6.7% in H1 2026 likely / certain
Lloyd's own warning Lloyd's Q2 2026 market messages: rate adequacy declining; adequacy for 2027 under threat; 2027 plans must reflect current conditions likely
Results Strong headline results (2025 CR 87.6%; H1 2026 CR 90.8%) helped by lighter catastrophe losses and reserve releases; underlying ratio rising certain / likely
Catastrophe losses H1 2026 global insured nat cat losses about $42–47bn – below 10-year average (Swiss Re $42bn, Munich Re $44bn, Gallagher Re $46bn, Aon $47bn) likely
Capital Reinsurers posted near-record first-half returns with excess capital building; Lloyd's approved capital up about 7% for 2026; 13 new syndicates likely
Rating agencies Fitch expects highly competitive conditions to drive further softening in most property and specialty lines in 2027; casualty is the main exposure likely
Watch item 1 January 2027 reinsurance renewals as the key indicator of whether softening continues likely

Summary view likely: the market is in an early-to-middle soft phase. Profits remain high because recent years were priced well and catastrophes were light, but new business is being written at falling margins. Without a major loss event, softening is likely to continue through 2027.

3. Fourteen structural trends

Each trend has: evidence, what it means, the client question it raises, and the consulting opportunity.

Trend 1 – A softening cycle with high capital
Evidence Section 2
Meaning Margins compress; discipline separates winners from losers; reserve releases may fade as soft years mature
Client question "Which of our classes are now below technical price, and what will we stop writing?"
Opportunity Cycle management: rate adequacy analytics, portfolio pruning, expense reduction (Module 10 Case 1)

Trend 2 – Institutional and private capital entering Lloyd's
Evidence Capital-backed syndicates (e.g., a Blackstone-backed Talbot syndicate); London Bridge 2 adding about $660m in Q4 2025; Names' capital interest returning; Lloyd's strategy targets "sophisticated capital" likely
Meaning More capacity chasing the same risks; more passive (follow) capital; capital providers demand transparency and returns
Client question "How do we attract and keep third-party capital, and what do investors need to see?"
Opportunity Capital structures; investor reporting; syndicate launches; governance of aligned vs third-party capital

Trend 3 – Carrier consolidation
Evidence Zurich completed its £8.1bn acquisition of Beazley on 1 October 2026; the combined specialty business has about $15bn GWP; Beazley delisted from the London Stock Exchange likely
Meaning Large global groups are buying London specialty franchises for talent, brands and Lloyd's platforms; fewer listed London specialists remain
Client question "Are we a buyer, a target, or a niche specialist – and what does each require?"
Opportunity M&A strategy and due diligence; integration (systems, operating model, Lloyd's approvals); retention of underwriting talent

Trend 4 – Broker consolidation and specialty M&A
Evidence Aon agreed a £12.4bn deal for US broker USI likely; UK distribution deal volume down but MGAs and Lloyd's brokers reached a record 29% of UK deals in 2026; private equity active in specialty distribution likely
Meaning Brokers gain scale, data and leverage over carriers; specialist wholesalers and MGAs are prized assets
Client question (carrier) "How dependent are we on three or four brokers, and how do we keep our position as they consolidate?"
Opportunity Distribution strategy; broker relationship analytics; MGA acquisition and integration

Trend 5 – Facilities and follow capacity under pressure
Evidence Lloyd's has cautioned that follow-market business models face increasing pressure as facility-led placement expands likely
Meaning Followers lose pricing power and data; leads and brokers gain; adverse selection risk rises
Client question "Is our follow book a business or a subsidy?"
Opportunity Channel profitability analysis; facility renegotiation; lead capability building

Trend 6 – MGA growth meets carrier caution
Evidence MGAs continue to grow and attract private equity; Fitch notes traditional carriers pulling back from MGA exposure likely
Meaning Capacity for MGAs becomes more selective; carriers demand alignment, data and performance
Client question (MGA) "Can we secure multi-year capacity, and what alignment will carriers demand?"
Opportunity Capacity strategy; DA oversight; data and reporting capability for MGAs (Module 10 Case 14)

Trend 7 – US domestic E&S competition
Evidence US domestic carriers and MGAs deploying capacity aggressively in E&S likely
Meaning London's share of US E&S business is contested, especially in standardised segments
Client question "Where does London still win in the US, and why?"
Opportunity US distribution strategy; speed and cost of DA operations; product differentiation

Trend 8 – Modernisation reset after Blueprint Two
Evidence Blueprint Two retired March 2026; incremental modernisation; common data standards; re-platforming not before 2028 likely
Meaning Firm-level investment matters more; standards and integration over a single market cutover
Client question "What should we build ourselves now?"
Opportunity Roadmap re-plan; CDR-aligned data; ingestion; reconciliation (Module 08)

Trend 9 – AI adoption, governed
Evidence LMA survey: 93% with AI governance in place or in development; LMA AI Adoption Toolkit (April 2026); AI-assisted syndicates launched likely
Meaning Moves from pilots to production in intake, DA, claims; governance becomes a speed advantage
Client question "Which AI use cases will move our expense ratio and selection in 2027?"
Opportunity AI strategy, governance, use-case scaling (Module 09)

Trend 10 – AI as a new insured risk
Evidence Affirmative AI liability products at Lloyd's; concern about silent AI and aggregation likely
Meaning A new specialty class and a hidden exposure in existing ones
Client question "How much AI risk are we already carrying without pricing it?"
Opportunity Silent AI exposure reviews; product design; wording strategy

Trend 11 – Regulation: lighter conduct, tougher prudential
Evidence FCA PS25/21 (Dec 2025) lightens conduct rules for larger commercial customers certain; PRA 2026 focus on internal models in a softening market and the DyGIST stress test likely; PRA SS5/25 on climate risk (Dec 2025), with gap reviews expected by June 2026 likely
Meaning Less conduct burden for large commercial; more scrutiny of capital, models, resilience and climate
Client question "Can we simplify conduct controls for large commercial books while strengthening model governance?"
Opportunity Regulatory simplification; model validation; climate risk integration; resilience

Trend 12 – Catastrophe and climate: secondary perils rising
Evidence Swiss Re notes rising wildfire risk even as H1 2026 losses were below average; a quiet first half does not guarantee a quiet year likely
Meaning Volatility from convective storms, floods and wildfire; exposure data quality and pricing of secondary perils matter
Client question "Is our pricing and capital reflecting secondary perils and climate trends?"
Opportunity Exposure data quality; cat model governance; climate scenario integration

Trend 13 – Geopolitics and war
Evidence Russia-related sanctions and aviation litigation continuing; marine war exposure in the Middle East (including the Strait of Hormuz) cited as a possible market-turning loss likely
Meaning Marine war, political violence and political risk pricing volatile; sanctions compliance costs high
Client question "What single geopolitical event would hit several of our classes at once?"
Opportunity Cross-class accumulation; sanctions technology; wording reviews

Trend 14 – Casualty and social inflation
Evidence Fitch flags casualty as the main exposure where rate adequacy could be tested in 2027 likely; social inflation continues to drive US liability costs certain
Meaning Reserve risk on older US years; pricing pressure on new business
Client question "How robust are our 2019–2023 US casualty reserves?"
Opportunity Reserve reviews; legacy transactions (ADC, LPT, RITC); claims litigation management

Trend 15 – Talent and capability
Evidence Lloyd's strategy commits to more early-careers intake and an "AI-trained" workforce likely; the London Matters 2025 report raised talent concerns likely
Meaning Competition for lead underwriters, data and AI talent; changing skills in operations
Client question "Which skills will our operations and underwriting teams need by 2028?"
Opportunity Workforce planning; capability building; operating model redesign

4. Scenarios for 2027–2030

Top-tier strategy work uses scenarios when the future is uncertain. Three plausible scenarios guessing – they are tools for thinking, not forecasts.

Scenario A – Long soft market Scenario B – Shock and hardening Scenario C – Structural reshaping
Story No major loss; capital keeps arriving; rates fall for 2–3 more years; margins thin A major catastrophe, geopolitical or systemic cyber/AI event removes capital; prices rise sharply Consolidation, broker power, facilities and AI change who earns profit, regardless of the cycle
Signposts 1/1 2027 renewals soft; H2 2026 cat losses low; new syndicates continue Large US hurricane or earthquake; major marine war loss; systemic cyber event; reserve shock in casualty More carrier and broker M&A; facility share rising; AI-native syndicates growing; Lloyd's cost reforms
Winners Low-cost, disciplined underwriters; distribution owners Well-capitalised leads with strong reinsurance; firms that held discipline Scale players; strong leads; data-rich distributors; efficient platforms
Losers Followers without selection; sub-scale high-cost carriers Thinly capitalised, aggressive growers; MGAs with single capacity source Sub-scale independents; passive followers; firms without data capability

No-regret moves (work in all three scenarios)

  1. Know rate adequacy by class and channel – monthly.
  2. Take out 1–2 points of operating expense through automation and simplification.
  3. Fix exposure data quality and cross-class accumulation views.
  4. Strengthen reserve reviews on older US casualty years.
  5. Build governed AI capability in intake, DA and claims.
  6. Secure strong, diversified reinsurance while it is cheap.

Scenario-specific options

Scenario Options to prepare
A Selective shrinkage; cost programme; reinsurance purchase; capital return to providers
B Capital ready to deploy into hardening classes; underwriting talent retained; rapid re-pricing capability
C M&A readiness (buy or partner); distribution partnerships; data and AI platforms; lead position investment

5. What each type of player should do likely

Player Priority agenda 2027
Large managing agents (part of global groups) Integration and platform choice (Lloyd's vs company); scale benefits; talent retention
Mid-size independent managing agents Distinctive lead positions; cost discipline; possible partnerships or sale
Follow-heavy and capital-backed syndicates Selection analytics; facility terms; investor transparency
Company market insurers Platform economics vs Lloyd's; distribution partnerships; cost
Global brokers Integration after M&A; facilities strategy; data monetisation; conflict management
Wholesale and specialist brokers Specialisation; carrier relationships; consolidation positioning
MGAs Multi-year, multi-carrier capacity; data and reporting quality; alignment
Capital providers Return discipline; manager selection; transparency
Corporation of Lloyd's Delivering lower cost of doing business, risk-based oversight and capital attractiveness under the 2026–2030 strategy
Technology vendors Integration with standards; AI with evidence of accuracy; London-specific functionality

When Event Why it matters

January 2027 PRA insurance supervision priorities letter Regulator's focus for the year
Q1 2027 Lloyd's FY2026 results and market messages Market performance, plan expectations

7. Running a strategy session with a client executive team

Step Content Time
1. Market view Present sections 2–3 tailored to the client's classes 20 min
2. Client position Client's performance vs market: underlying ratio, rate adequacy, expense ratio, lead share, channel mix 20 min
3. Scenarios Walk through A, B, C; ask executives to assign probabilities and identify signposts 30 min
4. Implications For each scenario: what would we regret not having done? 30 min
5. Decisions Agree no-regret moves and options to prepare; owners and dates 20 min

Partner technique: ask each executive privately before the session which scenario they believe is most likely. Disagreement among the team is usually the most valuable insight of the day.

8. Partner's lens

  1. Lead with an industry point of view. Open with "Here is where we think the market is going and what it means for you", supported by evidence. Clients pay for perspective.
  2. Separate cycle from structure. Clients over-react to the cycle and under-react to structural change. The cycle will turn; consolidation, broker power, facilities and AI will not reverse.
  3. Make the soft market a strategy topic, not a pricing topic. The question is not only "how do we price better?" but "which businesses should we be in when margins are thin, and what must we own to win?"
  4. Use scenarios to unlock decisions. Executives often avoid decisions because the future is uncertain. Scenarios show which decisions are right whatever happens.
  5. Refresh your view quarterly. A market view more than three months old is stale. Your credibility depends on knowing last week's renewal news and last month's deals.

9. Key takeaways

  1. Late 2026: an early-to-middle soft phase with high capital, strong headline profits and rising underlying ratios.
  2. Structural forces – consolidation, capital inflows, broker power, facilities, MGAs, AI, regulation, climate, geopolitics – are reshaping who earns profit.
  3. Zurich's completed acquisition of Beazley (October 2026) signals global groups buying London specialty franchises.
  4. Plan with scenarios: long soft market, shock and hardening, structural reshaping – and act on no-regret moves now.
  5. Watch 1/1 2027 renewals, Lloyd's FY2026 results, the PRA's 2027 priorities, the hurricane season and M&A.

10. Check your understanding

# Question Answer
1 Name three pieces of evidence that the market is softening. Lloyd's −3.7% in 2025 and −6.7% H1 2026; Lloyd's warning on 2027 rate adequacy; Fitch expectation of further softening; excess reinsurance capital.
2 Why are follow-heavy syndicates under pressure? Facility-led placement expands; followers lose pricing power and data; adverse selection risk.
3 Give two no-regret moves for any scenario. Rate adequacy monitoring; expense reduction; exposure data quality; reserve reviews; governed AI; buy cheap reinsurance.
4 What event in October 2026 reshaped the London specialty landscape? Zurich completed its £8.1bn acquisition of Beazley.
5 What is the key signpost for whether softening continues? 1 January 2027 reinsurance renewals.

Module 22

New Strategies in the London Market
Level Champion
Path phase Phase 3–4 (Module 18)
Time 3 hours reading
Prerequisites Modules 07, 20, 21
You will be able to Explain the strategic moves that Lloyd's, carriers, brokers, MGAs and capital providers are pursuing or debating in 2026; assess each strategy's economics, risks and fit for a given client; facilitate a strategy discussion and shape consulting work around it

Date of this view: November 2026. Evidence is tagged; strategies marked guessing are ideas being discussed in the market or logical next steps, not confirmed initiatives.

1. How to read this chapter

Each strategy is described with the same seven points: what it is, evidence, economics, who benefits, risks, maturity, and consulting angle. Section 5 gives a framework to choose between them for a specific client.

2. Strategies led by Lloyd's (the Corporation)

S1 – Cut the cost and friction of doing business at Lloyd's

Point Detail
What Reduce the cost and time of operating at Lloyd's for managing agents and capital providers
Evidence 2026–2030 strategy: "efficient and flexible marketplace"; ambition of a 1% incremental cost of operating at Lloyd's likely; in November 2026 Lloyd's combined its oversight functions into one division as a "first step in eliminating unnecessary friction and complexity" likely; LMA secured annual change cycles for the Principles for Business Oversight likely
Economics Lower central charges and lower internal compliance cost for managing agents
Who benefits Managing agents, capital providers, new entrants
Risks Simplification may be slower than hoped; performance expectations may rise in return
Maturity Early implementation
Consulting angle Help managing agents redesign their Lloyd's-facing reporting and oversight processes; quantify the "cost of Lloyd's" and track benefits

S2 – Risk-based, predictable oversight

Point Detail
What Oversight intensity tailored to each managing agent's performance and maturity; predictable change cycles
Evidence Strategy commitments to predictable, risk-based oversight and streamlined reporting likely
Economics Good performers face less burden; weak performers face more
Consulting angle "Oversight readiness" – self-assessment against principles; evidence packs; MI that shows Lloyd's what it needs

S3 – Make Lloyd's the most capital-efficient marketplace

Point Detail
What Attract institutional and private capital; simplify Names processes; tailor oversight for different capital types; target return on capital above 12% across a 10-year cycle
Evidence Strategy driver "maximised capital advantage"; Names simplification; London Bridge 2 growth; capital-backed syndicates likely
Economics More capital; lower cost of capital; more capacity in hard markets
Risks More passive capital adds to soft-market pressure
Consulting angle Capital structuring; investor reporting; capital efficiency reviews

S4 – Incremental modernisation and open architecture

Point Detail
What Move from a single market cutover (Blueprint Two) to incremental change, common data standards and open architecture
Evidence March 2026 decision; Velonetic refocused; CDR continues likely
Consulting angle Firm-level roadmaps aligned to standards (Module 08)

S5 – Innovation through Lloyd's Lab

Point Detail
What Accelerator for insurtechs on themes Lloyd's wants to develop
Evidence Cohort 16 (2026) themes included operational efficiency, new products and emerging risks such as AI and battery assets; Cohort 17 (September 2026) included a "portfolio steering and capital allocation" theme likely
Consulting angle Scouting and piloting Lab solutions for clients; building business cases for adoption

3. Strategies of carriers and managing agents

S6 – Scale through consolidation

Point Detail
What Global groups acquire London specialty franchises; mid-size players merge or sell
Evidence Zurich completed its £8.1bn acquisition of Beazley on 1 October 2026 (combined specialty business about $15bn GWP) likely
Economics Scale in distribution, data, reinsurance buying and capital; cost synergies
Risks Talent loss; culture clash; integration complexity; Lloyd's and regulatory approvals
Consulting angle Due diligence; integration management office; platform choice (Lloyd's vs company); underwriter retention

S7 – Build a lead franchise

Point Detail
What Invest in specialist underwriters, claims excellence and data to lead more business
Evidence Lloyd's strategy driver "leading underwriting performance" likely; facility growth squeezes followers likely
Economics Leads set price and terms, see more risks first and attract follow capital
Risks Expensive talent; requires patience in a soft market
Consulting angle Lead share analytics; underwriting talent strategy; claims as a differentiator

S8 – Smart follow: data-driven and algorithmic following

Point Detail
What Deploy follow capacity using data and rules across many risks, selecting which leads and segments to follow
Evidence Algorithmic follow platforms such as Ki (syndicate 1618) and tracker-style syndicates likely; AI-assisted syndicate 2479 from 2026 likely
Economics Very low cost per risk; scalable capacity
Risks Adverse selection in a soft market; model risk; dependence on lead quality
Consulting angle Lead-quality scoring; follow portfolio analytics; model governance

S9 – AI-native underwriting and operations

Point Detail
What Rebuild underwriting and operations around AI: intake, triage, pricing support, bordereaux, claims
Evidence LMA survey and AI Adoption Toolkit; AI-assisted syndicates likely
Economics Lower expense ratio; better selection; faster quotes
Risks Governance, accuracy, over-automation, regulatory scrutiny
Consulting angle AI strategy, governance and scaling (Module 09)

S10 – Dual and multi-platform optimisation

Point Detail
What Choose for each class the best platform: Lloyd's syndicate, UK company, Bermuda, US E&S carrier, EU entity
Evidence Hybrid groups already run multiple platforms; consolidation increases the choice likely
Economics Lower cost and capital; better distribution access
Consulting angle Platform economics model per class (licences, rating, capital, cost, tax)

S11 – Distribution partnerships and MGA platforms

Point Detail
What Carriers build or buy MGAs, incubate new MGAs, or form multi-year capacity partnerships
Evidence MGAs and Lloyd's brokers reached a record 29% of UK distribution deals in 2026; private equity active in specialty distribution likely
Economics Access to distribution and specialist talent; fee income
Risks Misaligned incentives; carriers pulling back from MGA exposure as markets soften likely
Consulting angle MGA partnership design; alignment terms; DA oversight

S12 – Cost transformation through operating model change

Point Detail
What Shared services, global capability centres, automation, application rationalisation
Evidence Soft market pressure; Lloyd's cost focus likely
Economics 1–2 points of expense ratio over two years guessing
Consulting angle Operating model design; location strategy; automation programme (Module 10 Case 8)

S13 – Capital efficiency and legacy clean-up

Point Detail
What Buy cheap reinsurance, use ADCs and LPTs on older casualty years, close open years, optimise mix
Evidence Soft reinsurance pricing; social inflation reserve pressure likely
Economics Released capital; higher return on capital (Module 07)
Consulting angle Capital efficiency reviews; legacy transaction support

S14 – Claims as a competitive weapon

Point Detail
What Differentiate on claims speed, expertise and certainty; use claims data in underwriting
Evidence Lloyd's strategy targets top-tier client satisfaction (NPS) likely; brokers choose leads partly on claims reputation
Consulting angle Claims transformation; leakage; triage; client experience measures

4. Strategies in new risks, products and markets

S15 – New risk classes

Class Strategy Tag
AI liability Affirmative AI products; silent AI management likely
Energy transition Renewables, battery storage, hydrogen, carbon capture – new technologies with little loss history likely
Cyber catastrophe capital Using ILS (e.g., cyber cat bonds) to expand cyber capacity likely
Space Satellite constellations and launch likely
Climate adaptation and resilience Products that reward risk reduction guessing

S16 – Parametric insurance

Point Detail
What Pay a pre-agreed amount when a measurable trigger occurs (wind speed, rainfall, earthquake intensity), without traditional loss adjustment
Evidence Lloyd's Lab has supported parametric start-ups (e.g., parametric cover for solar assets) likely
Economics Fast payment, low claims cost; basis risk for clients
Consulting angle Product design; trigger data; distribution through public-private partnerships

S17 – Closing protection gaps with public-private partnerships

Point Detail
What Work with governments and development bodies on catastrophe, terrorism, pandemic and climate risks that are under-insured
Evidence UK schemes such as Pool Re and Flood Re certain; parametric and disaster risk financing growth likely
Consulting angle Programme design; risk modelling; governance

S18 – International hubs

Point Detail
What Write closer to clients through Lloyd's Europe, Lloyd's Asia, Lloyd's Dubai, Lloyd's India (GIFT City) and similar platforms
Evidence Ongoing coverholder approvals for Lloyd's Europe; regional platforms likely
Consulting angle Market entry strategy; local operating models; regulatory set-up

5. Strategies of brokers and capital providers

Strategy What Evidence Consulting angle
S19 – Broker scale and data Large acquisitions; using placement data as an asset Aon's £12.4bn USI deal likely Integration; data strategy; conflict governance
S20 – Facilities and portfolio placement Pre-arranged capacity across many risks Lloyd's warning that follow models face pressure as facility-led placement expands likely Facility design (broker side); facility assessment (carrier side)
S21 – Specialist wholesale positioning Niche expertise and carrier relationships Wholesale broker acquisitions such as Clear Group's purchase of a Lloyd's wholesale broker likely Growth strategy; carrier panel management
S22 – Third-party capital vehicles Sidecars, SPAs, capital-backed syndicates, London Bridge cells London Bridge 2 inflows; investor-backed syndicates likely Structuring; investor reporting
S23 – Market utility for shared processes Shared processing for bordereaux, reconciliation or DA data among carriers on the same binders Discussed as a logical next step after Blueprint Two guessing Feasibility study; business case; governance design

6. Choosing strategies for a client

6.1 Attractiveness × ability to win

Low ability to win High ability to win
High attractiveness Partner or acquire capability Invest now
Low attractiveness Avoid Maintain or harvest

Attractiveness factors: profit pool size, growth, cycle resilience, capital intensity, regulatory direction. Ability-to-win factors: underwriting talent, lead position, distribution access, data and technology, capital, brand, cost position.

6.2 Fit by player type guessing – starting hypotheses for discussion

Strategy Large group MA Mid-size independent MA Follow / capital-backed syndicate Broker MGA
S1–S2 Lloyd's cost and oversight readiness ● ● ● ○ ○
S6 Consolidation ● (buyer) ● (target/partner) ○ ● ●
S7 Lead franchise ● ● (niches) ○ — —
S8 Smart follow ○ ○ ● — —
S9 AI-native operations ● ● ● ● ●
S10 Multi-platform ● ○ — — —
S11 MGA partnerships ● ● ○ ● ●
S12 Cost transformation ● ● ● ● ●
S13 Capital efficiency ● ● ● — —
S15–S17 New risks / parametric ● ● (niche) ○ ● ●
S20 Facilities ○ ○ ● (assess) ● —
S23 Market utility ● ● ● ● ●

● = high relevance; ○ = some relevance; — = low relevance.

6.3 Questions for a strategy refresh workshop

  1. Where are we a lead and where are we a passenger?
  2. Which classes are below technical price, and what will we stop doing?
  3. What would a competitor with twice our scale do with our book?
  4. Which partner (broker, MGA, capital provider) would hurt most if it left – and why would it stay?
  5. What two capabilities (talent, data, AI, capital) would most change our ability to win?
  6. Which of the 23 strategies are we pursuing by accident rather than by choice?

7. Shaping consulting work around these strategies

Client situation Engagement
Board asks "what is our strategy for the soft market?" Strategy refresh: market view (Module 20), portfolio diagnostics, strategy choices, roadmap
Group considering an acquisition Commercial and operational due diligence; integration planning
Follow-heavy syndicate under margin pressure Follow portfolio analytics; facility review; smart-follow model
Managing agent with high costs Cost transformation and Lloyd's oversight readiness
MGA losing capacity Capacity strategy; alignment proposal; DA data upgrade
Investor entering Lloyd's Structure choice; manager selection; investor reporting
Carrier exploring new classes Market entry: AI liability, energy transition, parametric

8. Partner's lens

  1. Strategy is choosing what not to do. In a soft market, the most valuable strategic choice is often what to stop writing, which partners to exit and which capabilities not to build.
  2. Separate fashion from fundamentals. AI, facilities and parametric are fashionable; the fundamentals are still selection, price, cost, capital and distribution. Test every new strategy against them.
  3. Follow the profit pool. Ask where profit is moving (towards leads, distribution, scale and data) and whether the client is positioned to capture it.
  4. Pair every strategy with an operating model. A strategy without the people, processes, data and governance to deliver it is a slogan. Top-tier work always ends in "what changes on Monday".
  5. Track the signals quarterly. Use the signposts in Module 20 §6 to know when a strategy's assumptions stop holding.

9. Key takeaways

  1. Lloyd's is pursuing lower cost and friction, risk-based oversight, capital efficiency and incremental modernisation.
  2. Carriers are choosing between scale (consolidation), lead franchise, smart follow, AI-native operations and cost transformation.
  3. Distribution power is shifting to brokers, facilities and MGAs; carriers respond with partnerships and selectivity.
  4. New risk classes, parametric products and international hubs offer growth with new risks.
  5. Use attractiveness × ability to win, and always pair strategy with an operating model.

10. Check your understanding

# Question Answer
1 What did Lloyd's change in November 2026 and why? Combined its oversight functions into one division to reduce friction, complexity and cost.
2 What is the main risk of smart follow strategies in a soft market? Adverse selection – following under-priced risks.
3 Name the transaction that completed in October 2026. Zurich's £8.1bn acquisition of Beazley.
4 What is basis risk in parametric insurance? The payout may not match the client's actual loss.
5 What two dimensions decide which strategy fits a client? Attractiveness and ability to win.
