Module 11

Consulting Playbook: Top-Tier Strategy Consulting Craft Applied to the London Market
Level Champion
Path phase Phases 2–4 – use continuously (Module 18)
Time 5–6 hours reading; then weekly practice
Prerequisites Modules 01–02; ideally 04–07
You will be able to Define a client problem precisely; build issue and hypothesis trees; size value in London market terms; structure a storyline using the pyramid principle; run the first two weeks of an engagement; handle executive challenge; shape and sell work

This chapter describes the widely documented working methods of top-tier strategy firms (McKinsey, Bain, BCG and similar) – as set out in public books, training material and practitioner accounts – applied to the London market. It is not insider material from any firm.

1. What top-tier consulting actually sells

Clients do not pay top-tier fees for information. Information is cheap. They pay for four things:

What they buy What it looks like
A clear answer "Exit two classes, re-price three, cut 1.8 points of expense ratio" – not "here are the options"
Confidence in the answer Facts, analysis and logic the board can rely on
Speed A decision-quality answer in 6–8 weeks, not 6 months
Change that sticks Plans, owners and tracking that turn the answer into results

Everything in this chapter serves those four outcomes. The London market adds two special requirements: you must speak the market's language fluently (otherwise clients do not trust the answer), and you must respect the market's constraints (Lloyd's, the bureau, brokers, subscription) or the answer cannot be implemented.

2. Step 1 – Define the problem precisely

2.1 Why it matters

Most failed engagements solve the wrong problem. "Help us with our DA operations" is a topic, not a problem. Top-tier teams spend real effort writing one precise question before any analysis.

2.2 Problem statement worksheet

Element Question London example
Key question What exactly must we answer? Specific, measurable, time-bound "How can Syndicate X reduce its expense ratio by 2 points by the 2028 year of account without increasing its loss ratio?"
Decision-maker Who decides, and what decision? CEO and board; approve a 2-year programme and 2027 business plan
Context What is happening that makes this urgent? Rates down 6.7% in H1 2026; Lloyd's challenging 2027 plans; expense ratio 4 points above peers
Criteria for success How will we know we succeeded? 2-point reduction locked into the 2028 SBF; no loss ratio deterioration; Lloyd's plan approval
Scope What is in and out? In: operations, DA processing, technology, facilities terms. Out: exiting classes (separate review)
Constraints What cannot change? Bureau processes; existing coverholder contracts until renewal; no redundancies in underwriting
Stakeholders Who must be involved or convinced? COO, CUO, CFO, Head of DA, IT; Lloyd's oversight team (indirectly)
Key sources Where will facts come from? Syndicate accounts, cost ledger, FTE data, process volumes, bordereaux logs

Test: if two senior people at the client read your key question and would give different success criteria, it is not precise enough.

3. Step 2 – Structure the problem: issue trees

3.1 Two kinds of tree

Tree Question it answers Use when
Diagnostic (why) tree Why is this happening? Problem is not yet understood
Solution (how) tree How could we fix it? Problem is understood; options needed

Both must be MECE: mutually exclusive (no overlaps) and collectively exhaustive (nothing missing).

3.2 Diagnostic tree – "Why is our combined ratio 5 points worse than peers?"

flowchart LR
  Q[Combined ratio 5 pts<br/>worse than peers] --> L[Loss ratio higher]
  Q --> E[Expense ratio higher]
  L --> L1[Attritional higher]
  L --> L2[Large / cat higher]
  L --> L3[Prior-year development worse]
  L1 --> L1a[Mix: more of weaker classes]
  L1 --> L1b[Pricing: lower rate adequacy]
  L1 --> L1c[Selection: worse risks within class]
  L1 --> L1d[Channel: DA or facility business worse]
  L2 --> L2a[Higher exposure per event]
  L2 --> L2b[Reinsurance less protective]
  L3 --> L3a[Reserving assumptions]
  L3 --> L3b[Claims handling and leakage]
  E --> E1[Acquisition costs higher]
  E --> E2[Operating costs higher]
  E1 --> E1a[Channel mix - DA commission]
  E1 --> E1b[Broker and facility terms]
  E2 --> E2a[Manual processing]
  E2 --> E2b[Systems duplication]
  E2 --> E2c[Scale - fixed costs on small premium]
  E2 --> E2d[Lloyd's and regulatory costs]

Each end branch must be testable with data. "Selection is worse" is tested by comparing loss ratios of risks within the same class and price band against peers or market data.

3.3 Solution tree – "How can we cut 2 points of expense ratio?"

flowchart LR
  S[Cut 2 pts of<br/>expense ratio] --> R[Reduce cost]
  S --> G[Grow premium on<br/>same cost base]
  R --> R1[Remove work: eliminate,<br/>simplify, standardise]
  R --> R2[Automate work: ingestion,<br/>reconciliation, bordereaux]
  R --> R3[Move work: shared services,<br/>offshore, outsourcing]
  R --> R4[Reduce IT run cost:<br/>rationalise applications]
  R --> R5[Renegotiate external costs:<br/>facility terms, vendors]
  G --> G1[Grow profitable classes]
  G --> G2[Raise capacity use of<br/>existing teams]

Tip: the first split of a tree decides the quality of the work. Common good first splits in London work: loss vs expense; price vs volume; open market vs delegated vs facility; lead vs follow; class; year of account.

4. Step 3 – Work hypothesis-first

4.1 The idea

Instead of collecting all the data and then looking for an answer, write your best guess of the answer on day one, then design the minimum analysis to prove or disprove it. This is faster and forces focus. You must be willing to drop a hypothesis when the data says no.

4.2 Hypothesis tree for the expense ratio problem

Hypothesis Analysis to test it Data source Kill criterion
H1: Over 40% of operations effort is re-keying and reconciliation Activity analysis of operations teams; volume and time sampling Team interviews, timesheets, process logs < 25% of effort
H2: DA bordereaux processing costs 2× peers per £m premium Cost per bordereau and per £m DA premium; benchmark Cost ledger, bordereaux logs, benchmark data Within 20% of peers
H3: Five legacy systems duplicate policy data, adding £4m+ IT run cost Application inventory and cost mapping IT budget, architecture Run cost < £2m
H4: Facility terms cost 1+ point more than open market acquisition Acquisition cost by channel Finance data by channel Difference < 0.3 points
H5: Premium credit control is manual, leaving > 15% of premium > 90 days Aged debt analysis Finance ledger < 5%

4.3 Workplan (the "analysis plan")

Analysis Hypothesis Output (ghost chart) Owner Due
Combined ratio bridge vs peers All Waterfall chart Analyst A Week 1
Activity analysis – operations H1 Stacked bar of effort by activity Consultant B Week 2
Bordereaux cost benchmark H2 Bar chart vs peers Consultant B Week 3
Application cost map H3 Table and heat map Tech lead Week 3
Channel profitability H4 Bar: acquisition + loss ratio by channel Analyst A Week 2
Aged debt H5 Ageing profile Analyst A Week 2

The "ghost chart": sketch the chart you expect before you have the data. It tells the team exactly what data to get and stops endless data collection.

4.4 The 80/20 rule

Usually 20% of analyses give 80% of the insight. Top-tier teams ask every few days: "What is the most important thing we do not yet know, and what is the quickest way to find it?" In London work, the quickest high-value analyses are almost always: combined ratio bridge, channel/class profitability, activity analysis, and aged debt.

5. Step 4 – Size the value (London driver trees)

5.1 The London value driver tree

flowchart LR
  ROC[Return on capital] --> P[Profit]
  ROC --> C[Capital required]
  P --> UW[Underwriting result]
  P --> INV[Investment return]
  UW --> NEP[Net earned premium]
  UW --> LR[Loss ratio]
  UW --> ER[Expense ratio]
  NEP --> GWP[Gross written premium<br/>= volume x rate]
  NEP --> RI[Reinsurance cost]
  LR --> ATT[Attritional]
  LR --> LG[Large and cat]
  LR --> PYD[Prior-year development]
  ER --> ACQ[Acquisition cost]
  ER --> OPS[Operating cost]
  C --> SCR[uSCR / ECA - driven by<br/>exposure, mix, reinsurance,<br/>reserve risk, operational risk]

5.2 Conversion rules to memorise

Change Value on £1bn net earned premium
1 point of loss ratio £10m a year
1 point of expense ratio £10m a year
1% better rate adequacy on renewals (if sustained) Up to about £10m a year (before any volume effect)
£1m premium debt collected 60 days earlier Small income effect, but lower credit risk and capital
10% lower capital on £600m at 12% cost of capital About £7m a year
1 FTE in London operations About £70–100k fully loaded guessing – check client data

5.3 Worked sizing – bordereaux automation

Client facts (fictional): £400m DA premium; 120 coverholders; 1,400 risk and claims bordereaux a month; 35 FTE in DA operations at £75k each = £2.6m.

Step Assumption Result
Share of DA operations effort on mapping, validation and queries 60% (from activity analysis) 21 FTE
Effort removed by ingestion and validation automation 50% of that work guessing – prove in pilot 10.5 FTE ≈ £0.8m a year
Faster exposure data (weeks earlier) Better cat aggregation and reinsurance buying Not sized in base case – mention as upside
Fewer authority breaches found late Lower leakage Upside
Expense ratio effect on £1bn NEP £0.8m ÷ £1bn 0.08 points

What a partner would say: "On its own, bordereaux automation is under a tenth of a point. It is worth doing only as part of a programme that also tackles reconciliation, policy system duplication and facility terms. Present it as one of five levers that together deliver 2 points." This honesty builds credibility.

5.4 Market-sizing drill (case-interview style)

Question: "What is the annual cost of processing delegated authority business across Lloyd's?"

Step Estimate
Lloyd's GWP 2025 £57.9bn certain
Share through DA about 45% likely → about £26bn
Cost of DA operations and oversight across carriers, as % of DA premium 1.5–3% guessing
Annual cost pool about £0.4–0.8bn
Share addressable by automation and standards 30–50% guessing
Market-wide value pool about £120–400m a year

The precision does not matter; the structure does. A partner uses this kind of estimate to judge whether a market problem deserves board attention.

6. Step 5 – Gather facts efficiently

6.1 Sources for London work

Source What you get
Syndicate annual reports and accounts (lloyds.com) Results, class mix, combined ratio, commentary – free and comparable
Lloyd's market results Market averages: combined ratio, major claims, rate change, expense trends
Client data requests Ledger, policy and claims extracts, FTE data, bordereaux logs, IT costs
Interviews How work really happens, where pain is, who matters
Process walk-throughs Watching operations teams do the work
Market bodies LMA, IUA, LIIBA, LMG publications and surveys
Regulators PRA priorities letters, FCA policy statements

6.2 Interview discipline

  • Prepare 5–7 questions linked to hypotheses (guide in Module 19 §3).
  • Ask for numbers: "How many?", "How long?", "How often?".
  • Close with: "If you were CEO, what would you change first?" and "Who else should I speak to?"
  • Write up within 24 hours: key facts, quotes, surprises, hypothesis impact.

6.3 Data request discipline

  • Ask for the minimum data that tests the hypotheses.
  • Give a template with field definitions.
  • Agree a date and an owner.
  • Expect London data to be messy: duplicated UMRs, different line bases (written vs signed), currencies, year of account vs calendar year. Agree definitions early.

7. Step 6 – Synthesise: the pyramid principle

7.1 The idea

Executives want the answer first, then the reasons, then the evidence. The pyramid principle (from Barbara Minto, a former McKinsey consultant) is the standard method:

flowchart TB
  G[Governing thought: the answer] --> K1[Key line 1]
  G --> K2[Key line 2]
  G --> K3[Key line 3]
  K1 --> E1[Evidence]
  K1 --> E2[Evidence]
  K2 --> E3[Evidence]
  K2 --> E4[Evidence]
  K3 --> E5[Evidence]
  K3 --> E6[Evidence]

Rules:

  1. Start with the answer (the governing thought).
  2. Each level summarises the level below.
  3. Ideas in each group are MECE and of the same kind.
  4. Ideas are in logical order (time, structure or importance).

7.2 Situation – Complication – Question (SCQ)

Use SCQ to open a storyline:

  • Situation: what the audience already accepts. "The syndicate delivered a 91% combined ratio in 2025."
  • Complication: what changed. "Rates are now falling 6–7% a year; the underlying ratio is 96% and rising; Lloyd's is challenging growth plans."
  • Question: what the audience needs answered. "How do we protect margin for the 2027 and 2028 years of account?"
  • Answer: the governing thought.

7.3 Worked storyline – executive summary

Governing thought: Syndicate X can restore 4 points of underlying combined ratio by the 2028 year of account by exiting two unprofitable classes, enforcing pricing discipline on follow business, and removing 2 points of expense through automation and system consolidation – worth about £40m a year on current premium.

1. The loss ratio problem is concentrated, not general. Two classes (US casualty binders and property facility business) produce 70% of the loss ratio gap to peers; the other classes perform at or better than market.

2. Pricing discipline has weakened most in follow business. Rate adequacy on follow lines fell from 102% to 91% in 18 months, against 98% on led business.

3. Expenses are 4 points above peers because of manual processing and duplicated systems. 45% of operations effort is re-keying and reconciliation; five policy systems cost £6m a year to run.

4. A two-year programme delivers £40m a year with manageable risk. Phase 1 (2027 plan): class exits and follow pricing rules – 2 points. Phase 2 (2027–28): automation and system consolidation – 2 points.

Notice: every key line is a full sentence with a conclusion, not a topic like "Loss ratio analysis".

7.4 Action titles and the "horizontal read"

Every slide has an action title – a full sentence stating the slide's conclusion. Reading only the titles of a deck, in order, should tell the full story. This is called the horizontal read (or "dot-dash" when written as bullet storyline).

Weak title (topic) Strong title (action)
"Loss ratio by class" "Two classes create 70% of the loss ratio gap to peers"
"Operations effort" "Almost half of operations effort is re-keying and reconciliation"
"Facility business" "Facility business runs 7 points worse than led business at similar rates"
"Roadmap" "A two-phase programme delivers 4 points by the 2028 year of account"

7.5 Ghost deck

Before analysis is complete, write the deck as blank slides with action titles and sketched charts. Update titles as evidence arrives. A ghost deck keeps the team focused and shows the client early where the story is going.

Ghost deck for the Syndicate X case (12 slides):

# Action title (draft) Chart
1 Restoring 4 points of combined ratio is achievable by the 2028 year of account and worth ~£40m a year Executive summary
2 Headline results hide a 96% and rising underlying combined ratio Waterfall: headline → underlying
3 The gap to peers is 2.5 points loss ratio and 4 points expense ratio Bar: client vs peer components
4 Two classes create 70% of the loss ratio gap Bar by class
5 Follow business rate adequacy has fallen 11 points in 18 months Line chart, led vs follow
6 Facility business runs 7 points worse than led business Bar by channel
7 Almost half of operations effort is re-keying and reconciliation Stacked bar of activities
8 Five policy systems cost £6m a year and duplicate data Application map
9 Five levers deliver 4 points Lever waterfall
10 Phase 1 (2027 plan) delivers 2 points with low execution risk Lever table
11 Phase 2 delivers the rest through automation and consolidation Roadmap
12 Decisions needed from the board today List

7.6 Chart choices for London work

Message Chart
Bridge from headline to underlying combined ratio Waterfall
Client vs peers on ratios Clustered bar
Trend in rate change / rate adequacy Line
Profitability by class or channel Bar sorted by value
Effort by activity Stacked bar
Value of levers Waterfall
Roadmap Gantt-style timeline

Rules: one message per chart; label data directly; source and date on every chart; show the year of account or calendar year basis clearly.

8. Step 7 – Communicate with executives

8.1 The 30-second answer

Prepare for every meeting a 30-second answer to "So what have you found?" Structure: answer → three reasons → what we need from you.

"You can get 4 points back by 2028. Two classes cause most of the loss gap, follow pricing has slipped, and operations carry 4 points of avoidable cost. We need your decision on the two classes before the 2027 plan goes to Lloyd's."

8.2 Pre-wiring

No important finding should surprise a senior stakeholder in a steering committee. Top-tier teams pre-wire: they walk each key stakeholder through the findings that affect them one to one before the meeting. In London, pre-wire the CUO before any finding about classes or underwriting, and the Head of DA before any finding about coverholders.

8.3 Handling challenge

Rule Example
Acknowledge what is true "You are right that 2024 was distorted by one large loss."
Return to data "Excluding that loss, the class still runs 6 points worse than market."
Separate fact from judgement "That figure is from your ledger. The peer comparison is our estimate from public accounts."
Offer to test "If you think mix explains it, we can normalise by sub-class by Friday."
Do not fold without new information Hold your position unless the challenge brings new facts.

See section 13 for London-specific challenges and strong responses.

8.4 Workshops

Phase Practice
Before Clear objective; pre-read; right people (decision-makers plus experts); pre-wire
During Start with the answer and the decision needed; use real data; capture decisions and actions visibly
After Send decisions and actions within 24 hours

9. Engagement architecture

9.1 Typical phases

Phase Duration Output
Diagnostic 4–6 weeks Fact base, root causes, value at stake, priorities
Design 6–10 weeks Target operating model, detailed initiatives, business case, roadmap
Implementation / transformation 6–24 months Delivered initiatives, value tracked, capability built

9.2 Team and governance

Role Responsibility
Partner / director Client relationship, answer quality, steering committee
Engagement manager Daily leadership, storyline, workplan, client management
Consultants and analysts Analyses, interviews, slides
Experts Actuarial, DA, claims, data, AI, regulatory
Client lead and counterparts Ownership of findings and implementation
Steering committee Decisions, priorities, removing blockers

9.3 Weekly rhythm

Day Activity
Monday Team problem-solving session: hypotheses, storyline, priorities
Midweek Client counterpart check-ins; interim findings
Thursday Partner review of draft pages
Friday Client lead update; plan next week
Every 2–4 weeks Steering committee

9.4 The first two weeks at a London managing agent

Day Activity
Before day 1 Read last 3 years' syndicate accounts; Lloyd's market results; client's public statements; draft day-one hypothesis; draft stakeholder map
Days 1–2 Kick-off with sponsor; agree key question, success criteria, governance; issue data request
Days 3–5 10–15 interviews: CEO, CUO, CFO, COO, Head of DA, Chief Claims Officer, CRO, 3–4 class underwriters, operations leads
Week 2 Combined ratio bridge; class and channel profitability; process walk-throughs (placement, bordereaux, technical accounting, claims); first ghost deck
End of week 2 Sponsor meeting: emerging hypotheses confirmed or dropped; plan for weeks 3–6

9.5 Implementation and value tracking

Tool Purpose
Initiative charter Owner, scope, value, milestones, risks for each initiative
Benefits register Each benefit with baseline, target, owner, timing, run-rate vs one-off
Stage gates Idea → validated → planned → executed → value realised
Value tracking dashboard Reported to steering committee monthly

London-specific rule: link benefits to the business plan (SBF) and to year of account results, so the CFO and Lloyd's see them in the numbers they already use.

10. Selling and shaping work

10.1 Where London market work comes from

Trigger Typical buyer Typical engagement
Lloyd's plan challenge or poor results CEO, CUO Underwriting performance review
Soft market margin pressure CEO, CFO, COO Cost and operating model transformation
Regulatory finding or new rules CRO, CCO Remediation, governance
Market change (Blueprint Two retired) COO, CIO Roadmap re-plan, data standards
M&A CEO, CFO Integration
New syndicate or MGA Founders, investors Business plan and set-up
Catastrophe Claims, CFO Surge support, claims review
AI ambition without governance CEO, CDO, CRO AI strategy, governance, scaling

10.2 The point-of-view meeting

Top-tier partners rarely open with "how can we help?". They bring a perspective: a short fact-based view of the client's situation from public data (syndicate accounts, market results), a hypothesis about the issue, and an offer to test it. Example opener: "Your underlying combined ratio has risen 4 points in two years, faster than the market. Our hypothesis is that follow business and facility terms explain most of it. We would like to test that with you in four weeks."

10.3 Proposal structure

  1. The client's situation and question – in their numbers.
  2. Our hypothesis and why we believe it.
  3. Approach: phases, analyses, decision points.
  4. Outcomes and how they will be measured.
  5. Team and relevant experience (your artefacts and case studies).
  6. Timeline and commercials.

11. Know the buyer – what each executive worries about

Role Top concerns in late 2026 likely Numbers they watch Opening question that earns respect
CEO Margin in a softening market; Lloyd's plan approval; growth vs discipline; talent Combined ratio, RoC, GWP, expense ratio "Where in your portfolio are rates now below technical price, and what is your plan for those classes in 2027?"
CUO Rate adequacy, mix, lead position, facility/follow performance RARC, rate adequacy, loss ratio by class, hit/retention "How do you separate your lead performance from your follow performance in MI?"
CFO Capital efficiency, reserves, cost, reporting RoC, ECA, expense ratio, aged debt, reserve releases "How much capital is tied up in open or deteriorating years?"
COO Cost per transaction, resilience, post-Blueprint Two roadmap, outsourcing Cost per policy/claim, FTE per £m GWP, backlogs "How many times is a single contract keyed across your systems?"
CRO Internal model credibility, aggregation, cyber/AI accumulation, PRA scrutiny SCR coverage, RDS, PML, validation findings "How does your internal model reflect falling rates in the 2027 plan year?"
Chief Claims Officer Leakage, DCA oversight, cat surge, litigation, social inflation Cycle time, leakage, reserve development "What share of your claims spend do you see at file level vs only through bordereaux?"
Head of DA Bordereaux quality, coverholder performance, audits Timeliness, data quality, GPI use, breaches "Which 10 coverholders drive most of your oversight effort, and is that where your risk is?"
CDO / CIO Data platform, standards, AI governance, legacy Data quality, run cost, delivery speed "Which data domain, if fixed, would unlock the most use cases?"
Compliance PS25/21 reclassification, sanctions, SM&CR Breaches, complaints "Have you re-mapped your products to the new FCA customer definitions?"
Broker leadership Facility income, placement efficiency, data ownership Revenue per placement, time to place "Which of your placement steps still depends on re-keying by the carrier?"

12. Twenty numbers to know before any London market meeting

# Metric Current reference
1 Lloyd's GWP £57.9bn FY2025; £34.7bn H1 2026 certain
2 Lloyd's combined ratio 87.6% FY2025; 90.8% H1 2026 certain
3 Lloyd's risk-adjusted rate change −6.7% H1 2026 certain
4 Lloyd's 2026 forecast GWP £64bn ±5%; CR 90–95% likely
5 London market size ~$187bn GWP 2024 likely
6 Company market premium £49.3bn 2024 likely
7 Lloyd's 2026 stamp capacity / approved capital ~£58bn / ~£36.2bn likely
8 DA share of Lloyd's premium ~45% likely
9 ECA uplift 35% on uSCR certain
10 Lloyd's RoC ambition >12% over a 10-year cycle likely
11–20 Client's own: combined ratio split; expense ratio vs peers; GWP by class and channel; lead vs follow split; aged debt > 90 days; bordereaux timeliness; quote turnaround; claims cycle time; number of systems; AI use cases in production Client data

13. Client challenge – strong responses

# Client challenge Strong answer
1 "You're a consultant – have you ever placed a risk?" "No – I'm not a broker or underwriter. My value is seeing the full flow across placement, accounting and claims, and where cost and leakage build up between your teams. Let me show you what we found in your bordereaux data."
2 "London is relationship-driven; AI won't change that." "Agreed – relationships drive the lead decision. AI's value is in the work around the decision – extraction, checks, triage – which takes most of your underwriting assistants' time."
3 "Blueprint Two failed, so why invest in data now?" "The market programme stopped; your data problem didn't. CDR-aligned data, ingestion and reconciliation pay back inside your firm whatever the market does next."
4 "Our expense ratio is high because of Lloyd's costs." "Lloyd's charges are part of it, but in similar firms most of the gap is internal manual processing and system duplication. Let's split the gap into what you control and what you don't."
5 "Facilities give us cheap premium." "They give volume at low acquisition effort. The question is margin: in your data facility business runs X points worse than your led business. Let's test whether that is mix or selection."
6 "We already have an AI policy." "Good start. How many use cases are registered, which are high-risk, and who is the accountable senior manager for each?"
7 "The PRA won't allow that model change." "The PRA's 2026 focus is models overstating profitability in a softening market. If our change makes assumptions more conservative and better evidenced, it supports the dialogue."
8 "Our coverholders won't change their bordereaux format." "You don't need them to change first. Ingestion maps their formats to your standard. Use binder renewals to add data requirements over 12–24 months."
9 "This benchmark doesn't apply to us – we're different." "Which part – class mix, channel, or scale? Let's normalise for those and see what gap remains."
10 "Show me the payback." Business case with explicit assumptions, baseline, low/base/high cases and a measurable 90-day checkpoint.

14. Phrases that signal London fluency

Phrase When to use
"Is that a lead or follow position?" Underwriting performance
"Written or signed line?" Placement and premium
"Bureau or non-bureau?" Accounting and settlement
"Which year of account?" Any Lloyd's results discussion
"Within the binder's authority?" Delegated authority
"Who are the claims agreement parties – and is it standard or complex?" Claims on subscription business
"Before or after reserve releases?" Combined ratio discussions
"Rate adequacy, not just rate change?" Pricing
"Open market, DA or facility?" Channel performance
"In scope after PS25/21?" Conduct

16. Full worked case – Syndicate X: restoring margin in a softening market

Fictional client built from typical London market patterns.

16.1 Situation

Syndicate X: £1.2bn GWP, £1.0bn net earned premium, 2025 combined ratio 91%. Mix: 30% US property (half via binders, half open market), 25% US casualty (mainly binders), 20% marine and energy (mostly led), 15% specialty (mixed), 10% property treaty reinsurance. About 30% of property premium comes through broker facilities.

16.2 Day-one hypothesis

"The 91% headline hides an underlying ratio near 96%. The problem is concentrated in delegated casualty and facility property, plus an expense base inflated by manual DA and accounting processes. Fixing both is worth 4 points."

16.3 Key analyses and findings

Combined ratio bridge (2025)

Item Points
Headline combined ratio 91.0
Remove benign cat year (cat 3 pts vs 6-pt normalised) +3.0
Remove prior-year releases +2.0
Underlying (normalised) combined ratio 96.0

Loss ratio by class and channel (normalised)

Segment Loss ratio vs market estimate
Marine & energy (led) 52% Better by 4
Specialty 58% In line
Property treaty 55% In line
US property – open market 57% In line
US property – facilities 66% Worse by 7
US casualty – binders 74% Worse by 9

Expense analysis

Item Finding
Operating expense ratio 12% vs peer estimate 8%
Activity analysis 45% of operations effort is re-keying, reconciliation and bordereaux mapping
Systems Five policy administration systems after two acquisitions; £6m a year run cost
Aged debt 14% of premium receivable older than 90 days

16.4 Recommendations and value

Lever Action Points £m a year
1. Casualty binder remediation Exit 3 worst coverholders; re-price and tighten authority on the rest 1.2 12
2. Facility discipline Cap facility share; require lead selection rights and risk-level data 0.8 8
3. Operations automation Submission and bordereaux ingestion; reconciliation automation 1.0 10
4. System consolidation Move to one policy platform over 24 months 0.6 6
5. Credit control Automated cash matching and broker scorecards 0.4 4 (incl. lower credit risk)
Total 4.0 ~40

16.5 Roadmap

Phase Timing Focus
1 Q4 2026 – Q1 2027 Binder and facility actions in 2027 SBF; credit control quick wins
2 2027 Ingestion and reconciliation automation; consolidation design
3 2027–2028 System consolidation; value locked into the 2028 plan

16.6 Risks and mitigations

Risk Mitigation
Broker reaction to facility caps Pre-wire with top 3 brokers; position as data partnership
Premium loss from binder exits Accept: unprofitable premium; show RoC improvement
Delivery risk on system consolidation Phase by class; keep legacy for run-off
Lloyd's view of lower GWP Present improved underlying ratio and RoC in SBF

17. Practice drills

Drill How
Problem statement Rewrite three vague client asks ("help with DA", "AI strategy", "reduce cost") as full problem statements
Issue tree Build MECE trees for: "Why is our claims cycle time long?"; "How can we grow profitably in a soft market?"
Sizing Estimate: annual cost of premium reconciliation across the London market; value of 1-day faster quote turnaround for a property team
Storyline Turn a syndicate teardown (Module 19 §1) into a 5-slide action-title storyline
30-second answer Record yourself answering "So what?" for each scenario in Module 10
Challenge practice Use the AI sparring prompt in Module 19 §6 with the CEO role

18. What separates a partner from a good consultant

Good consultant Partner
Answers the question asked Checks whether it is the right question
Presents analysis Presents a decision
Explains the process Explains the value
Knows the client's problem Knows the client's industry, competitors and regulator
Avoids conflict Holds a position under pressure, changes it only on new facts
Delivers the project Builds the relationship that leads to the next three projects
Uses frameworks visibly Uses frameworks invisibly – the client sees only clear thinking

19. Key takeaways

  1. Write a precise problem statement before any analysis.
  2. Structure with MECE issue trees; test with hypotheses; prioritise the 20% of analyses that matter.
  3. Size everything: 1 point of combined ratio on £1bn is £10m a year.
  4. Communicate answer-first with the pyramid principle and action titles.
  5. Pre-wire stakeholders; hold your position unless new facts arrive.
  6. Link value to the business plan and the year of account – the language of London CFOs and Lloyd's.

20. Check your understanding

# Question Answer
1 What makes a key question good? Specific, measurable, time-bound, with a clear decision-maker and success criteria.
2 Difference between diagnostic and solution trees? Diagnostic asks why; solution asks how.
3 What is a ghost chart? A sketch of the expected chart before data, to focus data collection.
4 What is SCQ? Situation, Complication, Question – opening structure for a storyline.
5 What is the horizontal read? Reading only slide titles should tell the whole story.
6 Value of 0.5 points of expense ratio on £800m NEP? £4m a year.
7 Why pre-wire? No senior stakeholder should be surprised in a steering committee; it builds support and surfaces objections early.

21. Further reading

  • Barbara Minto, The Pyramid Principle – structured thinking and writing.
  • Ethan Rasiel, The McKinsey Way – working practices of a top-tier firm.
  • Charles Conn and Robert McLean, Bulletproof Problem Solving – issue trees and hypothesis-driven work.
  • Gene Zelazny, Say It with Charts – business charts.
  • Syndicate annual reports and accounts on lloyds.com – your best free practice data.
Module 10

Business Scenarios and Case Studies
Level All – the bridge from knowledge to consulting
Path phase Phases 2–4 (Module 18)
Time 45–60 minutes per case
Prerequisites Modules 01–09 (each case lists its main links)
You will be able to Solve London market business problems the way a top-tier consulting team would: define the question, form a hypothesis, structure the problem, choose analyses, interpret findings, recommend, size value and anticipate objections

1. How to use this chapter

Each case follows the same structure used in top-tier consulting work (Module 11):

  1. Situation – facts and numbers.
  2. Key question – precise, measurable, time-bound.
  3. Day-one hypothesis – your best guess before analysis.
  4. Issue tree – how to break the problem down.
  5. Market mechanics – the London-specific knowledge you need.
  6. Analyses and illustrative findings.
  7. Recommendation and value.
  8. Risks and objections.
  9. Questions to ask the client.
  10. The trap – the mistake most people make.

Method: read only the situation and key question. Spend 30 minutes writing your hypothesis, issue tree, analyses and recommendation. Then compare. Cases are fictional unless they describe a named real event; real events are tagged.

Scoring rubric for your answers

Criterion 1 – weak 3 – good 5 – partner level
Problem definition Restates topic Clear question Question tied to a decision and a deadline
Structure List of ideas MECE tree MECE tree with the right first split
London fluency Generic insurance Correct terms Uses market mechanics to shape the answer
Quantification None Some numbers Value sized in ratio points and £m, with assumptions
Recommendation Options only One recommendation Recommendation with phasing, owners, risks
Communication Bottom-up Answer stated Pyramid: answer, three reasons, evidence

Case 1 – Restoring margin in a softening market

Links: Modules 01, 04, 07, 11

Situation. Syndicate A writes £900m GWP (£750m net earned). 2025 combined ratio 89%. Cat losses were light and reserve releases contributed 3 points. Risk-adjusted rates are falling about 6–7% a year across its main classes [likely – market level]. The CEO must submit the 2027 business plan (SBF) to Lloyd's.

Key question. How should Syndicate A shape its 2027 plan to protect a combined ratio below 95% on a normalised basis while keeping Lloyd's approval?

Day-one hypothesis. The underlying ratio is already about 95%; rate falls will push it above 97% in 2027 unless the syndicate shrinks under-priced classes, protects terms, and takes 1–2 points out of expenses.

Issue tree.

flowchart LR
  Q[Keep normalised CR < 95% in 2027] --> P[Price and terms]
  Q --> M[Portfolio mix]
  Q --> C[Cost]
  Q --> R[Reinsurance and capital]
  P --> P1[Rate adequacy by class]
  P --> P2[Terms: deductibles, limits, exclusions]
  M --> M1[Shrink below-technical classes]
  M --> M2[Grow classes still adequate]
  M --> M3[Lead vs follow vs facility mix]
  C --> C1[Operating expense]
  C --> C2[Acquisition cost]
  R --> R1[Cheaper reinsurance in soft market]
  R --> R2[Capital efficiency]

Market mechanics. Lloyd's approves the SBF and in 2026 is warning that rate adequacy for 2027 is under threat likely; the PRA is watching internal models that may overstate profitability likely. Plans with unrealistic loss ratios will be challenged.

Analyses and illustrative findings.

Analysis Finding
Combined ratio bridge 89% headline → 95% normalised (cat +3, releases +3)
Rate adequacy by class 3 of 8 classes below 95% of technical price
Channel split Facility business 6 points worse than led business
Expense benchmark Operating expense ratio 2 points above peer estimate
Reinsurance pricing Cat reinsurance cheaper by about 10% vs 2025 guessing

Recommendation.

  1. Reduce the three under-priced classes by 25–40% in the 2027 plan.
  2. Cap facility share; require lead-selection rights and risk-level data.
  3. Hold terms: set minimum deductibles and exclusions by class.
  4. Buy additional cat protection while cheap to release capital.
  5. Launch a cost programme for 1.5 points over two years.

Value. About 3 points of normalised combined ratio by 2028 ≈ £22m a year on £750m NEP; plus capital released.

Risks and objections. Premium shrinkage worries the board and capital providers → present return on capital, not GWP. Brokers push back on facility caps → pre-wire with key brokers.

Questions to ask. "Which classes are below technical price today?" "How much of your 2025 result was cat luck and reserve releases?" "What does your plan assume for rate change in 2027?"

The trap. Recommending growth because headline results are strong.

Case 2 – A coverholder stops reporting

Links: Modules 05, 06

Situation. A Florida coverholder writing homeowners' business for three syndicates (Syndicate B leads with 40%) has not sent risk bordereaux for three months. Last known GPI used: £38m of a £50m limit. Hurricane season starts in four weeks.

Key question. What must Syndicate B do in the next 30 days to protect its exposure position, and how should it prevent a repeat?

Day-one hypothesis. Exposure is understated by at least 20%; the coverholder may be in operational or financial difficulty; oversight is not risk-based.

Market mechanics. BAA terms set reporting deadlines and remedies; Lloyd's expects registration, oversight and coordinated audits where binders are shared likely; the lead managing agent coordinates.

Analyses and illustrative findings.

Analysis Finding
Exposure estimate Trend suggests GPI now about £47m – near limit; coastal share rising
Remittance Premium remittances also 60 days late
Oversight history Coverholder rated "medium" – last audit 26 months ago
Portfolio scan 6 other coverholders have bordereaux more than 45 days late

Recommendation.

  • Immediate: formal notice; data within 10 days; restrict new coastal business; inform exposure and reinsurance teams; check remittance and risk transfer terms.
  • Short term: coordinated audit with the other two syndicates.
  • Structural: automated bordereaux tracking; late data triggers higher risk tier; BAA renewal adds data and remittance requirements.

Value. Avoids unpriced cat exposure; reduces credit risk; oversight effort refocused on high-risk partners.

Questions to ask. "How many coverholders could be in this position today without you knowing?" "Are binder terms held as structured data?"

The trap. Treating it as an admin delay rather than an exposure and credit event.

Case 3 – Hurricane: claims surge, capital and reinsurance

Links: Modules 06, 07

Situation. A major hurricane hits the US Gulf Coast. Syndicate C (property binders and open market property) estimates a £180m gross loss; after quota share and per-risk reinsurance, £130m falls to its catastrophe programme. Its cat XoL is £150m xs £40m with one reinstatement at 100%; annual premium £15m.

Key question. How should Syndicate C manage claims, recoveries, liquidity and its plan in the next 90 days?

Day-one hypothesis. The gross loss estimate is probably low because DA claims data lags; recoveries and reinstatement premiums are not yet fully modelled; DCA capacity is the main operational risk.

Market mechanics. Event coding; major loss reporting to Lloyd's likely; claims agreed by the lead (and second lead for complex claims) under the Lloyd's Claims Lead Arrangements; reinstatement premium on cat XoL.

Illustrative calculation. With £130m falling to the cat programme: recovery = £130m − £40m = £90m; reinstatement premium = £15m × 90/150 = £9m; net retained = £40m + £9m reinstatement.

Recommendation.

  • Daily loss estimate updates; event-coded claims data model.
  • Pre-agreed DCA surge capacity; triage simple vs complex claims.
  • Reinsurance recovery tracking by event; accrue reinstatement premiums.
  • Liquidity plan; communicate early with Lloyd's and capital providers.
  • Review the 2027 plan: pricing, aggregates and reinsurance purchase.

Questions to ask. "How quickly can you produce a reliable gross and net loss estimate?" "Are DA claims linked to events and locations?"

The trap. Forgetting reinstatement premiums and DA claims lag in early loss estimates.

Case 4 – Systemic cyber event

Links: Modules 03, 09

Situation. A faulty software update causes a global IT outage (similar in nature to the July 2024 CrowdStrike outage certain as an event type). Syndicate D writes SME cyber through MGAs and large corporate cyber in the open market.

Key question. What is Syndicate D's exposure, and how should it change wordings and accumulation management?

Day-one hypothesis. Losses depend on whether wordings cover non-malicious system failure and on waiting periods; accumulation is poorly measured because technology dependency data is not captured.

Market mechanics. Lloyd's requires state-backed cyber attack exclusions (from March 2023) certain – not relevant here because the event is not malicious, but always checked; event definitions in reinsurance determine recoveries.

Recommendation. Wording inventory (system failure cover, waiting periods); collect technology dependency data at underwriting; scenario tests; align reinsurance event definitions; apply the same approach to AI systemic risk.

Questions to ask. "Which wordings cover non-malicious failure?" "Do you know which insureds depend on which vendors?"

The trap. Assuming cyber losses only come from attacks.

Case 5 – Aircraft stranded in Russia (real case)

Links: Modules 03, 07

Facts likely. After Russia's 2022 invasion of Ukraine, hundreds of leased aircraft remained in Russia. In June 2025 the High Court found lessors could recover under war risks cover rather than all risks; AerCap recovered just over $1bn. Permission to appeal was refused in September 2025, but the Court of Appeal granted permission in March 2026. Separate related proceedings continued in 2026.

Key question (for an exposed insurer). How should the insurer manage reserves, reinsurance and wording lessons from this litigation?

Lessons.

  1. The cause of loss and the exact wording decide which panel pays (war vs all risks).
  2. Sanctions can block payments; claims processes need sanctions checks.
  3. Litigation reserves remain uncertain for years.
  4. Wording analytics and political risk aggregation have real value.

Questions to ask. "What is your reserve range for the remaining litigation and how does reinsurance respond?" "Which other aviation and marine contingent covers have similar political exposure?"

The trap. Treating it as an aviation-only issue rather than a wording and aggregation lesson for all political and war exposures.

Case 6 – Launching a new syndicate

Links: Modules 02, 07

Situation. A US specialty insurer wants a Lloyd's platform for licences and rating. Thirteen new syndicates started on 1 January 2026 likely; capital is entering the market while rates soften.

Key question. Should the insurer enter Lloyd's in 2027, and through which structure?

Options.

Option Pros Cons
Own managing agent + syndicate Control Cost; time; Lloyd's approval burden
Syndicate-in-a-Box with a turnkey agent Faster; lower fixed cost Less control; reliance on host
Corporate member backing existing syndicates Fast capital deployment No underwriting control
SPA on a host syndicate Simple Follows host's underwriting

Recommendation logic. In a softening market, a distinctive underwriting edge is essential. If the insurer has a proven specialist team and a reason for Lloyd's licences, start with a SIAB or turnkey arrangement and plan modest first-year premium; otherwise deploy capital via a corporate member or SPA and revisit in a harder market.

Questions to ask. "Why Lloyd's rather than a company platform or fronting?" "What is your differentiated underwriting edge at falling rates?"

The trap. Building a plan on 2023–2024 pricing.

Case 7 – Closing a problematic year of account

Links: Module 07

Situation. Syndicate E's 2023 year of account has deteriorating US casualty reserves (social inflation). The board must decide whether to close it by RITC at end-2025 or leave it open.

Key question. Close, leave open, or transfer to a third party – which option is best for members of all affected years?

Analysis. Independent reserve review with a range; RITC price under each reserve scenario; capital effect of leaving open; quotes for LPT/ADC or third-party RITC.

Recommendation logic. If the reserve range is wide but quantifiable, buy an ADC to cap the tail and close by RITC to the 2024 year at a fair price. If the range cannot be estimated, keep open temporarily but set a plan and timeline; explore third-party RITC.

Questions to ask. "What drives the reserve uncertainty?" "How are members of different years treated fairly?"

The trap. Choosing the option that flatters one year's members at the expense of another's.

Case 8 – Cutting the cost of doing business

Links: Modules 06, 08, 11

Situation. A mid-size managing agent's operating expense ratio is 4 points above peers. The CEO wants 2 points out in two years without hurting underwriting.

Key question. Where can 2 points of expense ratio be removed by the 2028 year of account, and in what order?

Day-one hypothesis. Half of the gap is manual processing (re-keying, reconciliation, bordereaux), a quarter is duplicated systems after M&A, a quarter is scale and Lloyd's costs.

Analyses. Activity analysis; cost by value chain stage; FTE per £m GWP by function; application cost map; aged debt analysis.

Typical levers guessing.

Lever Typical impact
Submission and bordereaux automation 15–30% of affected operations effort
Reconciliation automation 20–40% of reconciliation effort
Shared services / nearshore 20–30% labour cost in moved activities
Application rationalisation 10–20% IT run cost
Risk-based DA oversight Fewer audits; lower external spend

The trap. Cutting underwriting support so far that underwriters lose time for selection – costing more in loss ratio than it saves.

Case 9 – After Blueprint Two: what now?

Links: Module 08

Situation. A managing agent froze its operations roadmap in 2024 waiting for Blueprint Two. In March 2026 Lloyd's retired the programme likely. The COO asks: "What now?"

Recommendation structure.

  1. Inventory what was built: CDR mapping, gateway integration, data fixes – keep what improves internal data.
  2. Re-baseline the roadmap on internal value: ingestion, data platform, reconciliation, DA data.
  3. Stay standards-aligned (CDR, ACORD) and integrate through APIs and messages.
  4. Engage through LMA/LMG on market direction.
  5. Apply an 18–24-month payback rule.

The trap. Either waiting again for the market, or abandoning standards altogether.

Case 10 – AI submission triage

Links: Module 09

Situation. A property team receives 300 submissions a week and quotes 30%. Underwriters spend most of their time reading emails and spreadsheets.

Approach.

  1. Baseline: time per submission, quote and bind rates, loss ratio of bound risks.
  2. Test set from 3 months of historical submissions; target ≥ 95% accuracy on key fields.
  3. Appetite scoring trained on profitability, not just past acceptance.
  4. Embed in the workbench; track overrides.
  5. Governance: medium risk tier; human decision; monthly accuracy review.

KPIs. Time to first review; % reviewed within 24 hours; hit ratio on top-scored submissions; underwriter time on analysis vs admin.

The trap. Scoring that copies past bias and speeds up binding of under-priced risks.

Case 11 – Broker facilities and follow performance

Links: Module 04

Situation. 30% of Syndicate F's property premium comes from broker facilities. Facility business runs 7 points worse than its led business at similar headline rates.

Key question. Should Syndicate F stay in the facilities, and on what terms?

Analyses. Loss ratio by channel normalised for mix; rate adequacy by channel; performance of leads followed; data rights; exit terms.

Recommendation logic. Keep facilities that follow strong leads and provide risk-level data; renegotiate or exit the rest; cap facility share of each class.

The trap. Valuing facility premium for its low acquisition effort while ignoring selection.

Case 12 – Conduct rules after PS25/21

Links: Modules 05, 07

Situation. A managing agent's coverholders sell to consumers, SMEs and larger commercial customers. Compliance asks what changes after FCA PS25/21 (December 2025).

Answer outline [certain for the rule; likely for practical effect].

  • Larger commercial customers above DISP SME thresholds and contracts of large risks are outside ICOBS, PROD and Consumer Duty; consumers and SMEs remain in scope.
  • Reclassify products and books; simplify controls for out-of-scope books; keep product governance for in-scope books.
  • Co-manufacturing: insurers can nominate one lead for PROD 4; MGAs cannot be lead.

Question to ask. "Do your bordereaux capture customer size data to classify each risk?" (Often they do not.)

The trap. Assuming all commercial business is now out of scope.

Case 13 – Major marine loss (real event)

Facts certain. In March 2024 the container ship Dali struck and collapsed the Francis Scott Key Bridge in Baltimore. Losses involve hull, cargo, P&I (through the International Group of P&I Clubs and its reinsurance programme, heavily placed in London), bridge property, business interruption and liability. It is expected to be among the largest marine insurance losses likely.

Lessons. One event triggers many classes and reinsurance layers; liability limitation laws and litigation decide the final cost; London reinsurers of the International Group programme carry significant exposure.

Question to ask. "How do you capture clash exposure across marine, property and reinsurance for the same port or asset?"

Case 14 – MGA capacity strategy

Links: Module 05

Situation. A UK specialty MGA writes £80m GWP on a single Lloyd's binder. The lead syndicate signals it will cut capacity by 40% for 2027 due to soft-market concerns.

Key question. How should the MGA secure capacity for 2027–2029?

Options. Add a second Lloyd's lead and company market capacity (multi-carrier panel); fronting carrier plus reinsurance; multi-year capacity deal with profit commission and retained risk; slow growth.

Recommendation logic. Build a panel of two to three capacity providers; offer alignment (retained share, profit-linked commission, risk-level data, API reporting); show underwriting performance by segment.

The trap. Competing on commission rather than on data and alignment.

15. Scenario-solving checklist (use every time)

  1. Clarify the decision and the deadline.
  2. Map actors and flows: risk, money, data.
  3. Quantify the problem in ratio points, £m and capital.
  4. Hypothesise the answer on day one.
  5. Structure with a MECE tree; pick the right first split.
  6. Analyse the 20% that gives 80% of insight.
  7. Recommend one option, phased, with owners.
  8. Size value and name assumptions.
  9. Anticipate objections from Lloyd's, regulators, brokers, followers and staff.
  10. Communicate answer-first.

16. Partner's lens

How a senior top-tier consultant would use these cases.

  1. Every case is a P&L case. Even "operational" problems (late bordereaux, aged debt) are exposure, credit or capital problems. Find the money first.
  2. The market constrains the answer. Lloyd's plan approval, the Claims Lead Arrangements, the bureau, brokers and followers all limit what one firm can do. Strong answers name the constraint and work with it.
  3. Timing beats perfection. Many London decisions have fixed windows (SBF, renewals, Coming into Line, hurricane season). A good answer delivered in time beats a perfect answer delivered late.
  4. Practise out loud. Partners can give a 30-second answer to any case. Record yourself on each case until you can.

17. Practice tasks

  1. Solve Cases 1, 2 and 8 under time pressure (30 minutes each) and score yourself with the rubric.
  2. Write a one-page answer-first recommendation for Case 9.
  3. Build the value model for Case 1 in a spreadsheet.
  4. Ask a practitioner to review one of your case answers and score it.

  • Insurance Insider – Lloyd's 2026 stamp capacity
  • FCA PS25/21
  • Keoghs – Lloyd's Claims Lead Arrangements
Module 19

Practice Toolkit and Templates

Purpose: The working tools for the path in Module 18. Each template is built for repeated practice. Use only public or fictional data – never real client data in personal practice or public AI tools.

1. Syndicate teardown (every two weeks)

Lloyd's publishes annual reports and accounts for each syndicate on lloyds.com certain. A teardown trains pillar B (financial acumen) faster than anything else.

1.1 Method (60–90 minutes)

Step What to extract Where in the accounts
1 Managing agent, syndicate number, owner group, capacity Front section / managing agent's report
2 Gross written premium; growth vs prior year Managing agent's report, income statement
3 Business mix by class and territory Segmental notes
4 Combined ratio; split into claims ratio and expense ratio Key performance indicators; technical account
5 Major/catastrophe losses mentioned Managing agent's report
6 Prior-year reserve releases or strengthening Notes on claims development
7 Rate change commentary Managing agent's report
8 Reinsurance strategy and cost Report and notes
9 Investment return Non-technical/technical account
10 Principal risks and outlook Risk section, outlook

1.2 Teardown sheet

Syndicate: ____   Managing agent: ____   Year: ____
GWP: £____ (growth __%)        Net earned premium: £____
Claims ratio: __%   Expense ratio: __%   Combined ratio: __%
  of which: major losses __ pts | prior-year releases __ pts
Underlying CR (ex major, ex PYD): __%
Top 3 classes: ____ / ____ / ____      Top territory: ____
Rate change stated: __%
Reinsurance comment: ____
Investment return: £____ ( __%)

THREE INSIGHTS
1. ____
2. ____
3. ____

IF I WERE ADVISING THE CEO, MY FIRST QUESTION WOULD BE:
____

WHERE AN OPERATIONS / DATA / AI CHANGE WOULD MOVE THE NUMBERS:
____ (which ratio, rough size, why)

1.3 Progression

  • Months 1–3: one syndicate at a time; get the numbers right.
  • Months 4–9: compare two syndicates writing similar classes; explain the difference.
  • Months 9+: compare a managing agent against the Lloyd's market average; write a one-page "board briefing".

2. Market news "so-what" note (3 per week)

Headline: ____                            Date: ____   Source: ____
FACT (what happened, with numbers): ____
CONFIDENCE: <span class="tag tag-certain">certain</span> / <span class="tag tag-likely">likely</span> / <span class="tag tag-guessing">guessing</span>
SO WHAT for:
  - Managing agents: ____
  - Brokers: ____
  - Coverholders/MGAs: ____
  - My spike (AI ops / DA & data): ____
CLIENT QUESTION THIS CREATES: ____
DOES THIS CHANGE ANY POSITION IN MY POVs? Yes/No – how: ____

Example (completed):

Headline: Lloyd's H1 2026 – risk-adjusted rates down 6.7%     Source: Lloyd's HY2026 release
FACT: CR 90.8%; underlying CR rose to ~84% from ~82%; major claims ratio 6.8%.
CONFIDENCE: <span class="tag tag-certain">certain</span> headline; <span class="tag tag-likely">likely</span> underlying split
SO WHAT:
  - Managing agents: margins depend more on expense control and selection; plan challenge from Lloyd's likely tougher for 2027.
  - Brokers: more capacity, more facility deployment; pressure on terms.
  - MGAs: carriers will scrutinise binder loss ratios harder.
  - My spike: cost-per-transaction and bordereaux automation business cases strengthen.
CLIENT QUESTION: "Which classes are now below technical price, and what does it cost you to process each risk?"
CHANGES MY POV? Yes – soft-market playbook should lead with expense ratio, not growth.

3. Practitioner interview guide (20–30 minutes)

Use for network conversations and for engagement discovery.

Section Questions
Role and context What does your team own? What volumes do you handle (risks, bordereaux, claims, transactions) per month?
Process Walk me through a typical item from arrival to completion. Where does it wait? Where is it re-keyed?
Pain points What breaks most often? What do you spend time on that should not need a person?
Data Which data do you trust least? Where does it come from? Who fixes it?
Controls What are you most worried an auditor, Lloyd's or the PRA would find?
Change What has been tried before? Why did it not stick?
AI Where do people already use AI tools informally? Where would you never trust it?
Measures Which numbers does your boss look at every month?
Close If you had one change funded tomorrow, what would it be? Who else should I speak to?

After every conversation: write five lines – the biggest insight, one number, one quote, one surprise, one follow-up.

4. Point-of-view (POV) paper template (2–3 pages)

TITLE: a clear position, not a topic
  Weak: "AI in delegated authority"
  Strong: "Bordereaux are a data product, not a report – and AI only helps once you treat them that way"

1. THE ISSUE (half page)
   What is happening in the market, with dated facts and sources.

2. WHY IT MATTERS (half page)
   Impact on P&L, capital, regulation or client service – with numbers or ranges.

3. OUR POSITION (half page)
   The claim. What most firms get wrong. Why.

4. WHAT TO DO (1 page)
   3–5 actions, each with: who owns it, first step, how to measure it, 90-day checkpoint.

5. RISKS AND OBJECTIONS (quarter page)
   The strongest counter-argument and your answer.

6. QUESTIONS FOR YOUR BOARD (quarter page)
   4–5 questions an executive can take into their next meeting.

Quality checks before publishing: every number sourced and dated; confidence tags on judgements; no vendor promotion; no confidential client information; reviewed by one practitioner.

Starter POV list (aligned to the recommended spikes):

  1. After Blueprint Two – five no-regret moves for managing agents.
  2. Soft-market playbook: protect margin through expense ratio and selection.
  3. Bordereaux are a data product, not a report.
  4. From AI pilots to production in the Lloyd's market – governance first, then scale.
  5. Silent AI: the exposure London has not priced.
  6. The real cost of a London market transaction – and how to halve it.

5. Value case template

Section Content
Baseline Current volumes, effort (FTE/hours), cost, cycle times, error rates, financial metrics
Levers What changes (automation, process redesign, data standard) and the mechanism
Benefits – cost FTE capacity released, external spend avoided, IT run cost
Benefits – financial Loss ratio, aged debt (cash and credit risk), reinsurance recoveries, capital
Benefits – risk and compliance Fewer breaches, audit findings, regulatory exposure
Costs Build/buy, run, change management, data remediation
Assumptions Each with source and confidence tag
Sensitivity Low / base / high cases
Checkpoints 90-day, 6-month, 12-month measurable milestones
Owner Named business owner of each benefit

Rule: show cost savings and loss ratio/growth benefits separately. CFOs trust the first and discount the second.

6. AI sparring partner prompts

Use an AI assistant to rehearse executive conversations and test your understanding. Paste the prompt, then answer as yourself.

6.1 Executive challenge drill

You are the Chief Operating Officer of a mid-sized Lloyd's managing agent.
You are sceptical of consultants and of AI. Your expense ratio is above peers,
the market is softening, and Lloyd's has retired Blueprint Two.
Interview me for 15 minutes about how I would reduce your operating cost.
Push back hard on vague answers, ask for numbers, and challenge any outdated
market facts. At the end, score me 1-10 on domain credibility, financial
reasoning, and practicality, with specific feedback.

6.2 Concept test

Ask me 10 questions, one at a time, on [topic: e.g., RITC and year of account].
Start easy and get harder. After each answer, tell me what was right, what was
missing, and what an experienced London market practitioner would add.

6.3 POV red team

Here is my point-of-view paper: [paste]. Act as a sceptical Lloyd's CUO.
Find the three weakest claims, any facts that need sources, and the strongest
counter-argument. Do not rewrite it – critique it.

6.4 Process walk-through check

I will describe the London market process for [e.g., bordereaux processing
under a binding authority] step by step. Identify errors, missing steps,
missing controls, and where data quality problems usually arise.

Caution: AI assistants can be confidently wrong about London market specifics. Check anything important against this guide's sources, Lloyd's publications or a practitioner. Treat the AI as a sparring partner, not an authority.

Module 12

Glossary A–Z

Plain-English definitions of the terms you will hear in London market and Lloyd's work. Cross-references point to the module with more detail.

Term Meaning
ACORD Global insurance data standards body; London uses ACORD GRLC messages and the CDR. (08)
Acquisition cost Brokerage, commission and other costs of obtaining business. (01)
Additional premium (AP) Extra premium from an endorsement. (04)
ADC – Adverse development cover Reinsurance protecting against reserves developing worse than expected. (07)
Agreement party Insurer whose agreement is needed for contract changes or claims under the subscription agreement. (04, 06)
Aggregate Total exposure or total losses across many policies or a period.
Agreed value Policy where the value is fixed at inception (marine, fine art). (15)
Aligned capital Capital provided by the managing agent's own group. (02)
Average Reduction of a claim in proportion to under-insurance. (15)
BAA – Binding authority agreement Contract delegating underwriting authority to a coverholder. (05)
Bordereau (plural bordereaux) Periodic report of risks, premiums or claims from a coverholder or cedant. (05)
Broker Intermediary placing insurance, normally agent of the insured. (02)
Bureau Central processing service for premium, claims, accounting and settlement (Velonetic). (06)
Burning cost Historical losses ÷ exposure, used for pricing. (17)
Capacity (stamp capacity) Maximum premium a syndicate may write in a year of account. (02, 07)
Cat XoL Catastrophe excess of loss reinsurance. (03)
CDR – Core Data Record Standard set of structured contract data captured at placement for downstream processing. (08)
Cedant Insurer buying reinsurance. (03)
Central Fund Mutual fund at Lloyd's, the third link of the chain of security. (07)
Chain of security Lloyd's three-link capital structure: syndicate assets, Funds at Lloyd's, central assets. (07)
CIDRA Consumer Insurance (Disclosure and Representations) Act 2012. (15)
CIL – Coming into Line Deadline for members to provide Funds at Lloyd's for the next year. (07)
Lloyd's Claims Lead Arrangements (LCLA) Lloyd's rules for agreeing claims on subscription contracts: the lead agrees standard claims; lead and second lead agree complex claims; followers are bound. Replaced the Lloyd's Claims Scheme. (06)
Combined ratio (Claims + expenses) ÷ earned premium. (01)
Composite policy One policy covering several parties with different interests, treated as separate contracts. (17)
Consortium Group of insurers writing together under a consortium leader. (04, 17)
Contra proferentem Ambiguous wording interpreted against the party that drafted it. (17)
Contract certainty All terms agreed before inception and documented promptly. (04)
Contribution Sharing a loss among policies covering the same risk. (15)
Corporate member Company providing capital at Lloyd's. (02)
Coverholder Firm authorised to bind risks on behalf of Lloyd's syndicates. (05)
Crystal Lloyd's tool for regulatory, licensing and tax information by territory. (04)
DCA – Delegated claims administrator Third party handling claims on behalf of insurers. (05)
Deductible / excess Part of the loss paid by the insured. (01)
Delegated authority (DA) Insurers giving third parties authority to underwrite or handle claims. (05)
Earned premium Premium for the expired portion of the policy period. (17)
ECA – Economic Capital Assessment Lloyd's member capital = uSCR × 135%. (07)
ECF – Electronic Claims File Market system for sharing and agreeing claims. (06)
Endorsement Change to a bound contract. (04)
EP curve Exceedance probability curve – probability of losses exceeding given amounts. (17)
E&S / surplus lines US non-admitted insurance market. (01)
Facultative Reinsurance of an individual risk. (03)
FAL – Funds at Lloyd's Members' capital supporting underwriting. (07)
Fair presentation Insured's pre-contract disclosure duty under the Insurance Act 2015. (15, 16)
FCA Financial Conduct Authority – conduct regulator. (07)
FET US Federal Excise Tax on premiums paid to non-US insurers. (06)
FNOL First notification of loss. (06)
Follower Underwriter taking a share on the leader's terms. (04)
FOS Financial Ombudsman Service. (15)
Franchise Threshold below which nothing is paid; above it, the full loss is paid. (15)
Fronting Licensed insurer issues a policy and reinsures the risk to another carrier. (17)
FSCS Financial Services Compensation Scheme. (15)
GPI – Gross premium income limit Maximum premium a coverholder may bind under a BAA. (05)
GUA – General Underwriters' Agreement Market agreement defining which changes the leader may agree alone. (04, 16)
Hard market Rising prices, tighter terms, scarce capacity. (04)
Hours clause Defines the time window that counts as one event for reinsurance. (03)
IBNR / IBNER Incurred but not reported / incurred but not enough reported. (06, 17)
IFRS 17 International accounting standard for insurance contracts. (07)
ILS Insurance-linked securities, e.g., cat bonds. (03)
Indemnity Restoring the insured to the same financial position as before the loss. (15)
Insurable interest Legal or financial relationship with the subject matter of insurance. (15)
IPT Insurance Premium Tax – 12% standard, 20% higher rate. (16)
IUA International Underwriting Association – company market body. (02)
Layer Band of cover above a retention or lower layer. (01, 17)
Lead underwriter / slip leader Underwriter setting terms in a subscription placement. (04)
LIIBA London & International Insurance Brokers' Association. (02)
LIMOSS LMA/IUA-owned body managing market service contracts. (02)
Lineslip Facility allowing a lead to accept declared risks for all participants. (04, 17)
LMA Lloyd's Market Association – represents managing agents. (02)
LMG London Market Group – umbrella body of Lloyd's, LMA, IUA and LIIBA. (02)
Lloyd's Europe Lloyd's Insurance Company S.A., Brussels, for EEA risks. (07)
Loss adjuster / loss assessor Expert appointed by insurer / by insured to handle a claim. (06, 16)
LPAN Premium advice note submitted by the broker to the bureau. (06)
LPT – Loss portfolio transfer Transfer of existing claims liabilities to a reinsurer. (07)
Managing agent Company managing syndicates on behalf of members. (02)
Master policy One policy for an organisation with certificates to members. (17)
MCR Minimum Capital Requirement under Solvency II/UK. (16)
MGA Managing General Agent – binds business for insurers. (05)
Moral hazard Increase in risk from people's behaviour or attitude. (15)
MRC – Market Reform Contract Standard London placing and contract document. (04)
Names Individual members of Lloyd's. (02)
Non-bureau Business processed directly between broker and insurer. (06)
Open year Lloyd's year of account not closed at 36 months. (07)
OSND / SND (Original) Signing Number and Date – bureau reference for accounting entries. (06)
Peril / hazard Cause of loss / feature increasing likelihood or size of loss. (15)
PML Probable maximum loss. (04)
PPL Placing Platform Limited – electronic placing platform. (02)
PRA Prudential Regulation Authority – prudential regulator. (07)
Premium trust fund (PTF) Trust funds holding Lloyd's syndicate premiums. (06)
Prior submit Delegated arrangement where the coverholder must get insurer approval before binding. (05)
Proportional / non-proportional Reinsurance sharing premium and losses / paying above a retention. (03)
Proximate cause Dominant, effective cause of loss. (15)
PS25/21 FCA policy statement (Dec 2025) simplifying insurance rules for commercial customers. (07)
Quota share Proportional treaty ceding a fixed percentage. (03, 16)
RARC Risk-adjusted rate change. (04)
RDS – Realistic Disaster Scenario Lloyd's prescribed catastrophe "what if" events. (17)
Reinstatement Restoration of a reinsurance limit after a loss, often for extra premium. (16)
Retrocession Reinsurance of reinsurers. (01)
Return premium (RP) Premium returned on endorsement or cancellation. (04)
RITC – Reinsurance to Close Transfer of a closing year of account's liabilities to a later year/syndicate. (07)
Risk code Lloyd's classification code for business. (08)
ROL – Rate on line Reinsurance premium ÷ limit. (16)
SBF – Syndicate Business Forecast Annual syndicate business plan submitted to Lloyd's. (07, 17)
SCR / uSCR Solvency Capital Requirement (1-year) / ultimate SCR (to ultimate). (07)
Service company Group-owned coverholder of a managing agent. (02, 05)
Several liability Each insurer liable only for its own share. (04)
Side letter Separate document varying a contract – a contract certainty risk. (17)
Signed line / written line Final share after signing down / share written by the underwriter. (04)
SM&CR Senior Managers & Certification Regime. (07, 16)
Soft market Falling prices, wider terms, plentiful capacity. (04)
SOV – Statement of values Schedule of insured locations and values. (03)
SPA – Special purpose arrangement Syndicate writing a quota share of a host syndicate. (02)
Subrogation Insurer's right to recover from a responsible third party after paying. (15)
Subscription market Market where many insurers share one risk. (01, 04)
Surplus treaty Proportional treaty ceding amounts above the cedant's retention, in lines. (16)
Syndicate One or more members underwriting at Lloyd's; not a legal entity. (02)
Technical account Part of an insurer's income statement showing underwriting results. (17)
TOBA Terms of Business Agreement between broker and insurer or client. (16)
TPA Third-party administrator (claims or policy administration). (05)
Treaty Reinsurance covering a whole portfolio. (03)
UCR Unique Claim Reference. (06)
UMR Unique Market Reference for a contract. (04)
Unearned premium Premium for the unexpired part of the policy period. (17)
Velonetic Bureau services provider for the London market (part of DXC). (02)
Warranty Insured's promise; breach suspends cover under the Insurance Act 2015. (16)
Whitespace Electronic placing platform used in London. (02)
XoL – Excess of loss Non-proportional reinsurance paying above a retention. (03)
Year of account (YOA) All Lloyd's policies incepting in a calendar year, accounted as one venture. (07)
Module 13

Self-Assessment, 12-Week Plan and Sources

1. Self-assessment by level

Answer without looking. Model answers are short; if yours is weaker, re-read the module in brackets.

Beginner (Modules 01–03)

# Question Model answer
1 Why is Lloyd's not an insurance company? It is a market where syndicates, run by managing agents and backed by members' capital, underwrite risk; the Corporation regulates and supports the market.
2 What are the four parts of the London market? Lloyd's, company market, brokers, support services/infrastructure.
3 What is subscription? One risk shared by many insurers; a lead sets terms, followers take shares.
4 Whose agent is the broker in the open market? The insured's.
5 Name three lines where London is a global leader. Marine, aviation, energy, cyber, political risk, reinsurance (any three).
6 What is the difference between treaty and facultative? Treaty covers a portfolio automatically; facultative covers one risk negotiated individually.
7 What does a combined ratio of 92% mean? For every £100 of earned premium, £92 is spent on claims and expenses – an underwriting profit of £8.

Intermediate (Modules 04–06)

# Question Model answer
1 Name the six sections of the MRC. Risk Details; Information; Security Details; Subscription Agreement; Fiscal and Regulatory; Broker Remuneration and Deductions.
2 Written lines total 125%. A 25% written line signs to? 20%.
3 What does the GUA do? Sets which contract changes the leader can agree alone and which need other agreement parties.
4 What are the three bordereaux types? Risk, premium, claims.
5 What does SND stand for and why does it matter? Signing Number and Date – the bureau reference that identifies each accounting entry and links premiums, endorsements and claims.
6 Who agrees a complex claim on a Lloyd's subscription contract? The Lloyd's lead and second Lloyd's lead under the Lloyd's Claims Lead Arrangements (which replaced the Lloyd's Claims Scheme); followers are bound.
7 Give three causes of aged premium debt. Late documentation, bureau rejections, DA remittance delays, client non-payment, disputes.

Advanced (Modules 07–09)

# Question Model answer
1 Explain RITC. At closure (normally 36 months), the closing year's members pay a premium to a later year/syndicate to take over remaining liabilities.
2 ECA formula? uSCR × 135%.
3 What did Lloyd's decide about Blueprint Two in March 2026? Retired the name and original vision; shift to incremental modernisation, common data standards (CDR retained) and refocused Velonetic.
4 What does PS25/21 change? Larger commercial customers excluded from ICOBS, PROD and Consumer Duty; co-manufacturers can nominate a lead for PROD 4; MGAs cannot be lead.
5 Name Lloyd's four strategic drivers (2026–2030). Leading underwriting performance; efficient and flexible marketplace; maximised capital advantage; a Lloyd's to be proud of.
6 Where does AI deliver most value today in London? Submission intake and triage, wording checks, bordereaux processing, claims summarisation and triage.
7 What is the PRA's main GI concern in 2026? Softening market leading internal models to overstate profitability and understate capital.

Champion (Modules 10–11)

Write a one-page recommendation for Scenarios 2, 8 and 10 in Module 10. Test each against: Is the problem quantified? Is the root cause clear? Is there one recommendation? Are trade-offs stated? Are 90-day KPIs defined?

Lloyd's Claims Lead Arrangements exact rules and thresholds Summarised; read the current LCLA text and guidance.
Lloyd's financial strength ratings Verified Nov 2026: S&P AA-, Fitch AA-, KBRA AA-, AM Best A+ (all stable). Recheck before quoting.
FOS award limits and FSCS eligibility thresholds FOS verified Nov 2026: £455,000 / £205,000 from 1 April 2026. FSCS thresholds still to verify.
EU AI Act high-risk timing Provisional deal 7 May 2026: Annex III to 2 Dec 2027, Annex I to 2 Aug 2028. Confirm formal adoption.
Russia aircraft litigation outcome Appeal pending as of the sources used.
Vendor and company names Ownership and market positions change; verify before naming in proposals.
Module 14

CII London Market Units LM1, LM2, LM3: Course Structure and Exam Strategy

Status in this guide: optional reference. The development goal is consulting capability, not exams – see Module 18. Use modules 14–17 when you need precise rules, law and calculations. The question weightings below remain a useful signal of which topics the industry treats as core.

Purpose: Map the Chartered Insurance Institute (CII) London Market units to this guide, so you can (1) pass the exams and (2) use the same knowledge in client work. The exam teaches the rules; the rest of this guide teaches how the rules play out in practice.

Source: CII examination syllabuses for LM1, LM2 and LM3, published October 2026, examined 1 January 2027 to 31 December 2027 (LM3 states 1 December 2027) certain. Always check the CII unit page for "qualification updates" before you sit the exam.

1. The three units at a glance

LM1 LM2 LM3
Title London Market insurance essentials London Market insurance principles and practices London Market underwriting principles
Objective (CII) Essential grounding in how the London insurance market operates Broader understanding of insurance process and practice across the London Market Underwriting disciplines; business planning, capacity, pricing, clear and certain contracts; delegated underwriting and distribution
Exam format 50 MCQs 55 MCQs + 4 case studies of 5 MCQs each (75 questions) 75 MCQs
Time 1 hour 2 hours 2 hours
Learning outcomes 11 11 5
Level Entry (Beginner) Intermediate Intermediate–Advanced
Law basis English law and practice English law and practice English law and practice
Detailed notes in this guide Module 15 Module 16 Module 17

Together, LM1, LM2 and LM3 lead to the CII Certificate in London Market Insurance likely – confirm current qualification rules and credit values on the CII website.

2. Where the marks are (question weighting)

The CII states that the number of questions per learning outcome is a guide, usually within ±2 certain. Use the weighting to plan study time.

LM1 – 50 questions

Insight: 20 of 50 questions (40%) come from principles and regulation. Learn these first.

Insight: Outcome 8 (MRC, fair presentation, GUA, on risk, premiums, termination, conflicts) plus the case studies decide the result. Case studies test application, so practise with Module 10.

5 Methods of writing business and distribution 24 Very high

Insight: Distribution and delegated underwriting (outcome 5) is almost a third of the exam. Business planning and capital (outcome 3) is the area most candidates find unfamiliar – Lloyd's SBF, monitoring and the technical account.
