What the exams do NOT cover (but clients will ask)

The CII units focus on principles, law and market practice. They do not go deeply into: the Blueprint Two decision and market modernisation (Module 08), AI (Module 09), Lloyd's 2026–2030 strategy and current results (Module 07), data architecture, or consulting skills (Module 11). Passing the exams is necessary for credibility but not sufficient for consulting.

4. Recommended study sequence

flowchart LR
  A[LM1<br/>6-8 weeks<br/>Principles, regulation,<br/>market structure] --> B[LM2<br/>8-10 weeks<br/>MRC, placement law,<br/>intermediaries, DA, claims]
  B --> C[LM3<br/>10-12 weeks<br/>Wordings, business planning,<br/>pricing, distribution]

guessing Study hours: about 50–60 hours for LM1, 80–100 hours for LM2, 100–120 hours for LM3, for someone working full time without London market operations experience. Adjust after your first practice test.

Weekly method (works for all three units)

  1. Read the CII study text chapter (the exam is set against it).
  2. Read the matching module in this guide for practical context.
  3. Make one-page notes per learning outcome in your own words.
  4. Do the practice MCQs in modules 15–17, then the CII examination guide questions.
  5. Review wrong answers: write why the right answer is right.
  6. In the final two weeks, do full timed mock exams.

5. MCQ exam technique

Technique Why
Read the last line of the question first Know what is being asked before reading the scenario.
Watch for "NOT", "MOST likely", "main" CII questions often test the best answer, not just a correct one.
Eliminate two options first Usually two options are clearly wrong.
For calculations, write the formula before the numbers Reinsurance, IPT, burning cost, return period questions reward method.
Answer by English law and current market practice Not by your own country's practice.
Time: LM1 ≈ 70 seconds per question; LM2 and LM3 ≈ 95 seconds Flag hard questions and return.
For LM2 case studies, read the case once fully, then each question The five questions build on the same facts.

6. Common traps across the three units

Trap Correct position
"Utmost good faith means the insured must disclose everything." For non-consumer insurance, the Insurance Act 2015 duty of fair presentation applies; for consumers, CIDRA 2012 applies (duty to take reasonable care not to misrepresent).
"Breach of warranty ends the contract." Since the Insurance Act 2015, cover is suspended during breach and restored when the breach is remedied (s.10).
"The broker is the insurer's agent." Normally the broker is the agent of the insured; exceptions arise in delegated authority and some administrative tasks.
"Lloyd's underwrites the risk." Syndicates underwrite; Lloyd's is the market and regulator of the market.
"Signed line is always the same as written line." Signed lines may be reduced (signed down) when the placement is over-subscribed.
"Subrogation applies to life insurance." Subrogation applies only to contracts of indemnity.
"GUA = General Underwriting Agreement." It is the General Underwriters' Agreement.
"FSCS pays 100% of all general insurance claims." Generally 90% with no upper limit; 100% for compulsory insurance and certain claims likely – check current FSCS rules.
Module 15

CII LM1: London Market Insurance Essentials – Study Notes

Exam: 50 MCQs, 1 hour | Syllabus: published October 2026, examined 1 Jan – 31 Dec 2027 certain How to use: Each section follows a CII learning outcome (LO). The notes cover the key points you need, in plain English, with links to the deeper modules. Confirm detail against the CII LM1 study text – it is the basis of the exam.

LO1 – Basic terminology used in the general insurance market (≈6 questions)

1.1 Good faith and fair presentation

Point Detail
Good faith Both parties must act honestly. Insurance has historically been a contract of "utmost good faith" (uberrimae fidei). The Insurance Act 2015 kept the principle but removed avoidance of the whole contract as the automatic remedy for breach (s.14).
Duty of fair presentation (non-consumer) Insurance Act 2015, s.3: before the contract is entered into, the insured must disclose every material circumstance it knows or ought to know, or enough information to put a prudent insurer on notice that it needs to ask further questions. The presentation must be reasonably clear and accessible, and material facts must be substantially correct.
What the insured "knows" Knowledge of senior management and of individuals responsible for the insurance (s.4); "ought to know" = what a reasonable search would reveal (including information held by agents, e.g., brokers).
Material circumstance One that would influence the judgement of a prudent insurer in deciding whether to take the risk and on what terms.
Consumers Consumer Insurance (Disclosure and Representations) Act 2012 (CIDRA): the consumer must take reasonable care not to make a misrepresentation; there is no general duty to volunteer information.
Insurer's side Insurer must also act in good faith (e.g., handling claims fairly).

Remedies for breach of fair presentation are in Module 16 – LO8.

1.2 Proximate cause

  • Defined as the dominant, effective or efficient cause that sets in motion a chain of events producing the loss – not necessarily the cause nearest in time (Leyland Shipping v Norwich Union, 1918) certain.
  • Insurer pays if the proximate cause is an insured peril and not excluded.
  • Concurrent causes: if one cause is insured and another is excluded, the exclusion usually prevails (Wayne Tank v Employers Liability, 1974) certain.
  • Classic chain example: fire (insured) → firefighters' water damages stock → water damage is covered because fire is the proximate cause.

1.3 Indemnity and how it is modified

Indemnity = putting the insured back in the same financial position as immediately before the loss – no better, no worse.

How indemnity is provided Cash payment, repair, replacement, reinstatement
Modifications that reduce payment Excess/deductible; franchise (no payment below a threshold, full payment above it); average (claim reduced in proportion to under-insurance: claim × sum insured ÷ value at risk); limits and sub-limits
Modifications that go beyond strict indemnity Reinstatement / new for old (no deduction for wear and tear); agreed value policies (value agreed at inception – common in marine, fine art); first loss policies
Not indemnity contracts Life and personal accident benefits (fixed sums)

Average – worked example: Stock worth £200,000 insured for £150,000. Loss £40,000. Payment = £40,000 × 150,000 ÷ 200,000 = £30,000.

1.4 Contribution

  • Applies when two or more policies cover the same interest, same subject matter, same peril, and both are in force at the time of loss.
  • The insured cannot recover more than the loss in total. Insurers share the loss.
  • Most policies have a rateable proportion clause (each insurer pays only its share).
  • Methods: maximum (independent) liability – based on each policy's sum insured; independent liability – based on what each would have paid alone.

Example (independent liability): Loss £6,000. Policy A would pay £6,000 alone; Policy B would pay £3,000 alone. Total £9,000. A pays 6/9 × £6,000 = £4,000; B pays 3/9 × £6,000 = £2,000.

1.5 Subrogation

  • After paying a claim, the insurer stands in the shoes of the insured to recover from a responsible third party.
  • Applies only to contracts of indemnity.
  • Arises from tort (negligence), contract, statute, or salvage.
  • Insurer cannot recover more than it paid (the insured keeps any excess beyond that, subject to the policy).
  • Policy conditions usually allow the insurer to start recovery action before payment.
  • Waiver of subrogation clauses (e.g., in construction contracts) prevent insurers suing other named parties.

LO2 – Fundamental principles of insurance (≈10 questions)

2.1–2.2 Risk and categories of risk

Risk = uncertainty about a loss; in insurance, the possibility of a financial loss from an event.

Category Meaning Insurable?
Pure risk Only loss or no loss (fire, theft) Generally yes
Speculative risk Loss, no loss or gain (business investment, gambling) Generally no
Particular risk Affects individuals; personal in cause and effect (burglary) Yes
Fundamental risk Affects society widely; impersonal in cause (war, pandemic, major flood) Often excluded or covered by state schemes / specialist markets
Financial risk Outcome can be measured in money Yes
Non-financial risk Cannot be measured in money (choice of career) No
Static vs dynamic Static: exists in an unchanging society (lightning); dynamic: from changes in society/economy (technology change) Static usually insurable

2.3 Pooling

Many people with similar risks pay premiums into a common pool; the pool pays the losses of the few. Works because of the law of large numbers: with more similar exposures, actual losses get closer to expected losses, so premiums can be set fairly.

2.4–2.5 Peril and hazard

Term Meaning Example
Peril The cause of loss Fire, storm, theft
Hazard A feature that increases the likelihood or size of loss from a peril Storing petrol near a boiler
Physical hazard Physical features of the risk Timber construction, no sprinklers (poor); fire alarm and sprinklers (good)
Moral hazard Attitude and behaviour of people connected with the risk Dishonesty, carelessness, poor claims history, poor management (poor); strong risk management culture (good)

2.6 Insurable and uninsurable risks

Insurable risk requirements: financial value; pure risk; particular (not catastrophic for the whole pool); fortuitous (accidental from the insured's view); insurable interest; not against public policy; large number of similar exposures; affordable premium.

Generally uninsurable: speculative/trading losses; deliberate acts of the insured; inevitable losses (wear and tear, depreciation); criminal fines and penalties (generally); some fundamental risks without specialist cover.

2.7–2.8 Purpose and functions of insurance

Primary functions Secondary functions
Risk transfer – from insured to insurer Loss prevention and control – surveys, risk advice
Common pool – losses of few paid by many Social benefits – security, employment, compensation for victims
Equitable premiums – each pays according to the risk brought to the pool Investment – premiums invested in the economy; invisible earnings for UK
Peace of mind / enabling business – lenders require insurance; trade possible

2.9 Importance of claims handling

The claim is the moment the insurer delivers its promise. Good claims handling: protects reputation and broker relationships, keeps clients, controls cost (leakage), meets regulation (fair treatment) and supplies data for pricing and reserving. See Module 06 Part B.

LO3 – Main classes of insurance written in the London Market (≈4 questions)

See Module 03. For the exam, know the main features of: marine (hull, cargo, liability, war), aviation (hull, liability, products, space), energy (onshore, offshore), property (including terrorism and political violence), casualty/liability (public/products liability, professional indemnity, D&O, medical malpractice, cyber), specialty (political risk, credit, K&R, contingency, fine art, bloodstock), personal lines/A&H through delegated authority, and reinsurance.

LO4 – The insurance cycle (≈1 question)

flowchart LR
  A[High prices and profits] --> B[New capital enters,<br/>competition increases]
  B --> C[Prices fall, terms widen<br/>= SOFT market]
  C --> D[Losses rise, capital lost<br/>or withdrawn]
  D --> E[Capacity falls, prices rise<br/>= HARD market]
  E --> A

Drivers: capital supply, catastrophe events, investment returns, competition, reserve releases or deteriorations. The London market in 2026 is in a softening phase (Lloyd's risk-adjusted rates −6.7% in H1 2026) certain.

LO5 – Reinsurance (≈3 questions)

Purposes of reinsurance: capacity (write larger risks), stability (smooth results), catastrophe protection, financial support (capital and solvency relief), expertise and confidence (new classes), withdrawal from a class (run-off).

Key terms: cedant/reassured; reinsurer; retrocession; treaty vs facultative; proportional (quota share, surplus) vs non-proportional (excess of loss, stop loss); retention; capacity; ceding commission; layer; reinstatement; burning cost. Details and calculations: Module 03 §2.7 and Module 16 – LO3.

LO6 – Structure of the London Market (≈5 questions)

Sub-point Key notes Guide
6.1 Main participants Lloyd's (Corporation, managing agents, syndicates, members), company market, brokers, P&I clubs, coverholders/MGAs, service providers Module 02
6.2 Why clients use London Capacity for large risks; expertise in complex/specialty classes; security (Lloyd's chain of security, ratings); global licences; innovation; claims reputation; concentration of brokers and underwriters in one place Module 01 §3
6.3 Market associations LMA (managing agents), IUA (company market), LIIBA (brokers), LMG (umbrella), plus Lloyd's Module 02 §6
6.4 How business is transacted Broker presents to lead; subscription (lead and follow); MRC; open market vs delegated authority vs lineslips/consortia; electronic placing platforms; central processing and settlement via the bureau Module 04

LO7 – Regulatory and legal environment (≈10 questions)

7.1 The industry regulators – role, aims, approach, principles

FCA PRA
Focus Conduct of business Prudential soundness
Objectives Strategic: markets function well. Operational: protect consumers, protect market integrity, promote effective competition. Secondary: international competitiveness and growth (from FSMA 2023) Safety and soundness of firms; appropriate protection for policyholders (insurers). Secondary: competition; competitiveness and growth
Who All insurance firms and intermediaries (solo-regulated firms like most brokers) Insurers, Lloyd's, managing agents (dual-regulated with FCA)
Approach Outcomes-based, data-led, Consumer Duty for retail Judgement-based, forward-looking, proportionate

FCA Principles for Businesses (12) certain: (1) integrity; (2) skill, care and diligence; (3) management and control; (4) financial prudence; (5) market conduct; (6) customers' interests; (7) communications with clients; (8) conflicts of interest; (9) customers: relationships of trust; (10) clients' assets; (11) relations with regulators; (12) Consumer Duty – act to deliver good outcomes for retail customers. (Principles 6 and 7 do not apply where Principle 12 applies.)

PRA Fundamental Rules (8) certain: integrity; skill, care and diligence; prudent risk management; adequate financial resources; effective risk strategy and risk management systems; organise and control affairs responsibly; deal with regulators openly; be able to resolve in an orderly way.

7.2 International regulators and licensing

  • Each country regulates insurance written on its residents/risks. A London insurer can write a risk abroad only if it holds a local licence or the business is allowed on a non-admitted basis (e.g., US surplus lines, large commercial risks, most reinsurance).
  • US: state-based regulation; National Association of Insurance Commissioners (NAIC) coordinates; Lloyd's syndicates are eligible surplus lines insurers and hold US trust funds.
  • EU: after Brexit, UK insurers lost passporting; Lloyd's set up Lloyd's Insurance Company S.A. (Lloyd's Europe) in Brussels.
  • IAIS sets global Insurance Core Principles.
  • Lloyd's holds licences in 200+ countries and territories and provides licensing guidance (Crystal) certain.

7.3 Governance of the Lloyd's Market

  • Lloyd's Act 1982: the Council of Lloyd's is the governing body with power to make byelaws; includes working, external and nominated members; Chair and Deputy Chairs elected from Council.
  • The Council delegates day-to-day management to the executive under the CEO and committees.
  • Lloyd's supervises managing agents through business plan approval, minimum standards / principles for doing business, Market Bulletins, and intervention powers.
  • PRA and FCA regulate the Society of Lloyd's and managing agents; Lloyd's works with both under cooperation arrangements.

7.4 Financial Ombudsman Service and FSCS

Financial Ombudsman Service (FOS) Financial Services Compensation Scheme (FSCS)
Purpose Free, independent resolution of complaints against financial firms Compensation when an authorised firm fails (insolvent)
Who can use Eligible complainants: consumers, micro-enterprises, small businesses (fewer than 50 employees and turnover below £6.5m or balance sheet below £5m), small charities and trusts, some guarantors Mainly individuals and small businesses (for general insurance, small businesses with turnover under £1m likely)
Process Complainant must first complain to the firm; firm has 8 weeks to give a final response; complainant has 6 months from final response to refer to FOS Claim made to FSCS after firm declared in default
Limits Maximum award limit adjusted annually by CPI: £455,000 for complaints referred from 1 April 2026 about acts on/after 1 April 2019; £205,000 for acts before 1 April 2019 likely – check the figure for your year General insurance: 90% of claim, no upper limit; 100% for compulsory insurance (e.g., employers' liability, motor third party) and certain claims such as injury likely
Binding? Decision binding on firm if complainant accepts —

Large London market commercial clients are usually not eligible for FOS or FSCS – an exam favourite.

7.5–7.6 Regulator powers over insurers and intermediaries

  • Authorisation: firms need Part 4A permission under FSMA 2000; must meet Threshold Conditions (e.g., adequate resources, suitability, effective supervision, appropriate business model).
  • Approval of individuals under the Senior Managers & Certification Regime (SM&CR); Conduct Rules apply to most staff.
  • Supervision: returns, visits, reviews, skilled person reports (s.166 FSMA).
  • Enforcement: fines, public censure, variation or cancellation of permission, prohibition of individuals, restitution orders.
  • Intermediaries: FCA authorises and supervises brokers (solo-regulated); rules include ICOBS, SYSC, client money (CASS 5), professional indemnity insurance requirements, capital resources rules.
  • Appointed representatives operate under a principal firm's responsibility.

7.7 Essentials of a valid insurance contract

Element Meaning in insurance
Offer and acceptance Proposal/presentation (offer) and acceptance by insurer; in London, the underwriter writing a line on the slip is acceptance
Consideration Premium (or promise to pay) and the insurer's promise to pay claims
Capacity to contract Parties legally able (e.g., not minors; company acting within powers)
Legality Lawful purpose; not against public policy (e.g., cannot insure illegal cargo)
Intention to create legal relations Presumed in commercial contracts
Certainty of terms Terms must be clear – links to contract certainty
Insurable interest Insured must have a legal/financial relationship with the subject matter – essential for insurance validity

LO8 – Systems and controls (sanctions) (≈2 questions)

8.1 Purpose of sanctions

Political and economic measures used by governments and international bodies (UN, UK, EU, US) to change behaviour of countries, regimes, entities or individuals: e.g., counter-terrorism, non-proliferation, human rights, response to armed conflict (Russia).

8.2 Systems and controls

Regime Key body
UK Sanctions and Anti-Money Laundering Act 2018; OFSI (HM Treasury) enforces financial sanctions; Department for Business and Trade for trade sanctions
EU Council regulations – relevant to Lloyd's Europe and EU entities
US OFAC (US Treasury); applies to US persons, US dollar transactions; secondary sanctions can affect non-US firms
UN Security Council resolutions implemented by member states

Controls a London firm needs:

  • Screening of insureds, beneficiaries, brokers, coverholders, vessels and other parties at quote, bind, endorsement, claims payment and periodically.
  • Sanctions clauses in contracts (e.g., LMA3100 sanction limitation and exclusion clause) certain.
  • Escalation and licence applications; reporting suspected breaches to OFSI.
  • Training, governance, record-keeping, testing of screening tools.
  • Marine: vessel tracking (AIS), ship-to-ship transfers, oil price cap compliance.

LO9 – Data protection and money laundering (≈2 questions)

9.1 Data protection

  • UK GDPR and Data Protection Act 2018 (amended by the Data (Use and Access) Act 2025 likely).
  • Principles: lawfulness, fairness and transparency; purpose limitation; data minimisation; accuracy; storage limitation; integrity and confidentiality (security); accountability.
  • Rights of individuals: to be informed, access, rectification, erasure, restriction, portability, objection, rights about automated decision-making.
  • Special category data (e.g., health) needs extra conditions – relevant to A&H, injury claims.
  • Regulator: Information Commissioner's Office (ICO); maximum fines up to £17.5m or 4% of global annual turnover certain.
  • Impact on London business: data sharing across many insurers and brokers in a subscription; international transfers; bordereaux contain personal data.

9.2 Money laundering

  • Stages: placement, layering, integration.
  • Proceeds of Crime Act 2002 (POCA): offences of concealing, arranging, acquiring criminal property; failure to disclose (regulated sector); tipping off.
  • Money Laundering Regulations 2017 apply mainly to life and investment insurance; general insurance firms are largely outside the MLRs' regulated sector but remain subject to POCA, terrorist financing law, sanctions and FCA financial crime systems and controls likely.
  • Insurance risks: premium paid then cancelled for refund to a third party; overpayment refunds; fraudulent claims; unusual payment routes; politically exposed persons (PEPs).
  • Controls: customer due diligence (know your customer), enhanced due diligence for higher risk, monitoring, suspicious activity reports (SARs) to the National Crime Agency via the Money Laundering Reporting Officer (MLRO), training and records.
  • Related: Bribery Act 2010; Criminal Finances Act 2017 (failure to prevent facilitation of tax evasion); Economic Crime and Corporate Transparency Act 2023 (failure to prevent fraud offence) likely.

LO10 – The broker's role (≈4 questions)

Sub-point Key notes
10.1 Role and responsibilities Agent of the client; advise on cover; fair presentation on client's behalf; choose secure insurers; obtain best terms; place and document cover (contract certainty); handle premiums and claims; act with skill and care (liable in negligence if not – E&O)
10.2 Broking process and parties Client/retail broker → London broker (placing broker) → lead underwriter → followers; also account handlers, technicians (documentation and processing), claims brokers
10.3 Premium handling Collects premium from client, deducts brokerage, pays insurers (often via bureau central settlement); holds money as client money (CASS 5) or under risk transfer for insurers; must meet premium payment terms
10.4 Claims Notifies claim to leader, collects information, presents and negotiates claim, advocates for client, collects claim funds and pays client

LO11 – The underwriter's role (≈3 questions)

Sub-point Key notes
11.1 Role and responsibilities Assess risk; decide acceptance, terms and price; work within authority and business plan; build a balanced profitable portfolio; manage aggregation; ensure contract certainty; manage relationships with brokers
11.2 Lead and following underwriters Lead: sets terms and rate, often handles claims agreement and changes (under GUA and claims lead arrangements), carries reputational responsibility. Followers: accept the leader's terms for their share, rely on leader's expertise; can sometimes add conditions. Each insurer is severally liable only for its own line

Practice questions (answers at the end)

  1. Under the Insurance Act 2015, the duty of fair presentation applies to: a) consumer contracts only b) non-consumer contracts c) life insurance only d) reinsurance only

  2. Which is a moral hazard? a) timber building b) no sprinklers c) poor claims history caused by careless management d) flood zone location

  3. Stock valued at £400,000 is insured for £300,000 subject to average. Loss £80,000. Payment? a) £80,000 b) £60,000 c) £75,000 d) £53,333

  4. Subrogation does not apply to: a) motor b) property c) personal accident fixed benefits d) liability

  5. Which body compensates customers when an authorised insurer becomes insolvent? a) FOS b) FSCS c) PRA d) OFSI

  6. In a London subscription placement, each insurer's liability is: a) joint b) joint and several c) several d) limited to the leader's line

  7. Which is a primary function of insurance? a) loss prevention b) investment c) equitable premiums d) social benefit

  8. The proximate cause is: a) the last event before the loss b) the dominant, effective cause c) the most expensive cause d) the cause named by the insured

  9. A firm must give a final response to a complaint before referral to FOS within: a) 4 weeks b) 8 weeks c) 6 months d) 12 months

  10. Which body enforces UK financial sanctions? a) FCA b) OFAC c) OFSI d) NCA

Answers: 1-b, 2-c, 3-b (80,000 × 300/400), 4-c, 5-b, 6-c, 7-c, 8-b, 9-b, 10-c.

Module 16

CII LM2: London Market Insurance Principles and Practices – Study Notes

Exam: 55 MCQs + 4 case studies (5 MCQs each), 2 hours | Syllabus: published October 2026, examined 1 Jan – 31 Dec 2027 certain Focus: LO8 (how business is conducted) carries about 14 questions, and the case studies test application across all outcomes. Confirm detail against the CII LM2 study text.

LO1 – Business nature of the London Market (≈1 question)

1.1 Principal parties and relationships

flowchart LR
  Client -- appoints, agent of client --> Broker
  Broker -- presents risk --> Lead[Lead underwriter]
  Broker -- shows led slip --> Followers
  Lead -- sets terms --> Followers
  MA[Managing agent] -- manages --> Syndicate
  Members -- capital --> Syndicate
  MA -- grants binding authority --> Coverholder
  Coverholder -- binds risks for --> Syndicate

Key relationships to know: client–broker (agency); broker–insurer (TOBA, premium and claims handling); lead–follower (subscription, GUA, claims lead arrangements); managing agent–members (agency agreements); insurer–coverholder (binding authority agreement). See Module 02.

LO2 – Main classes of insurance (≈3 questions)

Sub-point Key notes
2.1 Why use London Capacity, expertise, security, licences, innovation, claims reputation, subscription allows very large limits
2.2 Classes and typical losses Marine (collision, sinking, cargo damage, general average, war); aviation (crash, ground damage, passenger liability, war/confiscation); energy (blowout, fire/explosion, pollution, business interruption); property (fire, nat cat, terrorism, BI); liability (injury, damage, professional negligence, D&O claims, cyber breaches); specialty (expropriation, kidnap, event cancellation, credit default). See Module 03
2.3 Diversification and portfolio management Spread by class, territory, peril, size and channel; line size limits; aggregate limits by zone and peril; reinsurance; avoid correlation (e.g., cyber and property from one event); business plan targets

LO3 – Reinsurance (≈3 questions + calculations)

3.1 Methods

Method Description Key features
Facultative Individual risk, offered and accepted separately Reinsurer free to accept or decline; good for unusual or large risks; admin-heavy
Treaty Agreement covering a defined portfolio Obligatory – cedant must cede, reinsurer must accept within terms; automatic cover
Facultative-obligatory (fac/oblig) Cedant may choose to cede; reinsurer must accept Risk of adverse selection for reinsurer
Proportional Premium and losses shared in same proportion Quota share, surplus; ceding commission paid to cedant
Non-proportional Reinsurer pays losses above a retention up to a limit Risk XoL, catastrophe XoL, stop loss; priced as rate on line

3.2 Calculations – learn these patterns

Quota share (40% ceded)

  • Gross premium £5m → ceded £2m, retained £3m.
  • Loss £1.5m → recoverable £600k, retained £900k.
  • Ceding commission at 25% on ceded premium → £500k paid by reinsurer to cedant.

Surplus treaty

  • Retention (one "line") = £1m; treaty capacity = 9 lines (£9m). Total capacity = £10m.
  • Risk with sum insured £4m: cedant retains £1m (25%), cedes £3m (75%). All premium and losses on that risk shared 25/75.
  • Loss £800k → recoverable 75% = £600k.
  • Risk with sum insured £12m: capacity is £10m, so cedant must find £2m extra (e.g., facultative); within the treaty, retained 1/12 and ceded 9/12.

Excess of loss (XoL): £4m xs £1m

Loss Retained by cedant Recovered
£800k £800k £0
£3m £1m £2m
£7m £1m + £2m above layer = £3m £4m (layer exhausted)

Reinstatement premium: if the layer is used, a reinstatement restores the limit, usually for an extra premium pro rata to the amount (and sometimes the time) used. Example: annual premium £400k on a £4m layer, recovery £2m, one reinstatement at 100% → reinstatement premium = £400k × 2/4 = £200k.

Rate on line (ROL) = premium ÷ limit. £400k ÷ £4m = 10%.

LO4 – Market security (≈3 questions)

4.1 Basic accountancy and solvency

Concept Meaning
Assets What the insurer owns (investments, cash, reinsurance recoverables, premiums receivable)
Liabilities What it owes (claims reserves, unearned premium, debts)
Own funds / capital Assets minus liabilities
Solvency Ability to meet liabilities as they fall due; regulators require capital above a minimum
Historic solvency margin (Solvency I) Minimum margin calculated on a premium basis and a claims basis (the higher applied) – background only
Solvency II / Solvency UK SCR = capital to survive a 1-in-200 year loss over one year (99.5% VaR); MCR = lower floor (85% confidence, within 25–45% of SCR); solvency ratio = eligible own funds ÷ SCR (e.g., 150%) certain

4.2 Lloyd's chain of security

Three links: (1) syndicate-level assets (premium trust funds); (2) members' Funds at Lloyd's; (3) central assets including the Central Fund. See Module 07 §A3.

4.3 Rating agencies

  • A.M. Best, S&P Global, Fitch, Moody's give financial strength ratings – an opinion of ability to pay claims.
  • Brokers' security committees use ratings and their own analysis to approve insurers.
  • Many clients and contracts require a minimum rating (e.g., "A-" or better).
  • All Lloyd's syndicates share Lloyd's market ratings because of the Central Fund.

LO5 – Regulatory and legal requirements (≈6 questions)

5.1 Compulsory insurance in the UK

Reason: protect third parties/victims so compensation is available even if the wrongdoer cannot pay.

Insurance Law
Employers' liability Employers' Liability (Compulsory Insurance) Act 1969 – minimum £5m per occurrence certain
Motor third party Road Traffic Act 1988 – unlimited for injury; minimum for property damage certain
Others Nuclear installations; riding establishments; dangerous wild animals; oil pollution for certain ships; professional indemnity required by regulators for solicitors, accountants, insurance intermediaries and others

5.2 Consumer Rights Act 2015

  • Applies to consumer contracts, including consumer insurance.
  • Unfair terms are not binding on the consumer.
  • Terms must be transparent and prominent. The core terms (main subject matter and price) are not assessed for fairness if transparent and prominent.
  • Ambiguous terms are interpreted in the way most favourable to the consumer.

5.3 Contracts (Rights of Third Parties) Act 1999

  • A third party can enforce a contract term if the contract expressly allows it, or the term purports to confer a benefit on them (unless the parties did not intend it to be enforceable).
  • London policies often include a clause excluding the Act, and then name specific third parties (e.g., lenders, additional insureds) where needed.

5.4 EU solvency requirements and PRA risk-based capital

  • Solvency II (EU, 2016) – three pillars: (1) quantitative (SCR, MCR, technical provisions, own funds); (2) governance and supervision (ORSA, system of governance); (3) disclosure and reporting (SFCR, RSR, QRTs).
  • UK: Solvency UK reforms from 31 December 2024 (reduced risk margin, reporting changes) likely. PRA approves internal models; Lloyd's uses a market internal model with ECA = uSCR × 135% for members certain.

5.5 Insurance Premium Tax (IPT)

  • Tax on general insurance premiums for UK risks, paid by the insurer (usually passed to the client).
  • Standard rate 12%; higher rate 20% for travel insurance and certain products sold with vehicles or electrical goods by suppliers certain.
  • Exempt: reinsurance, long-term (life) insurance, certain commercial ships and aircraft, goods in international transit, risks outside the UK.
  • Calculation: premium £10,000 at standard rate → IPT £1,200; total £11,200. If a total of £11,200 includes IPT, the premium = 11,200 ÷ 1.12 = £10,000.

LO6 – Insurance intermediation (≈6 questions)

6.1–6.2 Types of intermediaries and brokers

Type Role
Retail broker Client-facing; places locally or sends to wholesale
Wholesale / London broker Places business from other intermediaries into London
Lloyd's broker Accredited by Lloyd's to place at Lloyd's
Reinsurance broker Places treaty and facultative reinsurance
Producing broker Overseas broker who produces business for a London placing broker
Placing broker Broker who presents and places the risk in London
MGA / coverholder Binds on behalf of insurers (agent of insurer)
Appointed representative Acts under the responsibility of an authorised principal firm
Introducer Only introduces clients
US surplus lines broker Licensed to place non-admitted business in a US state; pays surplus lines tax

Services: advice, risk management, placement, documentation, premium and claims handling, market security analysis, claims advocacy.

6.3 Terms of Business Agreement (TOBA)

A TOBA between broker and insurer sets out: authority (usually no authority to bind unless stated), premium handling (risk transfer or not), settlement terms, documentation duties, claims handling, data protection, complaints, conflicts of interest, termination. The London market uses a generic market TOBA template likely.

6.4 Broker remuneration

  • Brokerage/commission – percentage of premium paid by insurer (deducted from premium).
  • Fees – agreed with and paid by client (sometimes in place of commission).
  • Profit commission / contingent commission – based on profitability or volume.
  • Other income – placement service agreements, facility fees, investment income on client money.
  • Commercial clients can ask for disclosure of commission (ICOBS) certain; conflicts must be managed.

6.5 Law of agency

Topic Key points
Creation By agreement (express or implied), by ratification (principal later adopts an unauthorised act), by necessity, by estoppel (apparent/ostensible authority)
Types of authority Actual express; actual implied; apparent (ostensible)
Agent's duties Obey instructions; exercise reasonable care and skill; act personally (no delegation without consent); act in good faith – no secret profits, avoid conflicts; account for money
Principal's duties Pay agreed remuneration; indemnify agent for proper expenses
Knowledge Knowledge of the agent can be imputed to the principal (relevant to fair presentation)
Termination Agreement, completion, death, insolvency, revocation
London application Broker is normally agent of the insured; coverholder binding a risk is agent of the insurer; broker may act for insurers in limited tasks (e.g., holding premium under risk transfer) – conflicts must be managed

6.6 Legislation for intermediaries

FSMA 2000 (authorisation); FCA Handbook – ICOBS (conduct of business), SYSC (systems and controls, conflicts), CASS 5 (client money), PROD (product governance), SM&CR, Consumer Duty (retail); the Insurance Distribution Directive (IDD) – EU rules implemented in UK rules before Brexit and still applying to EU intermediaries; FCA PS25/21 (December 2025) narrows conduct rules for larger commercial customers certain.

LO7 – Underwriting function (≈7 questions)

Sub-point Key notes
7.1 London vs elsewhere Face-to-face and platform negotiation with brokers; subscription (shared risks); judgement-based specialty underwriting; global risks; strong reliance on lead underwriters; less standardised products
7.2 Broker–underwriter relationship Long-term, trust-based; broker presents, underwriter questions and negotiates; relationship affects access to business; must not compromise duties (fair presentation, conflicts)
7.3 Lead and follow Lead sets terms, price, often agrees changes and claims; followers accept leader's terms for their share; follow markets may rely on leader's expertise; signed lines may be reduced on over-placement
7.4 Market cycle Hard and soft phases driven by capital, losses, competition, investment returns; see Module 15 LO4
7.5 Modelling and managing exposures and losses Cat models (probabilistic), Realistic Disaster Scenarios (deterministic), aggregate monitoring by zone, PML, reinsurance; see Module 17 LO4.3
7.6 Reserving Provisions for outstanding claims (known) and IBNR (unknown or under-reported), plus unearned premium reserve. Needed for accurate profit, solvency, pricing and regulatory compliance
7.7 Open years and RITC Lloyd's year of account normally closes at 36 months by reinsurance to close; if liabilities too uncertain the year remains open and must be managed (run-off) until it can be closed. See Module 07 §A2

LO8 – The way business is conducted in the London Market (≈14 questions – highest weight)

8.1 Documents used to present risks

Market Reform Contract (MRC) draft; broker's presentation / submission (risk information, schedules, statements of values, loss records, surveys, financials); proposal forms (less common in London commercial business); prior policy wording.

8.2 Legal significance of quotations and renewals

  • A quotation is generally an offer that can be accepted while valid (subject to conditions/"subjectivities"); some quotes are only an invitation to negotiate – depends on wording.
  • Material change before acceptance may allow the insurer to withdraw.
  • Each renewal is a new contract – a fresh duty of fair presentation applies at every renewal.

8.3 Fair presentation and good faith – remedies for breach (Insurance Act 2015, Schedule 1) certain

Breach type Remedy
Deliberate or reckless Insurer may avoid the contract and keep the premium
Neither deliberate nor reckless – insurer would not have entered the contract Avoid the contract, return premium
– insurer would have entered on different terms (other than premium) Contract treated as if those terms applied
– insurer would have charged a higher premium Claim reduced proportionately: pay (premium charged ÷ premium that would have been charged) × claim

Example: Premium charged £60,000; with fair presentation it would have been £80,000. Claim £100,000 → paid £100,000 × 60/80 = £75,000.

8.4 Legal principles of a valid contract

Offer and acceptance, consideration, capacity, legality, intention, certainty, plus insurable interest. See Module 15 LO7.7.

8.5 Market Reform Contract (MRC)

Purpose: standard format for placing and recording the contract; supports contract certainty; one document for all subscribing insurers. Sections: Risk Details; Information; Security Details; Subscription Agreement; Fiscal and Regulatory; Broker Remuneration and Deductions. See Module 04 §3.

8.6 Placing open MRCs and electronic MRCs

  • Open MRC (paper/PDF): broker takes the slip to each underwriter, who writes and stamps a line.
  • Electronic MRC: placed on a platform (e.g., PPL, Whitespace); lines, stamps and messages are recorded electronically, with an audit trail and structured data.

8.7 General Underwriters' Agreement (GUA)

  • Market agreement setting out which contract changes the slip leader can agree alone and which need agreement of other agreement parties (e.g., second lead or all followers).
  • Changes are categorised (e.g., minor administrative changes by leader; material changes needing more parties).
  • Purpose: speed and certainty for mid-term changes in a subscription market.

8.8 How an underwriter knows they are on risk

  • When the underwriter writes (stamps and signs/initials) a line on the MRC – each written line creates a binding contract with that insurer, even before the slip is fully subscribed (General Reinsurance v Fennia Patria, 1983) certain.
  • Electronically: acceptance recorded on the platform.
  • Subject to any "subject to" conditions or subjectivities being met, and the inception date.
  • Line may later be signed down, but the insurer is on risk from writing the line.

8.9 Sections of an insurance policy

Schedule (insured, period, sums insured, premium); recital/preamble; operative (insuring) clause; exclusions; conditions (general and specific); definitions; endorsements; signature/attestation.

8.10 Warranties, conditions and exclusions

Term Effect (after Insurance Act 2015)
Warranty A promise by the insured. Breach suspends cover until remedied (s.10); "basis of contract" clauses that convert proposal answers into warranties are abolished (s.9) for non-consumer insurance
Terms not relevant to the loss (s.11) If a term aims to reduce the risk of a particular type of loss, the insurer cannot rely on its breach if the insured shows the breach could not have increased the risk of the loss that actually happened
Condition precedent Must be complied with before liability attaches (e.g., notifying claims within a time)
Condition Breach may give insurer a claim for damages but usually not a defence to the whole claim
Exclusion Defines what is not covered; insurer must prove the exclusion applies

8.11 Contract certainty

All terms agreed before inception and contract documentation provided promptly after. Introduced by the market after regulator pressure; MRC supports it. Failure leads to disputes, unpaid claims, regulatory criticism.

8.12 Collection and processing of premiums

Broker collects premium; premium payment terms in the MRC (e.g., premium payment clause with a settlement due date); submission to the bureau; signing number and date; central settlement; credit control and terms of trade. See Module 06 Part A.

8.13 Termination of insurance contracts

  • Expiry at end of period.
  • Cancellation under a cancellation clause (notice period; pro-rata or short-period return premium).
  • Mutual agreement.
  • Avoidance for breach of fair presentation (deliberate/reckless, or where insurer would not have written the risk).
  • Fraudulent claim – insurer may terminate from the time of the fraudulent act and refuse the claim (Insurance Act 2015, s.12) certain.
  • Non-payment of premium under a premium payment clause/warranty.
  • Exhaustion of aggregate limit; frustration; operation of law.

8.14 Conflicts of interest

  • Arise when a firm's interests (or one client's) conflict with another client's: e.g., broker paid by insurer while acting for client; broker acting as coverholder or claims handler for insurer; contingent commissions; placing into a broker's own facility; managing agent allocating risks between syndicates with different capital providers.
  • FCA SYSC 10: identify, prevent or manage conflicts; disclose to the client as a last resort; keep records.

LO9 – Delegated underwriting (≈4 questions)

Sub-point Key notes
9.1 Purpose Access local markets and small risks economically; use local expertise; scale distribution; efficient for high-volume business
9.2 Lloyd's controls Coverholder approval by Lloyd's; managing agent due diligence; registration of binding authority agreements before inception; standard wordings; limits on authority (classes, territories, premium income, line size); bordereaux reporting standards; audits; oversight and board reporting; restrictions on sub-delegation; Lloyd's monitoring of managing agents' DA controls. See Module 05
9.3 Lineslips and consortia Lineslip: a pre-agreed facility where a lineslip leader (sometimes with a second lead) can accept declared risks on behalf of all participants. Consortium: a group of insurers that agree to write business together, with a consortium leader underwriting on behalf of all members; Lloyd's consortia must be registered likely. See Module 04 §5

LO10 – Claims handling in the London Market (≈4 questions)

Sub-point Key notes
10.1 Insurer and broker roles Broker: notify, gather information, present and advocate the claim, collect payment. Insurer: acknowledge, investigate coverage, appoint experts, set reserves, agree and pay; lead and agreement parties agree on behalf of followers under claims lead arrangements
10.2 Claims personnel Claims broker/technician; lead claims adjuster; follower claims handlers; loss adjuster (appointed by insurer); loss assessor (appointed by insured); TPA/DCA; lawyers; experts (forensic accountants, engineers)
10.3 Principles in claims Apply indemnity (and modifications such as average and excesses), subrogation, contribution, proximate cause and exclusions. See Module 15 LO1

LO11 – Resolving complaints (≈4 questions)

Sub-point Key notes
11.1 Regulation of individuals SM&CR: Senior Management Functions (pre-approved; e.g., CEO, CFO, CRO, Chair, Chief Underwriting Officer for insurers) with Statements of Responsibilities and a Duty of Responsibility; Certification Regime (firm certifies fitness and propriety annually); Conduct Rules for almost all staff; regulatory references
11.2 Claims handling requirements ICOBS 8: handle claims promptly and fairly; provide reasonable guidance; do not unreasonably reject a claim; settle promptly once agreed (scope for larger commercial customers narrowed by PS25/21 – check current scope likely). Enterprise Act 2016: implied term to pay sums due within a reasonable time; damages for late payment certain
11.3 FOS Free, independent; eligible complainants only; 8-week final response; 6 months to refer; binding on firm if accepted. See Module 15 LO7.4
11.4 FSCS Pays when an authorised firm fails; general insurance 90% (100% compulsory and certain claims); eligibility mainly individuals and small businesses likely

Case study practice (exam style)

Case: Atlas Logistics (UK, turnover £300m) instructs London broker Brookfield to place property cover with a £50m limit. Brookfield places it on an electronic MRC: Syndicate 1001 leads with 30%, followers write 90% in total (total written 120%). Atlas did not disclose two previous warehouse fires because its insurance manager forgot; the underwriter says it would have charged 25% more premium if told. During the year, the leader agrees an extension of cover to a new warehouse. A fire causes a £10m loss.

  1. What is the leader's signed line? a) 30% b) 25% c) 36% d) 20%
  2. Which agreement allows the leader to agree certain changes without all followers? a) TOBA b) GUA c) BAA d) MRC
  3. Is the non-disclosure deliberate or reckless? a) Yes b) No – it is careless (neither deliberate nor reckless)
  4. How much is payable on the £10m claim (if premium charged was P and correct premium 1.25P)? a) £10m b) £8m c) £7.5m d) nil
  5. Is Atlas eligible to refer a complaint to FOS? a) Yes b) No – it is too large

Answers: 1-b (30 × 100/120 = 25%); 2-b; 3-b; 4-b (£10m × 1/1.25 = £8m); 5-b.

Practice MCQs

  1. A surplus treaty has a £500k retention and 10 lines. For a £3m risk, the ceded share is: a) 50% b) 83.3% c) 90% d) 16.7%
  2. Under the Insurance Act 2015, breach of warranty: a) ends the contract b) suspends cover until remedied c) has no effect d) makes the contract void from inception
  3. IPT standard rate on a £20,000 commercial property premium is: a) £1,200 b) £2,400 c) £4,000 d) £0
  4. Which is created by ratification? a) a TOBA b) agency c) a binding authority d) a consortium
  5. The Contracts (Rights of Third Parties) Act 1999 is often: a) mandatory b) excluded by express clause c) only for consumer contracts d) replaced by the Insurance Act
  6. A cedant buys £10m xs £5m. A £12m loss produces a recovery of: a) £7m b) £10m c) £12m d) £5m
  7. An insurer may terminate from the time of a fraudulent claim under: a) CIDRA s.2 b) Insurance Act 2015 s.12 c) CRA 2015 d) FSMA s.166

Answers: 1-b (retain £500k = 1/6; cede 5/6 = 83.3%); 2-b; 3-b; 4-b; 5-b; 6-a; 7-b.

Module 17

CII LM3: London Market Underwriting Principles – Study Notes

Exam: 75 MCQs, 2 hours | Syllabus: published October 2026, examined 1 Jan – 1 Dec 2027 certain Weighting: LO5 distribution (≈24), LO3 business planning and capital (≈18), LO2 policy wording (≈16), LO4 pricing (≈12), LO1 framework (≈5). The CII also publishes an LM3 indicative content 2027 document listing the detailed topics per assessment criterion – use it as your checklist. Confirm detail against the CII LM3 study text.

LO1 – Framework within which business is conducted (≈5 questions)

1.1 The framework

Topic Key notes
Domestic vs global markets Domestic markets serve local, standard risks under local regulation; global hubs (London, Bermuda, Zurich, Singapore, New York E&S) take large, complex, international risks needing large capacity and specialist expertise
Why a client chooses London Capacity, specialist expertise, security and ratings, global licences, broker access, flexibility of wordings, claims reputation; domestic may be cheaper, simpler, local law/language, local claims service, admitted status required by local regulation
Sources of capital Lloyd's: corporate members (insurance groups, investors), Names (individuals, now mostly via limited liability vehicles), third-party capital via SPAs and London Bridge (ILS); company market: shareholders' equity of insurers and reinsurers; alternative capital: ILS funds, cat bonds, sidecars
PRA Authorises, regulates and supervises insurers for safety and soundness; Solvency UK; internal model approval; Fundamental Rules
FCA Authorises and supervises conduct of all insurance firms and intermediaries; Principles for Businesses; ICOBS, PROD, Consumer Duty (retail), SYSC, CASS
SM&CR Senior Managers (approved, Statements of Responsibilities, Duty of Responsibility), Certification Regime, Conduct Rules
UK Corporate Governance Code Applies to premium-listed companies on a "comply or explain" basis: board leadership, division of responsibilities, composition, audit, risk and internal control, remuneration. The 2024 Code added a board declaration on the effectiveness of material internal controls (from 2026 reporting periods) likely
Lloyd's principles for doing business Lloyd's sets principles for managing agents (e.g., in areas such as underwriting, claims, delegated authority, governance, culture, capital, reserving, conduct, operational resilience) and assesses managing agents' maturity against them, scaling oversight to risk likely – check the current list in the study text
Solvency II / Solvency UK Pillar 1 capital (SCR, MCR, technical provisions, own funds); Pillar 2 governance (system of governance, ORSA, key functions: risk, compliance, actuarial, internal audit); Pillar 3 reporting (SFCR public, RSR private, QRTs)

1.2 Duties of the intermediary when creating a contract

  • Types: retail, wholesale/placing, producing, reinsurance brokers, MGAs/coverholders.
  • Regulation: FCA authorisation, ICOBS, SYSC conflicts, CASS 5 client money, professional indemnity cover, SM&CR.
  • Role in underwriting: understand client needs; make the fair presentation on behalf of the client (broker's knowledge can be attributed to the insured); choose suitable, secure insurers; negotiate terms; ensure the wording matches what was agreed; achieve contract certainty; manage conflicts.

LO2 – Role, purpose and implications of policy wording (≈16 questions)

2.1 Identity of the contracting parties

Topic Key notes
Why identity matters Insurable interest; who can claim; who owes the duty of fair presentation; sanctions screening; who receives payment; avoids disputes after mergers and name changes
Joint policy Parties with the same (joint) interest, e.g., joint owners. Historically, wrongdoing by one could affect all
Composite policy One document covering several parties with different interests (e.g., owner, lender, contractors). Treated as separate contracts – one party's non-disclosure or fraud generally does not prejudice the others certain
Access by others Contracts (Rights of Third Parties) Act 1999 (unless excluded); Third Parties (Rights against Insurers) Act 2010 – third party can claim directly against the insurer of an insolvent insured; assignment of policy proceeds; loss payee clauses; noted interests; co-insured endorsements

2.2 Importance of policy wording

  • Contract certainty: agreed terms before inception, documentation promptly after. If not achieved: disputes, delayed claims, litigation, regulatory criticism, reputational damage, uncertainty for reinsurers.
  • Consumer policies need specific protections: CIDRA 2012 (reasonable care not to misrepresent), Consumer Rights Act 2015 (fairness, transparency), FCA ICOBS (pre-contract information such as the Insurance Product Information Document, cancellation rights – usually 14 days for general insurance), Consumer Duty (good outcomes, fair value).

2.3 How policy wordings are constructed

Topic Key notes
Clear drafting Plain, consistent defined terms; clear insuring clause, exclusions, conditions; avoid conflicting clauses
Ambiguity Courts may resolve ambiguity against the party who drafted the term (contra proferentem); more relevant to insurer-drafted standard wordings; less weight where wording was negotiated between sophisticated parties
Who drafted Insurer wording (contra proferentem risk for insurer); broker manuscript wording (drafted for the client – ambiguity may be construed against the insured); market standard clauses (LMA/IUA/Institute clauses – interpreted consistently by courts, widely understood)
Regulation and non-domestic wordings Local law may require specific clauses, language, filed forms (e.g., US admitted business), service of suit or choice of law/jurisdiction clauses; using an English wording abroad may conflict with mandatory local law; consumer rules restrict terms
MRC – advantages Standard structure; supports contract certainty; one document for all insurers; easier processing and data capture; supports electronic placing
MRC – disadvantages Can become long; incorporation by reference to wordings may cause gaps; still often unstructured (PDF); follower conditions add complexity
MRC preparation requirements Complete all sections; avoid "to be advised" items at inception; state the full wording or reference precise clause numbers; record subjectivities and deadlines; state written and signed line basis; tax and regulatory information

2.4 Viewing the complete contract wording

Topic Key notes
Wording libraries Central approved repository of wordings and clauses with version control: consistency, faster drafting, fewer errors, easier comparison and analytics (an AI use case – see Module 09)
Side letter A separate document varying or adding to the main contract (e.g., special premium terms, profit commission, cancellation rights). Risk: other insurers, reinsurers or auditors may not see it → contract uncertainty, disputes, possible misrepresentation. Should be avoided or fully disclosed and incorporated
Rules of interpretation Words given their natural and ordinary meaning in context; read the contract as a whole; commercial common sense where wording is ambiguous; specific overrides general; written/typed overrides printed; ejusdem generis (general words after a list are limited to the same type); noscitur a sociis (a word is known by its context); contra proferentem as a last resort
Influences on wording Wording specialists (expert drafting, consistency); insurer-drafted wordings (protect insurer, may be inflexible); specific client requirements (manuscript wordings tailored to the client, often broker-drafted)

LO3 – Business planning and capital setting (≈18 questions)

3.1 Business planning process

Topic Key notes
What a business plan is Planned premium by class, territory and channel; expected loss ratios; expenses; reinsurance; capital; strategy and risk appetite. Usually annual, with mid-year reviews; Lloyd's syndicates submit a Syndicate Business Forecast (SBF) each year for the next year of account
Advantages Clear direction; aligns underwriting with capital and appetite; basis for monitoring and accountability; supports regulator and Lloyd's approval; supports reinsurance buying
Disadvantages Time and cost; can become out of date quickly (market cycle, catastrophes); may encourage "chasing premium" to hit targets; rigid if not reviewed
Regulatory guidance PRA expects business planning within Solvency UK governance and ORSA; Lloyd's issues annual business planning guidance (e.g., market loss ratio expectations, growth constraints, minimum standards) likely

3.2 Monitoring and reporting on the plan

Topic Key notes
SBF data provided to Lloyd's Premium by class (Lloyd's risk codes) and territory; gross and net loss ratios; expenses and acquisition costs; reinsurance programme; rate change assumptions; new and exiting classes; capital and RDS information likely
Internal reporting Actual vs plan by class; rate change and rate adequacy; loss ratios (attritional/large/cat); exposure vs appetite; reserve development; expenses. Early action when off plan
Why Lloyd's reviews plans Protect the Central Fund and the market's rating; ensure profitability and sustainable growth; challenge unrealistic assumptions; maintain market-wide discipline
Lloyd's actions on issues Request more information; approve with conditions; restrict growth or reduce capacity; require remediation plans; increase capital loading; in serious cases stop writing a class or replace management likely
Required reports Regular returns to Lloyd's – e.g., quarterly monitoring returns, performance management data returns, Lloyd's capital returns, RDS returns, Statement of Actuarial Opinion on reserves, Solvency UK returns likely – learn the names used in the current study text

3.3 How capital setting supports writing business

Topic Key notes
Risk appetite The amount and type of risk the insurer is willing to take: insurance risk (underwriting, reserving, catastrophe), credit risk (reinsurers, brokers, coverholders failing to pay), operational risk (people, process, systems, external events, including cyber and DA failures), plus market and liquidity risk
Effect on capital More or riskier business, larger lines, higher cat exposure, weaker reinsurance, poor controls → higher SCR/ECA → more capital needed. Capital limits how much business can be written
Lloyd's capital uSCR from syndicate model, ECA = uSCR × 135%; members provide Funds at Lloyd's; Central Fund protects the market certain. See Module 07 §A4
Enterprise Risk Management (ERM) Framework to identify, assess, manage, monitor and report all risks across the organisation: risk appetite statements, risk register, three lines of defence (business, risk/compliance, internal audit), ORSA, stress and scenario testing, risk culture

3.4 The technical account and financial statements

Statement What it shows Purpose
Balance sheet Assets, liabilities and equity at a point in time Financial position, solvency
Income statement (profit and loss) Revenue, expenses and profit over a period. For insurers (UK GAAP) split into a technical account (underwriting: earned premiums, claims incurred, operating expenses, allocated investment return) and a non-technical account (other income and costs, tax) certain Performance
Cash flow statement Cash in and out from operating, investing and financing activities Liquidity
Management accounts Internal, more frequent, detailed by class and team Decision-making and monitoring against plan
Why produce accounts Legal requirement; shareholders, members, regulators, rating agencies, Lloyd's Accountability and transparency
Ratio / term Formula / meaning
Assets / liabilities Owned resources / obligations (claims reserves, unearned premiums are main insurer liabilities)
Profit Income minus expenses
Return on capital employed (ROCE) Profit before interest and tax ÷ capital employed × 100
Liquidity Ability to pay short-term obligations; e.g., current ratio = current assets ÷ current liabilities
Gearing Debt ÷ equity (or debt ÷ capital employed); higher gearing = more financial risk
Combined ratio (Claims incurred + expenses) ÷ earned premium. Below 100% = underwriting profit. Example: earned premium £100m, claims £62m, expenses £30m → 92%

LO4 – Pricing of risk (≈12 questions)

4.1 Basic statistics

Concept Meaning / example
Frequency How often losses occur (e.g., 50 claims per 1,000 policies = 5%)
Severity Average size of loss (e.g., £8,000)
Mean Sum ÷ count. Losses 2, 3, 3, 5, 12 (£k) → mean 5
Median Middle value when ordered → 3. Less affected by large outliers
Mode Most frequent value → 3
When to use Mean for pricing totals; median when large losses distort; mode to find the most common outcome
Return period Average time between events of a given size: 1-in-100 years = 1% annual probability; 1-in-200 = 0.5%
Probability over several years P(at least one in n years) = 1 − (1 − p)ⁿ. For a 1-in-100 event over 10 years: 1 − 0.99¹⁰ ≈ 9.6%

4.2 Constructing an insurance rate

Premium terms

Term Meaning
Written premium Premium for contracts incepting in the period (booked)
Signed premium Premium processed/signed through the bureau (Lloyd's term)
Gross premium Before deduction of brokerage (or before reinsurance, depending on context)
Net premium After brokerage/acquisition cost (or after reinsurance)
Earned premium Portion relating to the expired part of the policy period
Unearned premium Portion for the unexpired period – held as unearned premium reserve

Claims terms

Term Meaning
Paid claims Already paid
Outstanding claims Reserves on known claims
Incurred Paid + outstanding
IBNR Incurred but not reported
IBNER Incurred but not enough reported (known claims likely to increase)

Building the rate – step by step

  1. Risk premium = frequency × average severity. Example: 2% × £5,000 = £100 per policy.
  2. Adjust for underwriting features: positive (good risk management, sprinklers, strong claims history) reduce the rate; negative (hazardous occupation, poor housekeeping, high cat exposure) increase it.
  3. Adjust for claims experience of the insured (experience rating) and trends (inflation).
  4. Add loadings: expenses, acquisition costs (brokerage), reinsurance cost, cost of capital, profit margin. - Office premium = risk premium ÷ (1 − loadings %). With 35% loadings: £100 ÷ 0.65 = £153.85.

Burning cost method (used where the insured has a credible loss history, common in reinsurance and large commercial risks)

Year Losses (developed and trended, £k) Exposure – e.g., turnover (£m)
1 400 80
2 250 85
3 600 90
4 350 95
5 400 100
Total 2,000 450

Burning cost = 2,000 ÷ 450 = £4.44k per £1m turnover. Next year turnover £110m → expected losses ≈ £489k. Add loadings to get the premium. Use when history is relevant, credible and adjusted for development, inflation and changes in exposure; avoid when data is thin or the risk has changed.

Claims triangulation – shows how claims for each underwriting/accident year develop over time.

Year 12 months 24 months 36 months
2023 100 150 165
2024 120 180 ?
2025 110 ? ?

Development factors: 12→24 = (150 + 180) ÷ (100 + 120) = 1.50; 24→36 = 165 ÷ 150 = 1.10. Projected 2025 ultimate ≈ 110 × 1.50 × 1.10 = 181.5. Pricing impact: using only reported claims (110) would understate the true cost; the rate must reflect ultimate losses, including IBNR.

4.3 Realistic Disaster Scenarios (RDS) and catastrophe models

Topic Key notes
RDS A defined "what if" event (e.g., a major hurricane hitting a specific city) used to measure a syndicate's gross and net loss from one event across all classes; tests aggregation, reinsurance and capital
Lloyd's RDS framework Lloyd's prescribes compulsory scenarios for all syndicates (examples include US windstorm, US earthquake, European windstorm, Japanese earthquake, terrorism, marine and aviation collision, cyber and liability scenarios), plus specific scenarios for some, and syndicate-defined scenarios for exposures not captured by the standard set likely – learn the current list from the study text
Catastrophe models Software (e.g., Moody's RMS, Verisk) simulating thousands of events: hazard, vulnerability, exposure and financial modules. Outputs: average annual loss (AAL), exceedance probability (EP) curves, PML at return periods (e.g., 1-in-200)
Purpose and value Pricing, accumulation control, reinsurance purchase, capital setting, regulatory and Lloyd's reporting
Deterministic vs probabilistic Deterministic: one specified event and its loss (RDS, stress tests) – easy to understand, no probability attached. Probabilistic: many simulated events with probabilities – used for pricing, capital and EP curves
Limitations Model uncertainty, data quality (geocoding, values), non-modelled perils (e.g., wildfire, flood in some regions), secondary uncertainty; underwriters must apply judgement

LO5 – Methods of writing business and distribution (≈24 questions – highest weight)

5.1 Types of placement

Type Key notes
Subscription market Lead sets terms, followers take shares; MRC; written vs signed lines; several liability; GUA for changes; claims agreement parties. See Module 04 §4
Fronting A licensed insurer (fronting insurer) issues the policy and reinsures most or all of the risk to another insurer/reinsurer (e.g., Lloyd's syndicate, captive). Why: local licensing/admitted requirements, rating requirements, captive programmes, global programmes needing local policies. Advantages: access to markets; compliance with local law; local policy and claims service. Disadvantages: fronting fee cost; credit risk for the fronting insurer if the reinsurer fails; regulatory scrutiny of pure fronting; less control
Layering Large limits split into a primary layer and excess layers (e.g., $25m primary; $50m xs $25m; $100m xs $75m). Advantages: more capacity; insurers choose risk level that suits appetite; better price for each layer. Disadvantages: complexity; risk of gaps or inconsistent wordings ("follow form" issues); multiple parties in claims; drop-down disputes when lower layers are exhausted or insolvent

5.2 Methods of distribution

Method Key notes
Local branch and overseas offices London insurers and Lloyd's platforms abroad (e.g., Lloyd's Asia in Singapore, Lloyd's China, Lloyd's Dubai, Lloyd's Europe) write business close to clients via local underwriters or service companies; local licences, language and claims service
Affinity programmes Insurance sold to members/customers of an organisation (bank, retailer, association, employer) under its brand – typically travel, gadget, pet, warranty, A&H. Often via a coverholder
Master policy One policy issued to an organisation covering many individuals/entities, with certificates to each member (e.g., group travel, contractors' schemes)
Open market, DA, facilities See LO5.3

5.3 Types of delegated underwriting

Type What it is Why used
Binding authority Agreement under which an insurer delegates authority to a coverholder to accept risks and issue documents within agreed limits Access small/high-volume and local business efficiently
Lineslip A facility (usually arranged by a broker) where a lineslip leader (and possibly a second lead) accepts declared risks on behalf of all participating insurers Speed and efficiency for a broker's flow of similar risks while keeping London underwriters in control
Consortium A group of insurers agreeing to write a class together, with a consortium leader underwriting on behalf of all members Access to expertise or distribution; scale; share infrastructure costs

5.4 Stakeholders' roles and responsibilities

Stakeholder Responsibilities
Insurer (managing agent) Due diligence, approval, setting authority, monitoring, audit, claims oversight, final responsibility for the risk and regulatory compliance
Coverholder Underwrite within authority, issue documents, collect premium, report bordereaux, comply with law and BAA, treat customers fairly, handle claims if authorised
Broker placing the binder Places the BAA in London; may handle premium and documentation; manages conflicts if also the coverholder
MGA A type of coverholder/agent with underwriting expertise, often across several carriers; may also be a broker – conflict risk
Consortium members Agree consortium terms; accept leader's decisions for their share; monitor leader; share costs
Lineslip participants Leader agrees declarations; followers bound by leader's decisions within the lineslip; monitor results; broker administers declarations
Conflicts of interest Coverholder acting for both insured (as broker) and insurer (binding); commission based on volume not profit; group-owned service companies; must be identified and managed (SYSC 10)
Policy documentation and contract certainty under DA Normally the coverholder issues policy documents/certificates within authority, using approved wordings; the managing agent remains responsible for ensuring documentation is correct and timely

5.5 Advantages and disadvantages

Method For insurers For coverholders / brokers / members
Binding authority + Access to local, small risks at low fixed cost; + local expertise. − Loss of direct control; − data delay and quality; − conduct and reputational risk; − higher acquisition cost Coverholder: + local control, speed, commission/profit share; − compliance burden, audits, reporting costs. Broker: + income and client service; − conflicts
Lineslip + Efficient access to a broker's flow; + followers save underwriting cost. − Followers rely on leader; − adverse selection risk Broker: + speed, certainty of capacity, client service. Leader: + influence and fees. Followers: − less control
Consortium + Access to expertise/business not otherwise available; + shared costs. − Dependence on leader's skill; − reputational link to leader Leader: + scale, fee income. Members: + diversification; − limited control, concentration risk

5.6 Management and controls

Topic Key notes
Underwriting controls Underwriting authorities, line guides, referral rules, peer review, pricing guidelines, aggregate limits, exclusions lists, business plan limits, audit trail
Conduct risk Risk that firm's behaviour causes poor customer outcomes or harms market integrity (e.g., mis-selling, unfair claims handling)
Product risk Risk that a product does not meet the target market's needs or fair value (PROD, Consumer Duty for retail)
Selection and approval Before joining a lineslip/consortium or granting a BAA: due diligence on leader/coverholder (experience, financials, systems, controls, conduct, sanctions, key staff), business plan fit, terms review, Lloyd's approvals and registration
PRA, FCA and Lloyd's roles PRA: prudential risk of delegated business, outsourcing and operational resilience. FCA: conduct and product governance of the distribution chain; coverholders are FCA-authorised in the UK. Lloyd's: coverholder approval, standards, registration, oversight of managing agents' DA controls
Levels of authority Full binding authority; limited (e.g., certain classes/limits); prior submit (insurer approval before binding); claims authority levels: none (all referred), limited settlement authority, full authority with loss fund; TPA/DCA used when coverholder lacks claims expertise or for independence and volume
Information provided Risk, premium and claims bordereaux; financial statements; complaints data; breaches; audit reports; MI on performance vs plan
Continuous monitoring Bordereaux validation; premium vs GPI limit; loss ratio trends; data quality; aged debt; complaints; breaches; referrals; site visits
Audit Independent check (often by specialist auditors, sometimes coordinated across insurers) of coverholders, DCAs/TPAs: compliance with BAA, underwriting files, premium handling and client money, claims handling, documentation, IT and data security, financial crime and sanctions, complaints and conduct, business continuity; findings tracked to closure

Practice MCQs

  1. A composite policy covering an owner and a lender means: a) fraud by the owner voids cover for the lender automatically b) each party's interest is treated as a separate contract c) only the owner can claim d) the lender must disclose the owner's claims
  2. Under the Third Parties (Rights against Insurers) Act 2010, a third party may: a) cancel the policy b) claim directly against the insurer of an insolvent insured c) change the wording d) avoid the contract
  3. Lloyd's ECA is: a) SCR × 1.0 b) uSCR × 1.35 c) MCR × 1.35 d) premium × 35%
  4. Earned premium £200m, claims incurred £130m, expenses £60m. Combined ratio: a) 65% b) 95% c) 105% d) 30%
  5. A 1-in-250 year event has an annual probability of: a) 0.25% b) 0.4% c) 2.5% d) 4%
  6. Frequency 4%, average severity £2,500, loadings 30%. Office premium: a) £100 b) £130 c) £142.86 d) £70
  7. A deterministic catastrophe approach: a) uses thousands of simulated events b) measures loss from one specified event c) produces an EP curve d) needs no exposure data
  8. Which arrangement lets a lead underwriter accept declared risks for all participants under a broker's facility? a) consortium b) lineslip c) binding authority d) fronting
  9. The main credit risk in fronting falls on: a) the insured b) the fronting insurer c) the broker d) the regulator
  10. A side letter is risky mainly because: a) it is illegal b) other parties may not see it, harming contract certainty c) it must be filed with the FCA d) it overrides English law

Answers: 1-b, 2-b, 3-b, 4-b, 5-b (1/250), 6-c (100 ÷ 0.7), 7-b, 8-b, 9-b, 10-b.
